Optimal Estimation in Orthogonally Invariant Generalized Linear Models: Spectral Initialization and Approximate Message Passing
Yihan Zhang, Hong Chang Ji, Ramji Venkataramanan, Marco Mondelli

Abstract
We consider the problem of parameter estimation from a generalized linear model with a random design matrix that is orthogonally invariant in law. Such a model allows the design have an arbitrary distribution of singular values and only assumes that its singular vectors are generic. It is a vast generalization of the i.i.d. Gaussian design typically considered in the theoretical literature, and is motivated by the fact that real data often have a complex correlation structure so that methods relying on i.i.d. assumptions can be highly suboptimal. Building on the paradigm of spectrally-initialized iterative optimization, this paper proposes optimal spectral estimators and combines them with an approximate message passing (AMP) algorithm, establishing rigorous performance guarantees for these two algorithmic steps. Both the spectral initialization and the subsequent AMP meet existing conjectures on the fundamental limits to estimation—the former on the optimal sample complexity for efficient weak recovery, and the latter on the optimal errors. Numerical experiments suggest the effectiveness of our methods and accuracy of our theory beyond orthogonally invariant data.



1
Introduction

We study the canonical problem of parameter estimation from a generalized linear model (GLM)
[
77
]
. Consider a dataset
{\mathcal{D}}=(X,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
where the design matrix
X=\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}&\cdots&x_{n}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
consists of
n
covariate vectors each of dimension
d
, and
y
is a vector of response variables each given by

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle=q(\left\langle x_{i},\beta_{*}\right\rangle,\varepsilon_{i}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq n.

Here,
q:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is a link function,
\varepsilon_{i}
a noise variable,
\beta_{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
an unknown vector of regression coefficients, and
\left\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\right\rangle
denotes the Euclidean inner product. The task is to estimate
\beta_{*}
from
{\mathcal{D}}
. The nonlinear link function
q
generalizes linear regression and encompasses the most commonly used statistical models in engineering and the natural sciences, including
logistic regression
[
99
]
, phase retrieval
[
92
,
47
]

(
y_{i}=|\left\langle x_{i},\beta_{*}\right\rangle|+\varepsilon_{i}
), and 1-bit compressive sensing
[
12
]

(
y_{i}=\sgn(\left\langle x_{i},\beta_{*}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{i})
).

In the classical regime where the sample size
n
is much larger than the parameter dimension
d
, the regression coefficients in
\beta_{*}
can be consistently estimated and standard inference procedures, such as confidence intervals and likelihood-based tests, provide accurate uncertainty quantification. However, in modern high-dimensional settings where
n
and
d
are both large and comparable (
n/d\to\delta\in(0,\infty)
), these classical results are no longer valid
[
38
,
94
]
. This has led to a flurry of work aimed at establishing both statistical limits and efficient estimators in this high-dimensional regime, largely focusing on the setting where the design matrix
X
is unstructured, i.e., its entries are i.i.d. Gaussian
[
5
,
8
,
38
,
97
,
94
]
.

Estimation in the high-dimensional regime can be broken down into two steps:
(i)
producing a pilot estimate
\widehat{\beta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
that has a nontrivial correlation with
\beta_{*}
, and
(ii)
refining the initialization
\widehat{\beta}
via an iterative procedure.
More precisely, in step
(i)
we aim to produce an estimate with a nonvanishing asymptotic overlap with
\beta_{*}
:

\displaystyle\liminf_{d\to\infty}\frac{|\langle\widehat{\beta},\beta_{*}\rangle|}{\|\widehat{\beta}\|_{2}\|\beta_{*}\|_{2}}>0.

(1.1)

This criterion is often called ‘weak recovery’ in the literature
[
70
,
66
,
61
]
. Spectral methods
[
31
]
provide a simple and popular approach for obtaining a
\widehat{\beta}
that satisfies
Equation

1.1
. A spectral estimator for a GLM is given by the principal eigenvector of the following data-dependent matrix:

D=X^{\top}\diag({\mathcal{T}}(y_{1}),\cdots,{\mathcal{T}}(y_{n}))X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{T}}(y_{i})x_{i}x_{i}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d},

(1.2)

where
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a user-defined preprocessing function. The performance of spectral estimators obtained via the matrix in
Equation

1.2
has been studied in significant detail
[
78
,
18
,
30
,
59
,
70
,
60
]
; specifically, for an i.i.d. Gaussian design and prior, it has been shown in
[
70
]
that a suitable choice of
{\mathcal{T}}
achieves weak recovery up to the information-theoretic limit for a class of link functions
q
, including phase retrieval.
Moving on to step
(ii)
, a variety of iterative procedures have been proposed, ranging from optimizing a suitable loss function (e.g., penalized log-likelihood) via gradient descent or alternating minimization
[
18
,
78
,
104
,
26
]
to Kaczmarz methods
[
102
]

and higher-order methods
[
19
]
.

In the Bayesian setting, where
\beta_{*}
is generated according to a prior distribution independent of
X
, the posterior mean
\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{*}\,|\,X,y\right]
minimizes the mean squared error. Although it is usually computationally infeasible, the asymptotic Bayes risk
\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\beta_{*}-\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{*}\,|\,X,y\right]\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]
has been precisely characterized for i.i.d. Gaussian designs, under suitable model assumptions
[
5
]
. For this setting, it has been shown
[
72
]
that the spectral estimator in
Equation

1.2
can be refined via a Generalized Approximate Message Passing (GAMP) algorithm
[
87
]
. In certain instances, this combined procedure achieves the Bayes risk
[
5
]
, and more broadly, GAMP estimators have been shown to be optimal among general first order methods (GFOMs)
[
22
,
74
]
.

The results above provide a clear picture of how to perform optimal estimation in GLMs when the design matrix
X
has i.i.d. Gaussian entries. Some of these asymptotic results, e.g. those for spectral methods and certain convex penalized estimators, can be extended to the broader setting of Gaussian designs with arbitrary covariance
[
105
,
107
,
21
]
.
However, in many applications the data have a complex structure and the correlations across the entries of
X
(including dependence across rows) are not adequately captured by a Gaussian design.
As a consequence, methods relying on Gaussian assumptions on the design may be highly suboptimal.
This is highlighted in
Figure

1
: the algorithms that are provably optimal when
X
has i.i.d. Gaussian entries
[
5
,
72
]
fall short of the performance promised by the theory when
X
is taken from computational genomics
[
58
]
datasets.

Figure 1
:
Performance of
5
algorithms on phase retrieval whose design matrix is given by one of two real datasets from GTEx, see
Section

6
for details.
The GAMP algorithm
[
72
]
(in yellow) with provable optimality on Gaussian data is no longer as effective.
The proposed Bayes-GVAMP (‘AMP’ in red) with spectral initialization (‘spec’ in black) shows dominant performance which is well predicted by our theory under the orthogonal invariance assumption.
Gradient descent (‘GD’ in blue) and GAMP are both initialized with the spectral estimator from
[
60
, Equation (20)]
(‘spec conj’ in green).
They achieve the same weak recovery threshold as spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP but worse overlaps.

A common way to capture the structure in the data and retain mathematical tractability is to model the design matrix
X
as random with an
orthogonally invariant
law. This corresponds to the singular vectors of
X
being generic (or, more formally, Haar distributed over the orthogonal group) while its spectrum can be arbitrary, which allows for row-column dependencies as well as a range of other structures in the design.
The universality of this design choice has been both observed empirically

[
82
,
14
,
61
]
and proved formally
[
40
,
42
,
29
,
43
,
101
]
for a broad class of well-known ensembles, including those obtained from discrete cosine/sine transforms and Hadamard-Walsh transforms; see
[
42
, Section 1.3]
.

Due to their flexibility, orthogonally invariant designs have been recently investigated in the statistical literature, e.g., in the context of both linear regression
[
57
]

and principal component analysis
[
46
,
4
,
41
]
.
For estimation in a GLM with orthogonally invariant design, variants of AMP algorithms have been proposed
[
90
,
VKM
]
, and their fixed points have been shown to match the Bayes risk conjectured using tools from statistical physics
[
52
,
95
]
. However, these AMP algorithms require an initialization that satisfies
Equation

1.1
and is independent of the design matrix. Assuming the availability of such an initialization is unrealistic for several popular choices of nonlinear link functions
q
(e.g., phase retrieval). Spectral estimators are a natural choice for initialization, but existing results are either restrictive in terms of the spectrum of the design matrix (unit singular values in
[
39
]
, correlated Gaussian design in
[
105
]
) or they remain at a conjectural level
[
65
]
. In short, obtaining rigorous performance guarantees on initialization methods has proved hard, let alone the combination of the initialization with iterative methods, such as AMP.

Contributions.

In this paper, we address several open questions on optimal estimation in orthogonally invariant GLMs: we provide optimal spectral estimators, show how they can be combined with an AMP algorithm, and establish rigorous asymptotic guarantees for both. Both the initialization and the iterative refinement via AMP meet existing conjectures on fundamental limits to estimation—the former on the optimal sample complexity for efficient weak recovery
[
66
]
, and the latter on the optimal errors
[
52
,
95
]
.

Figure 2
:
Performance of
5
algorithms on phase retrieval whose design matrix is given by either binary or ternary coded diffraction patterns (CDP), see
Section

6
for details. The regression coefficients are obtained by applying standard preprocessing to a
32\times 32
image of a truck from the CIFAR-10 dataset.
Our theoretical guarantees (‘thy’, solid curves) remain accurate when neither the design matrix is orthogonally invariant nor the prior is i.i.d., a strong sign of universality.

Our contributions are summarized as follows.

1.

We establish the asymptotic overlap of spectral estimators for a class of preprocessing functions (
Theorem

4.1
). More precisely, we unveil a criticality condition
Equation

4.13
under which
the largest eigenvalue of
D
in
Equation

1.2
escapes the bulk of the spectrum and the spectral estimator given by the top eigenvector of
D
achieves weak recovery in the sense of
Equation

1.1
, a phenomenon similar to the celebrated BBP phase transition
[
3
]
.

2.

Leveraging the characterization of
Theorem

4.1
, we identify a preprocessing function that achieves weak recovery with the smallest possible sample complexity (
Theorem

4.2
). Notably, the critical sample complexity above which spectral estimators achieve nonvanishing asymptotic overlap coincides with the one conjectured in
[
66
]
to be optimal among all efficient estimators.

3.

We design a general class of AMP iterations which, following the terminology of
[
13
]
, we call Generalized Vector AMP (GVAMP). GVAMP iteratively refines the spectral initialization by applying nonlinearities both to the singular values of the design matrix and to the entries of the iterates. We prove that the dynamics (and, hence, the performance) of this class of algorithms can be tracked via a state evolution recursion, a succinct set of low-dimensional deterministic equations (
Theorem

4.3
). This resolves the issue, common in related work, of requiring an initialization that achieves weak recovery, while being independent of the design matrix.

4.

Finally, via a suitable specialization of the generic GVAMP from
Theorem

4.3
, we obtain an algorithm—spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP—whose fixed points match the Bayes risk (
Theorem

4.6
), as conjectured in
[
52
,
95
]
. This offers an end-to-end algorithmic solution to the problem of generalized linear regression, with a rigorous performance characterization and conjectured optimality among a large class of efficient algorithms.

Due to the aforementioned universality of orthogonally invariant designs, we expect our findings to hold for design matrices common in applications. This is showcased in
Figures

1
and
2
: the empirical results match our theoretical predictions even when
X
is not generated from an orthogonally invariant law, but taken from computational imaging
[
17
]
or quantitative genomics
[
58
]
datasets; our proposed algorithms consistently outperform the approaches stemming from i.i.d. assumptions on the design.

2
Related work

Spectral methods.

A line of work has shown the effectiveness and, in some cases, optimality of spectral methods for weak recovery in generalized linear models with i.i.d. Gaussian designs
[
59
,
70
,
60
,
40
]
, correlated Gaussian designs
[
105
]
and partial Haar designs
[
61
,
39
]
.
Moreover, for i.i.d. Gaussian designs, spectral methods have been extended to mixed regression
[
106
]
, multi-index models
[
54
,
35
]
and higher-order tensors
[
33
,
34
]
. The
optimal combination of linear and spectral estimators is obtained in
[
71
]
.
The present paper further advances this body of literature by considering orthogonally invariant designs: we propose and analyze spectral estimators that are effective down to the conjectured computationally optimal weak recovery threshold
\delta_{*}

[
66
]
, i.e., the smallest
\delta
above which weak recovery is achievable by efficient algorithms.
The conjectured computational optimality of spectral methods is underpinned by a well-known heuristic connection to linearized AMP
[
65
]
.
Specifically, considerations based on statistical physics postulate that Bayes-GVAMP is optimal among all polynomial-time algorithms.
Spectral estimators, when viewed as power iteration, arise as the linearization of Bayes-GVAMP around its trivial fixed point.
Then,
\delta_{*}
can be heuristically determined as the smallest
\delta
above which the trivial fixed point is repulsive.
Our results regarding spectral estimators build upon and make rigorous this connection.

Approximate Message Passing.

Our generic formalism of GVAMP takes inspiration from a number of other iterative algorithms for Bayesian linear or generalized linear models including
Adaptive Thouless–Anderson–Palmer (AdaTAP)
[
79
,
80
]
,
Expectation Propagation (EP)
[
69
,
91
,
61
,
63
]
,
Expectation-Consistent approximation (EC)
[
81
,
53
]
, S-AMP
[
15
,
16
]
, RI-GAMP
[
VKM
]
,
Orthogonal AMP
[
62
,
40
,
42
,
43
,
41
]
,
Vector AMP
[
86
,
90
,
96
]
.
It is argued in
[
64
]
via a non-rigorous Plefka expansion approach that the derivations of AdaTAP, EC and VAMP are equivalent.
All the above algorithms operate on certain specific resolvent-type matrices of
X
(e.g., the linear MMSE estimate) and apply the MMSE denoisers to the iterates.
This feature is fundamentally due to the ways in which those algorithms are derived.
Our generic formalism of GVAMP does not have MMSE estimates baked in and allows for arbitrary trace-free spectral transformations of
X
and divergence-free entry-wise nonlinearities of the iterates.
This level of generality is expected to be useful beyond the Bayes-optimal setting.
VAMP-type algorithms with MMSE denoisers are also studied for more sophisticated models than GLMs
[
84
,
83
,
103
]
.
It is expected that our generic GVAMP formalism and the associated state evolution result can be extended to those settings.
AMPs of the style of
[
7
,
11
]
developed for an i.i.d. design find applications to diverse problems as both an algorithmic primitive and a proof technique
[
75
,
73
,
20
,
25
,
51
]
. The
GVAMP proposed in this paper, when specialized to an i.i.d. Gaussian design, differs from the AMP of
[
7
,
11
]
: it is empirically observed that GVAMP has the same fixed points as AMP (left panel of
Figure

8
) but converges faster.

Bayes risk.

Under an i.i.d. Gaussian design, the Bayes risk of generalized linear models is established in
[
5
]
by the adaptive interpolation method
[
6
]
.
To our knowledge, this method has not been successfully applied to orthogonally invariant designs.
However, the Bayes risk
is recently proved in
[
56
]
for linear models with orthogonally invariant design under an additional technical condition that restricts the support of the limiting spectral distribution of
X
to be sufficiently narrow.
The conjectured Bayes risk of generalized linear models with orthogonally invariant design in
[
52
,
95
,
66
]
has not been rigorously justified in its full generality.

[
88
,
50
]
extend such conjectures regarding Bayes risk to more complex architectures such as multilayer networks with orthogonally invariant weight matrices.

3
Preliminaries

3.1
Notation and definitions

All vectors are column vectors.
For a symmetric matrix
M\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
, we denote by
\lambda_{1}(M)\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{d}(M)
its (real) eigenvalues in non-increasing order and by
v_{1}(M),\cdots,v_{d}(M)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
the corresponding eigenvectors (of unit
\ell_{2}
norm).
For a matrix
V=\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}&\cdots&v_{k}\end{bmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times k}
, we use
\Pi_{V}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
to denote the orthogonal projection onto
\spn\{(v_{i})_{i\in[k]}\}
, and let
\Pi_{V^{\perp}}=I_{n}-\Pi_{V}
.

All
\lim
,
\liminf
,
\limsup
hold almost surely, unless otherwise specified.
We use
\delta_{x}(\cdot)=\delta(\cdot-x)
to denote the point mass at
x\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
The orthogonal group in dimension
n
is defined as

{\mathbb{O}}(n)\coloneqq\left\{O\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}:OO^{\top}=O^{\top}O=I_{n}\right\}.

The unique bi-invariant Haar measure on it (normalized to be a probability measure) is denoted by
\haar({\mathbb{O}}(n))
.
The empirical spectral distribution of a matrix
M
is defined as the empirical distribution of its eigenvalues if
M\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
is symmetric:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\delta_{\lambda_{i}(M)},

or of its singular values if
M\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
is rectangular:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n\wedge d}\sum_{i=1}^{n\wedge d}\delta_{\sigma_{i}(M)}.

Generically, we use
p_{\mathsf{A}},p_{{\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{B}}},p_{\left.{\mathsf{A}}\,|\,{\mathsf{B}}\right.}
to denote the marginal, joint and conditional densities of random variables
{\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{B}}
.

Definition 3.1

(Pseudo-Lipschitz functions)
.

For
p\geq 1
, a function
f\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is called a pseudo-Lipschitz function of order
p
if there exists
C>0
such that for all
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
,

\displaystyle\left|f(x)-f(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|x-y\right\|_{2}\left(1+\left\|x\right\|_{2}^{p-1}+\left\|y\right\|_{2}^{p-1}\right).

Denote by
\mathrm{PL}^{p}_{k\to 1}
the set of all such functions and
\mathrm{PL}_{k\to 1}=\bigcup_{p\geq 1}\mathrm{PL}^{p}_{k\to 1}
.

Definition 3.2

(Polynomial growth)
.

For
p\geq 1
, a function
f\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell}
is said to have polynomial growth of order
p
if there exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|f(x)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|x\right\|_{2}^{p}\right),

(3.1)

for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
.
Denote by
\mathrm{PG}^{p}_{k\to\ell}
the set of all such functions and
\mathrm{PG}_{k\to\ell}=\bigcup_{p\geq 1}\mathrm{PG}^{p}_{k\to\ell}
.

Note that any pseudo-Lipschitz function has polynomial growth, i.e.,
\mathrm{PL}^{p}_{k\to 1}\subset\mathrm{PG}^{p}_{k\to 1}
.

Definition 3.3

(Wasserstein convergence,
[
100
, Chapter 6]
)
.

For
p\geq 1
, we say that the empirical distribution of the rows of a (possibly random) matrix
\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}&\cdots&v_{k}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}v_{i,j}\end{bmatrix}_{(i,j)\in[n]\times[p]}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times k}
converges in Wasserstein-
p
to a random vector
\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{V}}_{k}\end{bmatrix}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1\times k}
with finite
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{V}}_{k}\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}^{p}\right]
, denoted by

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}&\cdots&v_{k}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{p}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{V}}_{k}\end{bmatrix},

(3.2)

if for any
f\in\mathrm{PL}^{p}_{k\to 1}
,
it holds almost surely that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(v_{i,1},\cdots,v_{i,k})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[f({\mathsf{V}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathsf{V}}_{k})\right].

(3.3)

We write
\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}&\cdots&v_{k}\end{bmatrix}\xrightarrow{W}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{V}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{V}}_{k}\end{bmatrix}
to mean
W_{p}
convergence for every fixed
p\geq 1
, where the right-hand side has finite mixed moments of all orders.

To verify
Equation

3.2
, it suffices to verify
Equation

3.3
for either (a) any
f\in\mathrm{PG}^{p}_{k\to 1}
continuous, or (b)
f(v_{1},\cdots,v_{k})=\left\|\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}&\cdots&v_{k}\end{bmatrix}\right\|_{2}^{p}
and any bounded Lipschitz
f
;
see
[
46
, Appendix E]
.

3.2
Model

Let
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
be a design matrix,
\beta_{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
a vector of regression coefficients,and
\varepsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
a vector of noise variables.
Consider a generalized linear model

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=q(X\beta_{*},\varepsilon).

(3.4)

In the above display, the function
q\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is applied component-wise to its vector inputs, i.e.,
q(z,\varepsilon)=\begin{bmatrix}q(z_{1},\varepsilon_{1})&\cdots&q(z_{n},\varepsilon_{n})\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
, where
z\coloneqq X\beta_{*}
.
Equivalently,

\displaystyle\left.y\,|\,X\beta_{*}\right.

\displaystyle\sim Q(\cdot\,|\,X\beta_{*}),

(3.5)

for a conditional density
Q\left(\cdot\,|\,\cdot\right)
applied component-wise, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}\left(y\,|\,z\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}Q\left(y_{i}\,|\,z_{i}\right)
.

We assume that
\beta_{*},X,\varepsilon
are mutually independent each subject to the following distributional assumption.

(A1)

\beta_{*}\sim P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}^{\otimes d}
where
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}
is a distribution on
{\mathbb{R}}
with mean
0
and variance
\rho\in(0,\infty)
.

(A2)

X
is bi-orthogonally invariant in law, meaning
X\overset{d}{=}\widetilde{O}X\widetilde{Q}^{\top}
for any deterministic
\widetilde{O}\in{\mathbb{O}}(n),\widetilde{Q}\in{\mathbb{O}}(d)
.
Equivalently,
X
is identified with its singular value decomposition
X=O\Lambda Q^{\top}
where
(O,Q)\sim\haar({\mathbb{O}}(n))\otimes\haar({\mathbb{O}}(d))
is independent of the rectangular diagonal matrix
\Lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
.
Letting
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\wedge d}
be the vector containing the diagonal elements of
\Lambda
, we further assume that almost surely, the empirical distribution of
\lambda
converges in Wasserstein (of every order) to the law
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
of a random variable
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
that is compactly supported on
(0,\infty)
and has positive variance.
Moreover,
\max_{1\leq i\leq n\wedge d}\lambda_{i},\max_{1\leq i\leq n\wedge d}\lambda_{i}^{-1}
are uniformly bounded over
n,d
.
Almost surely, for every
\varsigma>0
, there exists
N(\varsigma)
such that for every
n\wedge d\geq N(\varsigma)
,

\displaystyle\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}_{1\leq i\leq n\wedge d}\subset\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}+(-\varsigma,\varsigma).

(3.6)

The following two limits hold

\displaystyle\lim_{z\nearrow\inf\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}-z}\right]=\lim_{z\searrow\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}{z-{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}\right]=\infty.

(3.7)

(A3)

\varepsilon\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
satisfies
\varepsilon\xrightarrow{W}{\mathsf{E}}
for a real-valued random variable
{\mathsf{E}}
with finite variance.
Denote by
P_{{\mathsf{E}}}
the law of
{\mathsf{E}}
.

(A4)

n,d\to\infty
with
n/d\to\delta\in(0,\infty)
. All limits, unless otherwise specified, are taken with respect to this scaling.

We make a few comments on
Item

(A2)
.
The condition
Equation

3.6
therein implies that the empirical spectral distribution of
X
converges
strongly
to
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
in the sense that the top and bottom eigenvalues of
X
converge respectively to the upper and lower edges of the support of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle 0<\lim_{n\wedge d\to\infty}\min_{1\leq i\leq n\wedge d}\lambda_{i}=\inf\supp{\mathsf{\Lambda}}<\sup\supp{\mathsf{\Lambda}}=\lim_{n\wedge d\to\infty}\max_{1\leq i\leq n\wedge d}\lambda_{i}<\infty.

This in particular excludes outlying singular values to the left or right of the spectrum of
X
.
Otherwise, such outliers may result in outliers in the spectrum of
D
constructed as
Equation

1.2
for spectral estimators
[
10
, Theorem 2.5]
, causing confusion with the informative outlier arising from
\beta_{*}
.

The limits in
Equation

3.7
require
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
to have hard edges on the leftmost and rightmost boundaries of its support.
Similar assumptions are common in the literature of high-dimensional statistics; see e.g.
[
37
, Assumption 6 ‘Thickness of the bulk edge’]
and
[
105
, (A6), (A7)]
.
We believe that this is a purely technical assumption and can be removed via a perturbation argument
[
105
, Appendix C]
.
Indeed, simulations in
Section

6
numerically validate our theoretical results for
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
proportional to
\Beta(3,1)
and
\Beta(1,2)
for which the first and second limit in
Equation

3.7
is finite, respectively.
Similarly, since
\inf\supp\Beta(3,1)=\inf\supp\Beta(1,2)=0
, the assumption that the left edge of the support of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
is bounded away from
0
is technical and may be removed.
On the other hand, the bounded support assumption on
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
is likely needed, otherwise the spectrum of
D
in
Equation

1.2
will be unbounded
[
9
, Theorem 5.9]
.

The bi-orthogonal invariance assumption on the law of
X
captures situations where the left and right singular vectors of
X
are generic and independent of each other and of the singular values.
This unlocks analytic tractability of the model under consideration.
However, numerical experiments in
Section

6.2
suggest that our theoretical predictions remain accurate for certain design ensembles that are not orthogonally invariant or that are even deterministic.
Identifying suitable universality classes of
X
and justifying within them the validity of our theory is left for future work.
Progress in this direction includes
[
42
,
43
,
101
]
, although they are not directly applicable to our proposed algorithms.

Assuming that
q,P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}},\law({\mathsf{\Lambda}}),P_{{\mathsf{E}}}
are known (see
Section

7
for an additional discussion on this requirement), our interest is in constructing estimators of
\beta_{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
from the data
(X,y)
, and
the quality of an estimator
\widehat{\beta}\equiv\widehat{\beta}(X,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is measured by its asymptotic overlap with
\beta_{*}
defined in
Equation

1.1
.

We introduce some notation used frequently in the rest of the paper.
Given the random variable
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
in
Item

(A2)
, we also define
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n},{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}
by

\displaystyle\law({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\law({\mathsf{\Lambda}}),&\textnormal{if }\delta\leq 1\\
\delta^{-1}\law({\mathsf{\Lambda}})+(1-\delta^{-1})\delta_{0},&\textnormal{if }\delta>1\end{cases},

\displaystyle\law({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\delta\law({\mathsf{\Lambda}})+(1-\delta)\delta_{0},&\textnormal{if }\delta\leq 1\\
\law({\mathsf{\Lambda}}),&\textnormal{if }\delta>1\end{cases}.

(3.8)

By definition,
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n},{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}
are distributed according to the limiting spectral distribution of
X
, accounting for
n,d
singular values, respectively.
Also, the limiting spectral distributions of
XX^{\top},X^{\top}X
are given respectively by the laws of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2},{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2}
.
Let
(\overline{\kappa}_{2k})_{k\geq 0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
and
(\overline{m}_{2k})_{k\geq 0}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
be the rectangular free cumulants and moments, respectively, of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}
; see
Appendix

A
for the relevant background.
Let

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\overline{\kappa}_{2}\rho,

(3.9)

where
\rho
is the variance of
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}
from
Item

(A1)
.
Note that
\sigma^{2}>0
since
Item

(A2)
implies
\overline{\kappa}_{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}\right]>0
.
Let

\displaystyle({\mathsf{B}}_{*},{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})

\displaystyle\sim P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2})\otimes P_{{\mathsf{E}}},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Y}}

\displaystyle=q({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}}).

(3.10)

We also define
\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}\coloneqq\sigma^{-1}{\mathsf{Z}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
It is easy to verify that
\beta_{*}\xrightarrow{W}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}
,
\begin{bmatrix}z&\varepsilon&y\end{bmatrix}\xrightarrow{W}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{Z}}&{\mathsf{E}}&{\mathsf{Y}}\end{bmatrix}
.

4
Main results

This section presents our main formal results.

Section

4.1
contains a precise characterization of the spectral properties of the matrix
D
in
Equation

1.2
(in particular the overlap (
1.1
) of its principal eigenvector) and the optimal choice of preprocessing function.

Section

4.2
introduces our generic GVAMP formalism and characterizes its dynamics by a state evolution recursion when initialized with spectral estimators from
Section

4.1
.

Section

4.3
describes GVAMP with the optimal denoisers, leading to the central algorithmic deliverable of this paper—the spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP—with exact asymptotic guarantees.

Section

4.4
provides evidence on its computational optimality by connecting its performance to the conjectured Bayes risk.

4.1
Optimal spectral estimators

Fix any preprocessing function
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\coloneqq\diag({\mathcal{T}}(y)),

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\coloneqq X^{\top}TX,

(4.1a)

where
{\mathcal{T}}
acts component-wise on
y
.
The spectral estimator outputs

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{s}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq v_{1}(D).

(4.1b)

We will consider preprocessing functions
{\mathcal{T}}
subject to the following condition.

(A5)

The preprocessing function
{\mathcal{T}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
takes the form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{g}(y)}{\overline{g}(y)+\gamma}\,,

\displaystyle\textnormal{where }\gamma

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right],

(4.2)

for some function
\overline{g}
bounded from above and satisfying

\displaystyle\inf_{y\in\supp{\mathsf{Y}}}\overline{g}(y)

\displaystyle>-\gamma,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle>0.

(4.3)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\lim_{z\searrow\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{T}}}{z-{\mathsf{T}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\infty,

(4.4)

where
{\mathsf{T}}={\mathcal{T}}({\mathsf{Y}})
.

The first inequality in
Equation

4.3
ensures that
{\mathcal{T}}(y)
in
Equation

4.2
is well-defined for any
y\in\supp{\mathsf{Y}}
in that the denominator
\overline{g}(y)+\gamma
is positive.
Note that the positivity assumption on
\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]
implies
\gamma>0
.
This is because by the moment-cumulant relation (see
Appendix

A
),

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{m}_{4}-\overline{m}_{2}^{2}}{\overline{m}_{2}^{2}}>0,

(4.5)

where the inequality follows by recalling the definition of
\overline{m}_{2k}
and applying Cauchy–Schwarz
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}\right]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}\right]
.
The positivity of
\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]
is without loss of generality.
If
\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]<0
, one can instead consider
-\overline{g}(y)
.
Then, under the mapping
\overline{g}(y)\mapsto-\overline{g}(y)
,
\gamma\mapsto-\gamma
and the function
{\mathcal{T}}
remains unchanged.
We exclude the case where
\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]=0
since that induces the trivial function
{\mathcal{T}}(y)=1
for every
y
.
The condition
Equation

4.4
on the upper edge of
\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}
is akin to
Equation

3.7
imposed on
\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
and can potentially be removed.

Item

(A5)
is mild in the sense that there exists a preprocessing function subject to it that attains the conjecturally optimal weak recovery threshold; see
Theorem

4.2
.
The function class defined by
Item

(A5)
is largely motivated by our proof technique based on linearized GVAMP; see
Section

5
.
Though higher asymptotic overlap can be achieved by considering a larger function class (see
Sections

6
and
7
), those preprocessing functions do not lead to a weak recovery threshold lower than that in
Theorem

4.2
and their advantage in overlap is superseded by subsequent refinement of Bayes-GVAMP (
Theorem

4.4
).
On the other hand, spectral estimators with preprocessing functions satisfying
Item

(A5)
are more convenient to refinement by GVAMP since they arise from linearization of GVAMP.

Theorem

4.1
below characterizes the limit of the top two eigenvalues of
D
and the asymptotic overlap of the spectral estimator, provided a criticality condition.
To state these characterizations, we need a few definitions.
Recall
{\mathsf{T}}={\mathcal{T}}({\mathsf{Y}})
and write
{\mathsf{S}}={\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}
for brevity.
Define the function
\psi\colon(\sup\supp{\mathsf{T}},\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}
as

\displaystyle\psi(a)

\displaystyle=a\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{S}}}{\omega(a)-{\mathsf{S}}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\omega(a)-{\mathsf{S}}}\right]^{-1}=\omega(a)\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{T}}}{a-{\mathsf{T}}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{a-{\mathsf{T}}}\right]^{-1},

(4.6)

where
\omega(a)
is implicitly defined as the unique solution
\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus(\inf\supp{\mathsf{\Lambda}}^{2},\sup\supp{\mathsf{\Lambda}}^{2})
to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{T}}}{a-{\mathsf{T}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{S}}}{\omega-{\mathsf{S}}}\right],

(4.7)

or equivalently (by adding
1
to both sides of
Equation

4.7
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{a}{a-{\mathsf{T}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\omega}{\omega-{\mathsf{S}}}\right].

(4.8)

Define

\displaystyle\lambda^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\psi(a^{\circ}),

(4.9)

where
a^{\circ}\in(\sup\supp{\mathsf{T}},\infty)
is the largest critical point of
\psi
, i.e., the largest solution in
(\sup\supp{\mathsf{T}},\infty)
to
\psi^{\prime}(a^{\circ})=0
, or more explicitly,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{T}}}{a^{\circ}-{\mathsf{T}}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{a^{\circ}-{\mathsf{T}}}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{T}}}{(a^{\circ}-{\mathsf{T}})^{2}}\right]}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{S}}}{\omega(a^{\circ})-{\mathsf{S}}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\omega(a^{\circ})-{\mathsf{S}}}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{S}}}{(\omega(a^{\circ})-{\mathsf{S}})^{2}}\right]}

\displaystyle=1.

(4.10)

Finally, let

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\coloneqq\delta\frac{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}-\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]}{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{3}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}-2\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{4}}-\delta\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]}.

(4.11)

Theorem 4.1

(Spectral estimator)
.

Consider the spectral estimator defined through
Equation

4.1
using a preprocessing function subject to
Item

(A5)
.
Then we have almost surely,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(D)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{\circ},

(4.12)

where
\lambda^{\circ}
is defined in
Equation

4.9
.
Furthermore, if

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle>\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right],

(4.13)

then we have the following:

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D)

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}>\lambda^{\circ},

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left|\left\langle\beta_{*},v_{1}(D)\right\rangle\right|^{2}}{\left\|\beta_{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\eta\in(0,1),

(4.14)

where
\eta
is defined in
Equation

4.11
.

Theorem

4.1
follows from a sequence of results in
Appendix

B
whose proofs span
Appendices

C
,
D
and
E
.
See
Sections

5.1
and
5.2
for a detailed overview of the proof.

Remark 4.1

(Universality)
.

The value of the asymptotic overlap
\eta
in
Equation

4.11
enjoys a surprising universality property with respect to the limiting spectral distribution of
X
.
Specifically, the only dependence on
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}
in the expression
Equation

4.11
is through
\overline{\kappa}_{2},\overline{\kappa}_{4},\overline{\kappa}_{6}
(or equivalently by the moment-cumulant relation, the first three moments of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}
).
This indicates that as far as spectral estimators subject to
Item

(A5)
are concerned, the details of the spectral law of the design have minimal impact on the performance of the estimators.

The following result characterizes, among the family of preprocessing functions given by
Item

(A5)
, the optimal spectral threshold for weak recovery, which is the minimum
\delta
for which there exists a preprocessing function
{\mathcal{T}}
identified with
\overline{g}
that satisfies
Equation

4.13
.

Theorem 4.2

(Optimal spectral threshold and preprocessing)
.

1.

Assume that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\int\,\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[Q(y\,|\,{\mathsf{Z}})\left(\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}-1\right)\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[Q(y\,|\,{\mathsf{Z}})\right]}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle>1.

(4.15)

Then, by taking

\displaystyle\overline{g}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}-1\right)Q(y\,|\,{\mathsf{Z}})\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[Q(y\,|\,{\mathsf{Z}})\right]}=\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}-1\,|\,{\mathsf{Y}}=y\right],

(4.16)

the corresponding preprocessing function defined through
Item

(A5)
achieves weak recovery, in the sense that
Equation

4.13
holds.
Furthermore,

\overline{g}
in
Equation

4.16
satisfies
Equation

4.3

provided:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\,|\,{\mathsf{Y}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}-1\,|\,{\mathsf{Y}}\right]^{2}\right]>0.

(4.17)

2.

Conversely, if there is a preprocessing function subject to
Item

(A5)
achieving weak recovery (in the sense that
Equation

4.13
holds), then
Equation

4.15
must also hold.

Theorem

4.2
is proved in
Appendix

F
.

Item

2
follows from a Cauchy–Schwarz argument applied to the criticality condition
Equation

4.13
.
The optimal choice
Equation

4.16
of
\overline{g}
in
Item

1
can be read off from the equality case of the Cauchy–Schwarz argument.

Remark 4.2

(Conjectured computationally optimal weak recovery threshold)
.

Using the moment-cumulant relation (see
Appendix

A
)

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta

\displaystyle=\delta\frac{\overline{m}_{4}^{\prime}}{\overline{m}_{2}^{\prime 2}}-1,

we can write the condition
Equation

4.15
equivalently as

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle>\frac{\overline{m}_{2}^{\prime 2}}{\overline{m}_{4}^{\prime}}\left[1+\left(\int\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}-1\right)Q(y\,|\,{\mathsf{Z}})\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[Q(y\,|\,{\mathsf{Z}})\right]}\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{-1}\right].

(4.18)

The above condition coincides with
[
66
, (11)]
which is conjectured to be the computationally optimal weak recovery threshold, i.e., no other polynomial-time algorithm is believed to achieve positive asymptotic overlap if
Equation

4.18
is violated.
We observe that the threshold given by
Equation

4.18
(or equivalently
Equation

4.15
) exhibits remarkable universality with respect to the limiting spectral distribution of
X
in that it only depends on the law of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2}
through its first two moments (or equivalently its first two free cumulants).
This implies that the threshold
Equation

4.18
remains the same for all designs with matching first two moments of their limiting spectral distributions.

Remark 4.3

(Effect of non-Gaussian design on spectral threshold)
.

Recall from
[
46
, Remark 5.1]
that when
X_{i,j}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/d)
,
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}
is distributed according to the Marchenko–Pastur law whose rectangular free cumulants are
\overline{\kappa}_{2}=1
and
\overline{\kappa}_{2k}=0
for
k\geq 2
.
Using this in
Equation

4.15
recovers the optimal spectral threshold identified in
[
70
, Equation (20)]
for i.i.d. Gaussian design.
We therefore see clearly that the effect of non-Gaussian design on the optimal spectral threshold is through the cumulant ratio
\overline{\kappa}_{4}/\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}
on the left of
Equation

4.15
.

4.2
Spectrally initialized GVAMP and its state evolution

This section introduces spectrally initialized GVAMP and characterizes its limiting dynamics using a deterministic state evolution recursion.
A generic GVAMP iteration updates its iterates according to the following rules: for every
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle r^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{t}(X^{\top}X)\widetilde{r}^{t}+\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}(X)^{\top}\widetilde{p}^{t},

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=f_{t+1}(r^{t+1};\beta_{*},\Theta),

(4.19a)

\displaystyle p^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{t}(XX^{\top})\widetilde{p}^{t}+\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(X)\widetilde{r}^{t},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=g_{t+1}(p^{t+1};z,\varepsilon,\Xi),

(4.19b)

with initialization
\widetilde{r}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\widetilde{p}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
The matrices
\Theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d^{\prime}},\Xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n^{\prime}}
are side information subject to
Item

(A6)
.

(A6)

The matrices
\Theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d^{\prime}},\Xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n^{\prime}}
are independent of each other and of
O,Q
from
Item

(A2)
.
They satisfy the following Wasserstein limits:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{*}&\Theta\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}&{\mathsf{\Theta}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}z&\varepsilon&\Xi\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{Z}}&{\mathsf{E}}&{\mathsf{\Xi}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix},

as
d,n\to\infty
and
d^{\prime},n^{\prime}
are fixed.

The functions
\Phi_{t},\Psi_{t}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
apply to each eigenvalue of their symmetric input matrices separately, retaining the eigenvectors;
and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t},\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
apply to each singular value of their rectangular input matrices separately, retaining the singular vectors.
For instance, for a symmetric matrix
A
,
\Phi_{t}(A)
is understood as
\Phi_{t}(A)=\sum_{i}\Phi_{t}(\lambda_{i}(A))v_{i}(A)v_{i}(A)^{\top}
.
The functions
f_{t+1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2+d^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{R}},g_{t+1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{3+n^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are applied row-wise to their inputs, e.g.,
f_{t+1}(r^{t+1};\beta_{*},\Theta)=\begin{bmatrix}f_{t+1}(r^{t+1}_{1};\beta_{*,1},\Theta_{1,:})&\cdots&f_{t+1}(r^{t+1}_{d};\beta_{*,d},\Theta_{d,:})\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
.
See
Section

5.3
for motivation behind the formalism of
Equation

4.19
.

The initializers
\widetilde{r}^{0},\widetilde{p}^{0}
are obtained from the family of spectral estimators described in
Section

4.1
.
Specifically, consider a matrix
D
as in
Equation

4.1a
constructed from a preprocessing function of the form
Equation

4.2
identified with some
\overline{g}
.
In particular, let
\gamma
be defined through
Equation

4.2
using this
\overline{g}
.
We will derive a state evolution result for the GVAMP iteration
Equation

4.19
initialized with (functions of) the leading eigenvector
v_{1}(D)
.
We consider initializers
\widetilde{r}_{0}=f_{0}(r^{0})
and
\widetilde{p}^{0}=g_{0}(p^{0};y)
, where

\displaystyle r^{0}

\displaystyle=s\,c_{r}\,\sqrt{d}\,v_{1}(D),

\displaystyle p^{0}

\displaystyle=c_{p}\,\gamma(\gamma I_{n}+\overline{G})^{-1}X\,\sqrt{d}\,v_{1}(D),

(4.20)

with

\displaystyle\overline{G}

\displaystyle=\diag(\overline{g}(y)),

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\sgn\left(\left\langle v_{1}(D),\beta_{*}\right\rangle\right),

\displaystyle c_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\rho+w_{2}}}{w_{2}},

\displaystyle c_{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\rho+w_{2}}}{w_{1}w_{3}}.

(4.21)

The constants
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
in
Equation

4.21
along with
w_{4}
are given below:

\displaystyle w_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\rho\frac{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}+\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}-\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]},

(4.22)

\displaystyle w_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\rho}{\delta}\frac{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}-2\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{4}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]}{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}-\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]},

(4.23)

\displaystyle w_{3}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}},\qquad w_{4}=\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right].

(4.24)

Note from
Equation

4.2
that
w_{3}w_{4}=\gamma
.
Moreover, the denominators of
w_{1},w_{2}
are positive provided
Equation

4.13
holds.

State Evolution.

The dynamics of
Equation

4.19
are accurately predicted by a deterministic
state evolution
recursion in the high-dimensional limit. Indeed,
Theorem

4.3
below shows that for
t\geq 1
, the empirical distribution of
r^{t}
converges to the law of
b_{t}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+{\mathsf{K}}_{t}
, where
b_{t}
is a deterministic scalar and
{\mathsf{K}}_{t}
is a zero-mean Gaussian independent of
{\mathsf{B}}_{*}
. Similarly, the empirical distribution of
p^{t}
converges to the law of
a_{t}{\mathsf{Z}}+{\mathsf{J}}_{t}
, for a scalar
a_{t}
and a zero-mean Gaussian
{\mathsf{J}}_{t}
independent of
{\mathsf{Z}}
.
We recursively define these state evolution parameters as follows.

Let

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{1}},

\displaystyle b_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{2}}.

(4.25)

Define

\displaystyle{\mathsf{R}}_{0}

\displaystyle=b_{0}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+{\mathsf{K}}_{0},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{0}

\displaystyle=a_{0}{\mathsf{Z}}+{\mathsf{J}}_{0},

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=f_{0}({\mathsf{R}}_{0}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{0}

\displaystyle=g_{0}({\mathsf{P}}_{0};{\mathsf{Y}}),

(4.26)

where

\displaystyle({\mathsf{B}}_{*},{\mathsf{K}}_{0})

\displaystyle\sim P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,b_{0}),

\displaystyle({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{J}}_{0})

\displaystyle\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2})\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,a_{0}),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Y}}

\displaystyle\sim Q\left(\cdot\,|\,{\mathsf{Z}}\right).

(4.27)

Furthermore, let

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{0}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-2}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{0}{\mathsf{Z}}\right],

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}_{0}

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{0}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}\right],

(4.28)

and
\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{-1}=\gamma^{-1}\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}}){\mathsf{P}}_{0},\widetilde{a}_{-1}=\gamma^{-1}a_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]
.

Let
({\mathsf{J}}_{r})_{0\leq r\leq t}
and
({\mathsf{K}}_{r})_{0\leq r\leq t}
be two centered Gaussian processes that are independent of each other and of
(\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{0},\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{0},{\mathsf{B}}_{*},{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n},{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d})
, with covariance structures specified below.
The law of
{\mathsf{J}}_{0},{\mathsf{K}}_{0}
is given in
Equation

4.27
.
For any
t\geq 0
, the correlation between
{\mathsf{J}}_{t+1}
(resp.
{\mathsf{K}}_{t+1}
) and
{\mathsf{J}}_{0}
(resp.
{\mathsf{K}}_{0}
) is given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{J}}_{t+1}{\mathsf{J}}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{t}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{t}\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{-1}\right]-2\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{t}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}\right]\widetilde{a}_{t}\widetilde{a}_{-1}\rho+\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{t}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{4}\right]\widetilde{a}_{t}\widetilde{a}_{-1}\rho

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}-1)\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}\right]\widetilde{a}_{-1}\widetilde{b}_{t}\rho-a_{t+1}a_{0}\sigma^{2},

(4.29a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{K}}_{t+1}{\mathsf{K}}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{t}\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{-1}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2}-1)\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}\right]\widetilde{a}_{t}\widetilde{a}_{-1}\rho

\displaystyle\quad+\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{t}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2}\right]\widetilde{a}_{-1}\widetilde{b}_{t}\rho-b_{t+1}b_{0}\rho.

(4.29b)

For every
0\leq r,s\leq t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{J}}_{r+1}{\mathsf{J}}_{s+1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2})\Psi_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2})\right]\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{r}\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{s}\right]-\widetilde{a}_{r}\widetilde{a}_{s}\sigma^{2}\right)+\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2})\Psi_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}\right]\widetilde{a}_{r}\widetilde{a}_{s}\rho

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Psi}_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n})\widetilde{\Psi}_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{r}\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{s}\right]-a_{r+1}a_{s+1}\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2})\widetilde{\Psi}_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}\right]\widetilde{a}_{r}\widetilde{b}_{s}\rho+\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2})\widetilde{\Psi}_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}\right]\widetilde{a}_{s}\widetilde{b}_{r}\rho,

(4.29c)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{K}}_{r+1}{\mathsf{K}}_{s+1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2})\Phi_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{r}\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{s}\right]-b_{r+1}b_{s+1}\rho

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Phi}_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d})\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d})\right]\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{r}\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{s}\right]-\widetilde{a}_{r}\widetilde{a}_{s}\sigma^{2}\right)+\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Phi}_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d})\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2}\right]\widetilde{a}_{r}\widetilde{a}_{s}\rho

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2})\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}\right]\widetilde{a}_{s}\widetilde{b}_{r}\rho+\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{s}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2})\widetilde{\Phi}_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}\right]\widetilde{a}_{r}\widetilde{b}_{s}\rho.

(4.29d)

In
Equations

4.29a
,
4.29b
,
4.29c
and
4.29d
, the scalars
a_{r+1},b_{r+1}
are defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{r}{\mathsf{Z}}\right],

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{r}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}\right],

(4.29e)

\displaystyle a_{r+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}\right]\widetilde{a}_{r}+\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Psi}_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}\right]\widetilde{b}_{r}\right),

\displaystyle b_{r+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Phi}_{r}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}){\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}\right]\widetilde{a}_{r},

(4.29f)

and the random variables
(\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{r+1})_{0\leq r\leq t},(\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{r+1})_{0\leq r\leq t}
are defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{r+1}

\displaystyle=g_{r+1}({\mathsf{P}}_{r+1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{\Xi}}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{r+1}

\displaystyle=f_{r+1}({\mathsf{R}}_{r+1};{\mathsf{B}}_{*},{\mathsf{\Theta}}),

(4.29g)

where

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{r+1}

\displaystyle=a_{r+1}{\mathsf{Z}}+{\mathsf{J}}_{r+1},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{R}}_{r+1}

\displaystyle=b_{r+1}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+{\mathsf{K}}_{r+1}.

(4.29h)

This completely specifies the laws of the Gaussian processes
({\mathsf{J}}_{t})_{t\geq 0},({\mathsf{K}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.

We are now ready to present the state evolution result for spectrally initialized GVAMP which asserts that the random variables defined above identify the Wasserstein limits of the iterates of
Equation

4.19
.
For this result to be valid, the matrix denoisers
\Phi_{t},\Psi_{t}
and vector denoisers
f_{t+1},g_{t+1}
are required to satisfy the conditions below.

(A7)

\Phi_{t},\Psi_{t}
are
trace-free
for every
t\geq 0
, that is,
\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{t}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi_{t}({\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2})\right]=0
.
Moreover,
\Phi_{t},\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}\in\mathrm{PG}_{1\to 1}
are continuous with probability
1
under the law of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}
;

\Psi_{t},\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}\in\mathrm{PG}_{1\to 1}
are continuous with probability
1
under
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}
.

(A8)

f_{t+1},g_{t+1}
are
divergence-free
with respect to their first inputs for every
t\geq 0
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f_{t+1}^{\prime}({\mathsf{R}}_{t+1};{\mathsf{B}}_{*},{\mathsf{\Theta}})\right]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t+1}^{\prime}({\mathsf{P}}_{t+1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{\Xi}})\right]

\displaystyle=0,

where
f_{t+1}^{\prime},g_{t+1}^{\prime}
denote the partial derivatives of
f_{t+1},g_{t+1}
with respect to the first arguments.

Recalling
Item

(A2)
and using
Proposition

K.3
,
Item

(A7)

implies that almost surely,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\tr(\Phi_{t}(X^{\top}X))

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\tr(\Psi_{t}(XX^{\top}))=0.

Finally, we isolate two technical assumptions regarding the regularity of matrix and vector denoisers.

(A9)

For every
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}\left\|\Phi_{t}(\Lambda^{\top}\Lambda)\right\|_{2},

\displaystyle\limsup_{d\to\infty}\left\|\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}(\Lambda)\right\|_{2},

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\Psi_{t}(\Lambda\Lambda^{\top})\right\|_{2},

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\|\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(\Lambda)\right\|_{2}

are all finite almost surely.

(A10)

For every
t\geq 0
,
f_{t+1},g_{t+1}
are Lipschitz in all arguments.
Furthermore, the partial derivatives of
r\mapsto f_{t+1}(r;b,\theta)
and
p\mapsto g_{t+1}(p;z,e,\xi)
are continuous with probability
1
with respect to the law of
({\mathsf{R}}_{t+1},{\mathsf{B}}_{*},{\mathsf{\Theta}})
and
({\mathsf{P}}_{t+1},{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{\Xi}})
, respectively.

Theorem 4.3

(State evolution of spectrally initialized GVAMP)
.

Consider the GVAMP iteration
Equation

4.19
initialized with
\widetilde{r}^{0},\widetilde{p}^{0}
given in
Equation

4.20
.
If
Items

(A5)
,
(A6)
,
(A7)
,
(A8)
,
(A9)
and
(A10)
and
Equation

4.13
hold, then for any fixed
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}r^{0}&\cdots&r^{t}&\widetilde{r}^{0}&\cdots&\widetilde{r}^{t}&\beta_{*}&\Theta\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{R}}_{0}&\cdots&{\mathsf{R}}_{t}&\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{0}&\cdots&\widetilde{{\mathsf{R}}}_{t}&{\mathsf{B}}_{*}&{\mathsf{\Theta}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix},

(4.30)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}p^{0}&\cdots&p^{t}&\widetilde{p}^{0}&\cdots&\widetilde{p}^{t}&z&\varepsilon&\Xi\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{P}}_{0}&\cdots&{\mathsf{P}}_{t}&\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{0}&\cdots&\widetilde{{\mathsf{P}}}_{t}&{\mathsf{Z}}&{\mathsf{E}}&{\mathsf{\Xi}}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}.

(4.31)

The full proof of
Theorem

4.3
is deferred to
Appendix

G
.
See
Section

5.3
for a brief overview of the proof.

Remark 4.4

(Choice of initializer)
.

The initializer
r^{0}
in
Equation

4.20
is given by the spectral estimator up to a rescaling factor
c_{r}
, while
p^{0}
is motivated by the heuristics in
Section

5.4
based on linearized GVAMP.
The rescaling factor
c_{r}
in
Equation

4.20
ensures that
r^{0}\xrightarrow{W_{2}}{\mathsf{R}}_{0}=b_{0}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+\sqrt{b_{0}}{\mathsf{G}}
as
d\to\infty
, with
{\mathsf{G}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
independent of
{\mathsf{B}}_{*}
; see
Equation

4.26
. That is,
the signal strength and the noise variance are both equal to
b_{0}
.
The rescaling factor
c_{p}
in
p^{0}
has a similar effect.
These special properties are immaterial for a generic GVAMP algorithm with spectral initialization.
However, they are crucial for obtaining a succinct state evolution for the spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP in the next section.

Remark 4.5

(Sign calibration)
.

The spectral initializer
r^{0}
in
Equation

4.20
contains a sign factor
s
defined in
Equation

4.21
, which ensures that the sign of the eigenvector is such that
\left\langle r^{0},\beta_{*}\right\rangle\geq 0
.
However,
s
is not computable by the statistician since it involves the unknown
\beta_{*}
.
In practice, without the sign calibration, one can run the state evolution twice with
s=1
and
s=-1
and one of the trials is guaranteed to track the dynamics of spectrally initialized GVAMP accurately.
Drawing inspiration from
[
28
, Appendix H]
, we believe that
s
is statistically identifiable via e.g. moment methods under additional structures (such as asymmetry) of the link
q
and prior
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}
, and non-identifiable otherwise (see also
[
48
, Remark 3.6]
).
We do not address this issue in more detail but remark that for the purpose of understanding the overlap
Equation

1.1
which is defined with an absolute value, the sign calibration is inconsequential and the theoretical prediction of asymptotic overlap given by our state evolution result is accurate regardless of the value of
s\in\{-1,1\}
.

4.3
Spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP and its state evolution

If the link function
q
and the laws of
{\mathsf{B}}_{*},{\mathsf{\Lambda}},{\mathsf{E}}
in
Items

(A1)
,
(A2)
and
(A3)
are known, one can choose the matrix and vector denoisers defining GVAMP to maximize the asymptotic overlap between
r^{t}
and
\beta_{*}
.
We now present this special case of GVAMP, called Bayes-GVAMP, which uses
q,P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}},\law({\mathsf{\Lambda}}),P_{{\mathsf{E}}}
to define the denoisers used in each iteration.

Define two functions
g_{x1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
g_{z1}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{R}}
as

\displaystyle g_{x1}(r_{0};\gamma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int xP_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}(x)\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma}{2}x^{2}+r_{0}x\right)\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}(x)\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma}{2}x^{2}+r_{0}x\right)\,\mathrm{d}x}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{B}}_{*}\,|\,{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\overline{{\mathsf{J}}}=\frac{r_{0}}{\gamma}\right],

(4.32)

\displaystyle g_{z1}(p_{0},y_{0};\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int zQ(y_{0}\,|\,z)\exp\left(-\frac{\tau}{2}\left(z-\frac{p_{0}}{\tau}\right)^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}z}{\int Q(y_{0}\,|\,z)\exp\left(-\frac{\tau}{2}\left(z-\frac{p_{0}}{\tau}\right)^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}z}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}\,|\,{\mathsf{P}}=p_{0},{\mathsf{Y}}=y_{0}\right],

(4.33)

for any
r_{0},\gamma,p_{0},y_{0},\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
The expectations in
Equations

4.32
and
4.33
are taken over random variables whose laws are specified as follows.
In
Equation

4.32
,
({\mathsf{B}}_{*},\overline{{\mathsf{J}}})\sim P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}\left(0,1\right)
.
In
Equation

4.33
,
{\mathsf{Z}}=\frac{1}{\tau}{\mathsf{P}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tau}}\overline{{\mathsf{K}}}
, where
({\mathsf{P}},\overline{{\mathsf{K}}})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,(\tau-\sigma^{-2})\tau\sigma^{2})\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
Furthermore,
{\mathsf{Y}}\sim Q\left(\cdot\,|\,{\mathsf{Z}}\right)
.
We use
g_{x1}^{\prime},g_{z1}^{\prime}
to denote the derivatives of
g_{x1},g_{z1}
with respect to their first arguments.
Elementary calculations show that

\displaystyle g_{x1}^{\prime}(r_{0};\gamma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Var}\left[{\mathsf{B}}_{*}\,|\,{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\overline{{\mathsf{J}}}=\frac{r_{0}}{\gamma}\right],

\displaystyle g_{z1}^{\prime}(p_{0},y_{0};\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Var}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}\,|\,{\mathsf{P}}=p_{0},{\mathsf{Y}}=y_{0}\right].

(4.34)

We also define functions
g_{x2}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
g_{z2}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
as

\displaystyle g_{x2}(r,p;\gamma,\tau)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\gamma}r+X^{\top}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\tau}I_{n}+XX^{\top}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}p-\frac{1}{\gamma}Xr\right),

(4.35a)

\displaystyle g_{z2}(r,p;\gamma,\tau)

\displaystyle\coloneqq Xg_{x2}(r,p,\gamma,\tau),

(4.35b)

for any
r\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
\gamma,\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

The Bayes-GVAMP algorithm maintains a collection of iterates that are updated for every
t\geq 1
as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{1}^{t}

\displaystyle=g_{x1}(r^{t};\gamma_{1}^{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{1}^{t}

\displaystyle=g_{z1}(p^{t},y;\tau_{1}^{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(4.36a)

\displaystyle v_{1}^{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[g_{x1}^{\prime}({\mathsf{R}}_{t};\gamma_{1}^{t})\right]\in{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle c_{1}^{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[g_{z1}^{\prime}({\mathsf{P}}_{t},{\mathsf{Y}};\tau_{1}^{t})\right]\in{\mathbb{R}},

(4.36b)

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}^{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v_{1}^{t}}\widehat{x}_{1}^{t}-r^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}^{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{1}^{t}}\widehat{z}_{1}^{t}-p^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(4.36c)

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}^{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v_{1}^{t}}-\gamma_{1}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle\tau_{2}^{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{1}^{t}}-\tau_{1}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}},

(4.36d)

\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{2}^{t}

\displaystyle=g_{x2}(\widetilde{r}^{t},\widetilde{p}^{t};\gamma_{2}^{t},\tau_{2}^{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

\displaystyle\widehat{z}_{2}^{t}

\displaystyle=g_{z2}(\widetilde{r}^{t},\widetilde{p}^{t};\gamma_{2}^{t},\tau_{2}^{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

(4.36e)

\displaystyle v_{2}^{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\tau_{2}^{t}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2}+\gamma_{2}^{t}}\right]\in{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle c_{2}^{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}}{\tau_{2}^{t}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}+\gamma_{2}^{t}}\right]\in{\mathbb{R}},

(4.36f)

\displaystyle r^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}}\widehat{x}_{2}^{t}-\widetilde{r}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

\displaystyle p^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}^{t}}\widehat{z}_{2}^{t}-\widetilde{p}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(4.36g)

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}}-\gamma_{2}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle\tau_{1}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}^{t}}-\tau_{2}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}},

(4.36h)

where

\displaystyle{\mathsf{R}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}^{t}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+\sqrt{\gamma_{1}^{t}}\overline{{\mathsf{J}}}_{t},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{P}}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{1}^{t}-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\right){\mathsf{Z}}+\sqrt{\tau_{1}^{t}-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}}\overline{{\mathsf{K}}}_{t},

(4.37)

\overline{{\mathsf{J}}}_{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
is independent of
{\mathsf{B}}_{*}
, and
\overline{{\mathsf{K}}}_{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
is independent of
{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}}
.
The above iteration is initialized with
r^{0},p^{0}
given in
Equation

4.20
and

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{2}},

\displaystyle\tau_{1}^{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{1}}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}.

(4.38)

The Bayes-GVAMP iteration
Equation

4.36
can be shown to be a special case of the generic GVAMP iteration in
Equation

4.19
.
Using the definition of
g_{x2},g_{z2}
and applying elementary manipulations to
Equation

4.36
bring us to

\displaystyle r^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\left[\left(\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}\gamma_{2}^{t}}-1\right)I_{d}-\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}\gamma_{2}^{t}}X^{\top}\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}^{t}}{\tau_{2}^{t}}I_{n}+XX^{\top}\right)^{-1}X\right]\left(\frac{1}{v_{1}^{t}}\widehat{x}_{1}^{t}-r^{t}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}\tau_{2}^{t}}X^{\top}\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}^{t}}{\tau_{2}^{t}}I_{n}+XX^{\top}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{c_{1}^{t}}\widehat{z}_{1}^{t}-p^{t}\right),

(4.39a)

\displaystyle p^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{c_{2}^{t}\tau_{2}^{t}}XX^{\top}\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}^{t}}{\tau_{2}^{t}}I_{n}+XX^{\top}\right)^{-1}-I_{n}\right]\left(\frac{1}{c_{1}^{t}}\widehat{z}_{1}^{t}-p^{t}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{c_{2}^{t}\gamma_{2}^{t}}X\left[I_{d}-X^{\top}\left(\frac{\gamma_{2}^{t}}{\tau_{2}^{t}}I_{n}+XX^{\top}\right)^{-1}X\right]\left(\frac{1}{v_{1}^{t}}\widehat{x}_{1}^{t}-r^{t}\right).

(4.39b)

This corresponds to taking the denoisers in
Equation

4.19
to be

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}\gamma_{2}^{t}}-1\right)-\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}\gamma_{2}^{t}}\cdot\frac{x}{\frac{\gamma_{2}^{t}}{\tau_{2}^{t}}+x},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}\tau_{2}^{t}}\cdot\frac{x}{\frac{\gamma_{2}^{t}}{\tau_{2}^{t}}+x^{2}},

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}}\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}^{t}+\tau_{2}^{t}x}-1,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v_{2}^{t}}\cdot\frac{x}{\gamma_{2}^{t}+\tau_{2}^{t}x^{2}},

(4.39c)

\displaystyle\Psi_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}^{t}\tau_{2}^{t}}\cdot\frac{x}{\frac{\gamma_{2}^{t}}{\tau_{2}^{t}}+x}-1,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}^{t}\gamma_{2}^{t}}\cdot x\left(1-\frac{x^{2}}{\frac{\gamma_{2}^{t}}{\tau_{2}^{t}}+x^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}^{t}}\cdot\frac{x}{\gamma_{2}^{t}+\tau_{2}^{t}x}-1,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{2}^{t}}\cdot\frac{x}{\gamma_{2}^{t}+\tau_{2}^{t}x^{2}},

(4.39d)

\displaystyle f_{t}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{v_{1}^{t}}g_{x1}(r;\gamma_{1}^{t})-r,

\displaystyle g_{t}(p;y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{1}^{t}}g_{z1}(p,y;\tau_{1}^{t})-p.

(4.39e)

The matrix denoisers
\Phi_{t},\Psi_{t}
and the vector denoisers
f_{t},g_{t}
above indeed satisfy the trace-free and divergence-free conditions required by
Items

(A7)
and
(A8)
, as shown in
Lemma

H.1
.

Theorem 4.4

(State evolution of spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP)
.

Consider the Bayes-GVAMP algorithm in
Equation

4.36
initialized with
Equation

4.20
.
Then for any fixed
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}r^{t}&\beta_{*}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{R}}_{t}&{\mathsf{B}}_{*}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}p^{t}&z&\varepsilon\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{P}}_{t}&{\mathsf{Z}}&{\mathsf{E}}\end{bmatrix},

where the random variables on the right are defined in
Equation

4.37
.

Theorem

4.4
is proved in
Appendix

H
by inducting on
t
to simplify the state evolution recursion
Equation

4.29
to
Equation

4.36
under the configuration of
Equation

4.39
.

Figure

3
numerically validates that the trajectory of overlaps achieved by spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP
Equation

4.36
is accurately tracked by its state evolution.
It is observed that Bayes-GVAMP typically converges rapidly (within
10
steps) even at moderate values of
\delta
.

Figure 3
:
The trajectory of spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP in
Equation

4.36
on phase retrieval is accurately tracked by its state evolution. Both trajectories are plotted for
3
spectral distributions in
Equation

6.3
at
\delta=2
.

4.4
Conjectured Bayes risk

In this section, we recall the Bayes risk derived in
[
66
]
using the non-rigorous replica method and show that Bayes-GVAMP has the same fixed points.
Define two functions
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathrm{out}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(b,a)

\displaystyle=\int P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}(x)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}ax^{2}+bx\right)\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathrm{out}}(y;\xi,v)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Q\left(y\,|\,\sqrt{v}\,{\mathsf{\Omega}}+\xi\right)\right],

(4.40)

where the expectation is over
{\mathsf{\Omega}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
Denote by
\partial_{b}\log{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(b,\cdot)
and
\partial_{\xi}\log{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathrm{out}}(\cdot;\xi,\cdot)
the derivatives of the maps
b\mapsto\log{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(b,\cdot)
and
\xi\mapsto\log{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathrm{out}}(\cdot;\xi,\cdot)
, respectively.

Consider the following system of equations in the variables
q_{x},q_{y},\widehat{q}_{x},\widehat{q}_{y},\gamma_{x},\gamma_{y}
:

\displaystyle q_{x}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\left(\sqrt{\widehat{q}_{x}}\,{\mathsf{\Xi}},\widehat{q}_{x}\right)\left(\partial_{b}\log{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\left(\sqrt{\widehat{q}_{x}}\,{\mathsf{\Xi}},\widehat{q}_{x}\right)\right)^{2}\right],

(4.41a)

\displaystyle q_{z}=\frac{1}{\widehat{Q}_{z}+\widehat{q}_{z}}\left(\frac{\widehat{q}_{z}}{\widehat{Q}_{z}}+\mathbb{E}\left[\int{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathrm{out}}\left(y;\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{q}_{z}}{\widehat{Q}_{z}(\widehat{Q}_{z}+\widehat{q}_{z})}}\,{\mathsf{\Xi}},\frac{1}{\widehat{Q}_{z}+\widehat{q}_{z}}\right)\left(\partial_{\xi}\log{\mathcal{Z}}_{\mathrm{out}}\left(y;\sqrt{\frac{\widehat{q}_{z}}{\widehat{Q}_{z}(\widehat{Q}_{z}+\widehat{q}_{z})}}\,{\mathsf{\Xi}},\frac{1}{\widehat{Q}_{z}+\widehat{q}_{z}}\right)\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}y\right]\right),

(4.41b)

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{x}=\frac{q_{x}}{\rho(\rho-q_{x})}-\gamma_{x},

(4.41c)

\displaystyle\widehat{q}_{z}=\frac{q_{z}}{Q_{z}(Q_{z}-q_{z})}-\gamma_{z},

(4.41d)

\displaystyle\rho-q_{x}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\rho^{-1}+\gamma_{x}+\gamma_{z}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2}}\right],

(4.41e)

\displaystyle Q_{z}-q_{z}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}}{\rho^{-1}+\gamma_{x}+\gamma_{z}{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}}\right],

(4.41f)

where the expectations in the first two equations are over
{\mathsf{\Xi}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
and the constants
Q_{z},\widetilde{Q}_{z}
are defined as

\displaystyle Q_{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{d}^{2}\right]}{\delta},

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Q_{z}}.

(4.42)

We also recall that
\rho
is the variance of
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}
(
Item

(A1)
).
The system of equations
Equation

4.41
is referred to as the replica saddle point equations in the literature
[
66
]
.

Define
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{B}}\equiv\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{B}}(X,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(with subscript
B
for ‘Bayesian’) as the posterior mean of
\beta_{*}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{B}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{*}\,|\,X,y\right]=\frac{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\beta P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}^{\otimes d}(\beta)Q^{\otimes d}\left(y\,|\,X\beta\right)\,\mathrm{d}\beta}{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}^{\otimes d}(\beta)Q^{\otimes d}\left(y\,|\,X\beta\right)\,\mathrm{d}\beta}.

(4.43)

It is well-known that
\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{B}}
minimizes the mean squared error for estimating
\beta_{*}
given
(X,y)
from the generalized linear model
Equation

3.5
.

Conjecture 4.5

(
[
66
, Conjecture 2.1]
)
.

There exists a tuple

\displaystyle(\widehat{q}_{x}^{*},\widehat{q}_{z}^{*},\gamma_{x}^{*},\gamma_{z}^{*},q_{x}^{*},q_{z}^{*})\in[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\rho]\times[0,Q_{z}]

solving
Equation

4.41
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\beta_{*}-\widehat{\beta}_{\textnormal{B}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\rho-q_{x}^{*},

where the expectation is over
\beta_{*},X,y
.

When the solution to
Equation

4.41
is not unique,
q_{x}^{*}
is obtained by maximizing a certain free entropy functional.
We do not define this functional formally due to its complexity, but instead refer the reader to
[
66
, Conjecture 2.1]
.
In what follows, we verify that the set of solutions to
Equation

4.41
is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of fixed points of the state evolution recursion for Bayes-GVAMP in
Equation

4.36
.

Theorem 4.6
.

The set of fixed points

\displaystyle(\gamma_{1},\tau_{1},\gamma_{2},\tau_{2},v_{1},v_{2},c_{1},c_{2})\in[0,\infty)\times\left[\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}},\infty\right)\times\left[\frac{1}{\rho},\infty\right)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\rho]\times[0,\rho]\times[0,\sigma^{2}]\times[0,\sigma^{2}]

(4.44)

of the recursion for
(\gamma_{1}^{t},\tau_{1}^{t},\gamma_{2}^{t},\tau_{2}^{t},v_{1}^{t},v_{2}^{t},c_{1}^{t},c_{2}^{t})
in
Equation

4.36
is the same as the set of tuples

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{q}_{x},\widehat{q}_{z}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}},\gamma_{x}+\frac{1}{\rho},\gamma_{z},\rho-q_{x},\rho-q_{x},\sigma^{2}-q_{z},\sigma^{2}-q_{z}\right)

(4.45)

such that

\displaystyle(\widehat{q}_{x},\widehat{q}_{z},\gamma_{x},\gamma_{z},q_{x},q_{z})\in[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\rho]\times[0,Q_{z}]

are the solutions to the replica saddle point equations
Equation

4.41
.

Theorem

4.6
is proved in
Appendix

I
by verifying the equivalence between
Equations

4.36
and
4.41
under the change of variables in
Equation

4.45
.

The tuple of fixed points in
Theorem

4.6
may not be unique.
Due to the equivalence between
Equations

4.44
and
4.45
, let us only examine
Equation

4.44
.
For instance, consider the (noiseless) phase retrieval model
q(z,\varepsilon)=\left|z\right|
with Gaussian prior
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}={\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
Specializing
Conjecture

4.5
to this setting, the asymptotic Bayes risk is conjectured to be
v\in[0,1]
which solves the equation
{\mathcal{F}}(v)=v
for a certain function
{\mathcal{F}}
;
see
Section

J.1
for the precise definition of
{\mathcal{F}}
and a derivation of this result.

Figure

4
shows the non-uniqueness of the solution
v
under
3
different spectral distributions of
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
(see
Section

6.1
for their definitions) and certain values of
\delta
.

Figure 4
:
Plots of the function
{\mathcal{F}}
and its fixed points, i.e. solution
v\in[0,\rho]
to
v={\mathcal{F}}(v)
, for
3
spectral distributions
Equation

6.3
.
From the leftmost to the rightmost panel,
\delta
is taken to be
1.44
,
1
,
1.34
, respectively.

Under the same setting above, we also plot in
Figure

5
the overlap achieved by (the state evolution of) spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP at convergence, the conjectured Bayes-optimal overlap
\sqrt{1-v/\rho}
(which can be seen by applying the tower property of conditional expectation to
Conjecture

4.5
), and for comparison, the asymptotic overlap of spectral estimator given by
Theorem

4.2
.
It is observed that spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP in
Equation

4.36
achieves the conjectured Bayes-optimal overlap when the solution
v
is unique, and otherwise achieves an overlap corresponding to a worse solution
v
.

For an i.i.d. Gaussian design which is unstructured, it has been well observed
[
5
]
that multiple state evolution fixed points and the consequent performance gap between the optimal AMP algorithm
[
87
,
72
]
and the Bayes-optimal posterior mean
Equation

4.43
may emerge in the presence of structured
prior
.
Here,
Figures

4
and
5
are exclusively under the unstructured Gaussian prior.
Our finding therefore complements the above observation by providing another source for multiple fixed points and performance gaps, which is a structured
design
.
We note that such performance gaps can be drastic.
Indeed, in both the leftmost and rightmost panels of
Figure

5
, there exists a range of
\delta
in which the posterior mean achieves perfect recovery whereas Bayes-GVAMP is ineffective.

Figure 5
:
Asymptotic overlap
Equation

4.11
achieved by the spectral estimator in
Theorem

4.2
(‘spec’ in black), overlap achieved by the state evolution of spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP in
Equation

4.36
run till convergence (‘SE’ in red), and the conjectured Bayes risk
Equation

4.41
expressed in terms of overlap (‘replica’ in blue). All curves are plotted for
3
spectral distributions given in
Equation

6.3
.

5
Heuristics and proof overview

In
Section

5.1
