, we draw heuristic connections between linearized GVAMP and spectral estimators, thereby motivating spectral estimators of the form
Equation

4.1
subject to
Item

(A5)
.
In
Section

5.2
, we highlight the key difficulties in the spectral analysis of the random matrix
D
and provide a proof overview of
Theorem

4.1
.

Section

5.3
motivates our generic GVAMP formalism.
In
Section

5.4
, we offer additional intuition on the design of the spectral initialization in
Equation

4.20
for GVAMP.

5.1
Simulating spectral estimators using linearized GVAMP

Let us consider the following GVAMP algorithm with linear denoising functions:

\displaystyle u^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}XX^{\top}-I_{n}\right)g(u^{t-1}),

\displaystyle v^{t}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}X^{\top}g(u^{t-1}),

(5.1)

where

\displaystyle g(u)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)u,

and
G=\diag(\widehat{g}(y))
is a diagonal matrix obtained by applying an arbitrary function
\widehat{g}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
to
y
component-wise.
As shown in
Section

C.1
,
Equation

5.1
is a special case of the GVAMP algorithm in
Equation

4.19
and also of the RI-GAMP algorithm in
[
VKM
]
.
By
[
65
, Equation (16)]
, with a specific choice of
G
, the iteration
Equation

5.1
arises as the linearization of the Bayes-GVAMP algorithm
[
90
]
(see also
Equation

4.36
) around its trivial fixed point.
In the context of generalized linear models with partial Haar design, a similar iteration is derived in
[
61
, Equation (21a)]
from expectation propagation and is used to heuristically analyze spectral estimators.

Note that the iteration of
u^{t}
above operates on a random matrix with vanishing trace:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\tr\left(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}XX^{\top}-I_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

Furthermore, the denoising function
g
has zero divergence since

\displaystyle\tr\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

For large
t
, we expect that
u^{t}/\left\|u^{t}\right\|_{2}
converges in the following sense:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\left|\left\langle u^{t},u^{t-1}\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|u^{t}\right\|_{2}\left\|u^{t-1}\right\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=1.

(5.2)

In other words, assuming that the limit

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left\langle u^{t},u^{t-1}\right\rangle}{\left\|u^{t-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}}

(5.3)

exists, then we expect that two adjacent iterates are collinear for large
t
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\left\|u^{t}-\gamma u^{t-1}\right\|_{2}}{\left\|u^{t-1}\right\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=0.

(5.4)

Note that
\gamma\equiv\gamma(\widehat{g})
depends on the function
\widehat{g}
.
We caution that
\gamma=1
need not hold, in which case
Equation

5.2
or
Equation

5.4
does not imply
\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-1}\left\|u^{t}-u^{t-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}=0
.
See the discussion following
Lemma

B.1
for more details.
The fixed point of the iteration
Equation

5.1
can then be written as:

\displaystyle\gamma\widehat{u}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}XX^{\top}-I_{n}\right)\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)\widehat{u}

(5.5)

for some unit vector
\widehat{u}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
.
This equation is closely related to an eigenequation of
D
of the form
Equation

4.1a
(with respect to some
{\mathcal{T}}
to be specified).
Indeed, rearranging terms, we get

\displaystyle\left[(\gamma-1)I_{n}+\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}\right]\widehat{u}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}XX^{\top}\right)\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)\widehat{u}.

Left-multiplying both sides by

\displaystyle X^{\top}\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)\left[(\gamma-1)I_{n}+\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}\right]^{-1},

we get

\displaystyle X^{\top}\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)\widehat{u}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}X^{\top}\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)\left[(\gamma-1)I_{n}+\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}\right]^{-1}XX^{\top}\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)\widehat{u}.

We recognize that this is an eigenequation of the matrix

\displaystyle X^{\top}\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)\left[(\gamma-1)I_{n}+\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}\right]^{-1}X

(5.6)

corresponding to the eigenvector (up to rescaling)

\displaystyle X^{\top}\left(\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}\right)\widehat{u}.

(5.7)

The matrix in
Equation

5.6
is asymptotically equivalent to
D
in
Equation

4.1a
with
{\mathcal{T}}
taking the form of
Equation

4.2
and
\overline{g}
therein given by

\displaystyle\overline{g}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\widehat{g}(y)}{\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]}-1,

which clearly satisfies the condition
\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]=0
required by
Equation

4.3
.
We remark that the value of
\gamma
in
Equation

5.3
can be derived using state evolution (see
Appendix

B
, or more specifically
Lemma

E.1
), leading to the explicit expression in
Equation

4.2
.
This motivates the spectral estimator
Equation

4.1
with respect to preprocessing functions subject to
Item

(A5)
.

5.2
Analysis of
D
and proof overview of
Theorem

4.1

Our analysis of the matrix
D
in
Equation

4.1a
deviates from the standard random matrix theory approach.
Recalling
Item

(A2)
, we can write this matrix as
D=Q\Lambda^{\top}O^{\top}TO\Lambda Q^{\top}
which formally resembles a spiked multiplicative model
[
36
]
.
There are, however, three important distinctions.
In a standard spiked multiplicative model, it is typically assumed that
(a)

\Lambda,T
are independent of the Haar matrices
O,Q
,
(b)

T
is positive semidefinite which brings the convenient property that the spectra of
Q\Lambda^{\top}O^{\top}TO\Lambda Q^{\top}
and
T^{1/2}O\Lambda\Lambda^{\top}O^{\top}T^{1/2}
differ only at
0
, and
(c)

\Lambda,T
contain spectral outliers at fixed locations.
The last assumption can be equivalently understood as requiring the low rank spikes on
\Lambda,T
to be aligned with the eigenvectors corresponding to the given outlying eigenvalues.
In our setting, none of
(a)
,
(b)
,
(c)
hold:
T
involves the Haar randomness from
O,Q
and can take negative values (see
Theorem

4.2
); the spike enters
T
through a
1
-dimensional projection
X\beta_{*}
along the unknown parameter vector
\beta_{*}
.
Existing results on spiked multiplicative models are therefore inapplicable.
Instead, we exploit and make rigorous the connections in
Section

5.1
between spectral estimators and linearized GVAMP by combining tools from AMP, random matrix theory and free probability.

The precise characterization of asymptotic spectral properties of
D
in
Theorem

4.1
is accomplished in three steps.
First, motivated by the linearization heuristics in
Section

5
, we consider an instance
Equation

5.1
of GVAMP in
Equation

4.19
, derive its state evolution and study its fixed points.
These results are summarized in
Lemma

B.1
(proved in
Appendix

C
).
Second, using tools from random matrix theory and free probability, we determine the limit of the second (non-outlying) eigenvalue of
D
; see
Lemma

B.2
(proved in
Appendix

D
).
Taking the above two ingredients as input, the crux of the argument lies in
Lemma

B.3
(proved in
Appendix

E
) which shows that the linearized GVAMP iterate is asymptotically aligned with
v_{1}(D)
of interest and the corresponding outlying eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}(D)
can be accurately located.
Given this, the asymptotic overlap achieved by the spectral estimator is computed by invoking the state evolution result in the first step.

We discuss in more detail the proof strategy for characterizing the right edge of the bulk of
D
(
Lemma

B.2
) as it is of independent interest in random matrix theory and free probability.
This part is in turn divided into three steps.

Section

D.1
sets the stage for the study of a ‘null’ counterpart of
D
for which some assumptions in
Sections

3.2
and
4.1
can be relaxed.
Informally,
X
and
T
in the null matrix are independent.

Section

D.2
shows that the top
k
eigenvalues (for any fixed
k\geq 1
relative to
n,d
) of the null matrix all converge to the supremum of the support of its limiting spectral distribution, a type of result known as strong convergence
[
vanHandel
]
.
By eigenvalue interlacing (
Lemma

D.1
), this immediately implies that the second largest eigenvalue of
D
has the same limit.
A key component in this step in fact establishes a more general statement regarding the strong asymptotic freeness of Haar orthogonal and deterministic matrices, which implies strong convergence of matrix products involving such matrices.
This is done by a reduction using functional calculus to known strong asymptotic freeness of GOEs and deterministic matrices
[
45
, Theorem 4.2]
.
The limiting spectral distribution is expressed in terms of free multiplicative convolution, a characterization of whose right edge does not appear to be available.
In
Section

D.3
,
using tools from complex analysis and free probability, we develop such an explicit characterization for the right edge of the free convolution of more general infinite-dimensional operators in terms of the largest critical point of a certain functional.
This functional can be loosely understood as the inverse Stieltjes transform of the free convolution.
Finally,
Section

D.4
specializes this explicit characterization to the setting of
Sections

3.2
and
4.1
under which further simplifications can be made, eventually leading to the characterization in
Lemma

B.2
.

5.3
The generic GVAMP formalism and proof overview of
Theorem

4.3

Our generic GVAMP formalism in
Equation

4.19
draws inspiration from multiple sources (see
Section

2
) with the closest one in spirit being
[
43
]
.
In settings where the data matrix
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d}
is symmetric, an abstract form of the VAMP iteration
r^{t+1}=\Phi_{t}(X)f_{t}(r^{t})
is introduced in
[
43
]
, where
\Phi_{t}
is trace-free and
f_{t}
is divergence-free in the same senses as
Items

(A7)
and
(A8)
respectively.
These two conditions are the key to a tractable state evolution without loss of optimality.
In settings such as GLMs where
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
is typically rectangular, our GVAMP formalism can be understood as operating on an augmented block matrix
Y_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(n+d)\times(n+d)}
:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}r^{t+1}\\
p^{t+1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\Phi_{t}(X^{\top}X)&\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}(X)^{\top}\\
\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(X)&\Psi_{t}(XX^{\top})\end{bmatrix}}_{Y_{t}}\begin{bmatrix}f_{t}(r^{t})\\
g_{t}(p^{t})\end{bmatrix},

(5.8)

while still imposing divergence-free conditions on the vector denoisers, but only imposing trace-free condition on the diagonal blocks of
Y_{t}
.
Note that
Equation

5.8
cannot be reduced to the symmetric version studied in
[
43
]
for two reasons.
First,
Y_{t}
is not invariant under conjugation by
{\mathbb{O}}(n+d)
, but is instead block-invariant under conjugation by
{\mathbb{O}}(n)\times{\mathbb{O}}(d)
.
Second,
Y_{t}
is not symmetric since
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t},\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}
may differ.
This flexibility is particularly useful in applications to GLMs where the optimal choices of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t},\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}
are indeed different.
Informally, this is because the statistical model
Equation

3.4
only reveals
y
but not
\beta_{*}
, which eventually breaks the symmetry between
n
- and
d
-dimensional iterates in
Equation

5.8
.
The proof of the state evolution result (
Theorem

4.3
) for GVAMP follows the iterative Haar conditioning strategy of
[
46
]
with careful treatment of the distinguished vectors
\beta_{*}
and
y
(or more generally
z,\varepsilon
) which
f_{t}
and
g_{t}
can take as inputs (though omitted from the notation in
Equation

5.8
).
The formalism
Equation

5.8
can be easily seen equivalent to
Equation

4.19
and is general enough to include the Bayes-GVAMP
Equation

4.36
first derived heuristically in
[
90
]
.
The general theory developed here allows us to rigorously track the dynamics of GVAMP with spectral initialization.

5.4
Design of spectral initialization

To properly initialize GVAMP with spectral estimators, the
d
- and
n
-dimensional initializers
r^{0},p^{0}
need to respect a certain algebraic relation to ensure the correctness of state evolution.
Consider again the linearized GVAMP algorithm in
Equation

5.1
with some
\widehat{g}
.
Let
\overline{G}=\frac{G}{\tr(G)/n}-I_{n}
.
Denote the fixed point of this iteration by the same notation with time indices removed.
Let
\widehat{u},\gamma
be as in
Equations

5.3
and
5.5
.
According to
Equation

5.7
,
v_{1}(D)
is aligned with
\widehat{v}=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}X^{\top}\overline{G}\widehat{u}
which is in turn aligned with
v^{t}
for large
d,t
.
Then by the update rule of
Equation

5.1
,

\displaystyle\gamma\widehat{u}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}XX^{\top}-I)\overline{G}\widehat{u}=X\widehat{v}-\overline{G}\widehat{u}.

Solving for
\gamma\widehat{u}
gives

\displaystyle\gamma\widehat{u}

\displaystyle=\gamma\left(\gamma I_{n}+\overline{G}\right)^{-1}X\widehat{v},

thereby motivating the choice of
c_{p}^{-1}p^{0}
in
Equation

4.20
.

6
Examples and numerical experiments

We present numerical simulations of our proposed algorithms (as well as some existing methods from the literature) and empirical validate the corresponding theory on the phase retrieval and Poisson regression models, with both synthetic and real data.
Some implementation details are deferred to
Appendix

J
.

Figures

6
and
7
compare the performance of five algorithms for phase retrieval and Poisson regression, respectively, for three choices of design spectral distribution. The algorithms are:
the spectral estimator in
Theorem

4.2
(‘spec’, shown in black in the plots),
the spectral estimator in
[
60
, Equation (20)]
(‘spec conj’ in green);
Bayes-GVAMP in
Equation

4.36
(‘AMP’ in red), gradient descent (‘GD’ in blue), and the Generalized Approximate Message Passing algorithm developed for an i.i.d. Gaussian
X

[
72
, Section 3]
(‘GAMP’ in yellow).

Bayes-GVAMP is initialized with ‘spec’, whereas gradient descent and GVAMP are initialized with ‘spec conj’.
The spectral estimator from
[
60
]
(‘spec conj’) takes the same form as
Equation

4.1
but with the preprocessing function

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{g}(y)}{\overline{g}(y)+1},

(6.1)

where
\overline{g}
is given by
Equation

4.16
.
For i.i.d. Gaussian
[
60
]
, correlated Gaussian
[
105
]
and partial Haar designs
[
39
]
, the same
{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}
in
Equation

6.1
is shown to be the optimal choice that simultaneously minimizes the weak recovery threshold and maximizes overlap within a large function class that surpasses the one defined by
Item

(A5)
.
Using non-rigorous methods from statistical physics,
[
65
, Conjecture 2]
predicts that such optimality of
{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}
holds for any orthogonally invariant design.
The above
{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}
does not satisfy
Item

(A5)
and therefore our results in
Theorems

4.1
and
4.4
do not apply.
Here, we numerically test its performance and observe that the spectral estimator with respect to
{\mathcal{T}}^{\circ}
alone has inferior performance to Bayes-GVAMP initialized with our spectral estimator defined by
Equation

4.16
and
Item

(A5)
.

For gradient descent, we use the standard full-batch version

\displaystyle\theta^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\theta^{t}-\eta\nabla L(\theta^{t}),

where the learning rate
\eta>0
is fixed and the empirical loss

\displaystyle L(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\widehat{q}(\left\langle x_{i},\theta\right\rangle),y_{i})

is defined with respect to a postulated model
\hat{q}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and a suitable loss function
\ell\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
(both detailed below).

We observe that GAMP
[
87
,
72
]
, which is believed to be computationally optimal for i.i.d. Gaussian designs
[
5
,
74
]
, is significantly suboptimal for the non-Gaussian designs in
Figures

6
and
7
.
Indeed, in some cases, e.g. when
X
has
\Beta(1,2)
-distributed singular values (middle panels of
Figures

6
and
7
) or comes from GTEx datasets (
Figure

1
), GAMP does not even retain the performance of its spectral initializer
Equation

6.1
, and is also worse than general-purpose algorithms such as gradient descent.

We observe that in all plots, the overlaps achieved by both the spectral estimator in
Theorem

4.2
and Bayes-GVAMP initialized with it are accurately predicted by our theories in
Theorems

4.4
and
4.1
(‘spec, thy’ and ‘AMP, thy’ respectively plotted as solid curves with the corresponding color coding).

6.1
Synthetic data

In all numerical experiments with synthetic data, the design matrix is constructed according to
Item

(A2)
with
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
taken to be

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}},&\delta\leq 1\\
\sqrt{\delta}\,\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}},&\delta>1\end{cases},

(6.2)

for some positive random variable
\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
with compact support, second moment
1
and positive variance.
It is easy to verify that the above construction ensures
\overline{\kappa}_{2}=\overline{m}_{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}\right]=1
, a natural normalization consistent with the standard Gaussian design where
X_{i,j}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/d)
.
All synthetic experiments are repeated for
3
choices of
\widetilde{{\mathsf{\Lambda}}}
:

\displaystyle\sqrt{3/6}\unif([1,2]),

\displaystyle\sqrt{6}\Beta(1,2),

\displaystyle\sqrt{5/3}\Beta(3,1),

(6.3)

whose densities are constant, linear and quadratic on their respective supports:

\displaystyle p_{\unif([1,2])}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{[1,2]}(x),

\displaystyle p_{\Beta(1,2)}(x)

\displaystyle=2(1-x)\mathds{1}_{[0,1]}(x),

\displaystyle p_{\Beta(3,1)}(x)

\displaystyle=3x^{2}\mathds{1}_{[0,1]}(x).

Throughout this section, we fix
\rho=1
and consider the Gaussian prior
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}={\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
This implies
\sigma^{2}=1
by definition
Equation

3.9
.
We fix the number of regression coefficients to be
d=1000
and vary
\delta
by varying
n
accordingly.
For each value of
\delta
, we report the overlap as per
Equation

1.1
of different algorithms averaged over
10
i.i.d. trials with error bar at
1
standard deviation.

Phase retrieval.

Consider the (noiseless) phase retrieval model

\displaystyle q(z,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\left|z\right|

(6.4)

for which
Q\left(y\,|\,z\right)=\delta_{\left|z\right|}(y)
.
For gradient descent, we take the postulated model
\widehat{q}(z)=\left|z\right|
to be the same as the true model
q
in
Equation

6.4
and use the squared loss
\ell(\widehat{y},y)=\frac{1}{2}(\widehat{y}-y)^{2}
.
The learning rate
\eta
is taken to be
0.1,0.01,0.1
respectively for the
3
spectral distributions in
Equation

6.3
.

Figure 6
:
Overlaps achieved by
5
algorithms on the phase retrieval model
Equation

6.4
. The same experiments are repeated for
3
spectral distributions given in
Equation

6.3
of the design matrix
X
.

Poisson regression.

Consider Poisson regression where

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle\sim\pois(\left\langle x_{i},\beta_{*}\right\rangle^{2}).

(6.5)

That is, the conditional density
Q
is given by

\displaystyle Q\left(y\,|\,z\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{z^{2y}e^{-z^{2}}}{y!},

\displaystyle\textnormal{ for }y\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}.

For gradient descent, we take the postulated model
\widehat{q}(z)=z^{2}
to be the noiseless analogue of the true model
Equation

6.5
and use the absolute loss
\ell(\widehat{y},y)=\left|\widehat{y}-y\right|
.
The learning rate is fixed to be
\eta=0.001
for all
3
spectral distributions in
Equation

6.3
.

Figure 7
:
Overlaps achieved by
5
algorithms on the Poisson regression model
Equation

6.5
. The same experiments are repeated for
3
spectral distributions given in
Equation

6.3
of the design matrix
X
.

6.2
Real data

For the phase retrieval model
Equation

6.4
, we also carry out numerical experiments in which the design matrices and/or regression coefficients arise from real data.
In all such experiments, spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP in
Equation

4.36
achieves the best performance against
4
other competitors (
Figures

2
and
1
).
Unlike Bayes-GVAMP, methods such as GAMP
[
87
,
72
]
that are developed under i.i.d. assumptions do not adapt well to the data distribution.
Moreover, the theoretical guarantees in
Theorems

4.1
and
4.4
respectively for the spectral estimator in
Theorem

4.2
and Bayes-GVAMP initialized with it remain accurate for real data that do not satisfy the distributional assumptions in
Section

3.2
.
This suggests both the flexibility of the orthogonal invariance assumption and the universality of our theory with respect to the data distribution.

Coded diffraction patterns.

Coded diffraction patterns (CDP) are a popular ensemble of designs that capture the physical measurements in phase retrieval.
We consider the real analogue of binary and ternary complex CDP defined in
[
17
, Section 2.2]
.
More details are given in
Section

J.3
.
The vector of regression coefficients is obtained by vectorizing, centering and normalizing the
3
channels (R, G, B) of a
d=32\times 32
image of a truck from the CIFAR-10 dataset
[
55
]
.
In both Bayes-GVAMP
Equation

4.36
and GAMP
[
72
]
, the vector denoisers are constructed assuming an i.i.d. Gaussian prior.
The numerical results are shown in
Figure

2
.
Each data point in the plot is obtained by taking the average of
15
trials with
5
trials for each of the R, G, B channels.

GTEx.

We consider two GTEx datasets ‘Skin - Sun Exposed (Lower leg)’ and ‘Muscle - Skeletal’ (‘skin’ and ‘muscle’ in respective plot titles)
[
58
]
.
Upon standard preprocessing (see
Section

J.3
for details), they become matrices of sizes
6461\times 701
and
7036\times 803
respectively.
The design matrix
X
is obtained by taking the first
n
rows of these matrices.
For Bayes-GVAMP, expectations involving
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}
are replaced with averages with respect to the corresponding empirical spectral distribution of
X
.
The regression coefficients are generated i.i.d. according to
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}={\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
The numerical results are shown in
Figure

1
where each data point is obtained by averaging over
100
i.i.d. trials.

7
Discussion

Estimation of link function and prior distribution.

Our general results for spectral estimators (
Theorem

4.1
) and GVAMP (
Theorem

4.3
) do not assume knowledge of the link function
q
or prior distribution
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}
.
However, the optimal choices of preprocessing functions for spectral estimators (
Theorem

4.2
) and denoisers for GVAMP (
Theorem

4.4
) do require such knowledge.
When
q
and
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}
are parametrized by a fixed (relative to
n,d
) number of parameters, it is possible to consistently estimate them in the proportional regime (
Item

(A4)
) by e.g. moment methods.
Then all our results continue to hold with these consistent estimates plugged in.
In more general (semiparametric or nonparametric) scenarios, nuisance functionals such as
q
and
P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}
are no longer expected to be consistently estimable.
Under both prior and link misspecification, the degradation of the posterior mean squared error is quantified in
[
52
,
95
]
using the non-rigorous replica method.
Empirical Bayes methods
[
89
,
76
,
44
]
have been proposed and analyzed for several Bayesian statistical models with i.i.d. design.
We believe it will be fruitful to transfer similar ideas to our setting of orthogonally invariant GLM.
A common drawback of these methods is that they require either sufficiently large sample size or sufficiently low signal-to-noise ratio.
Developing data-driven estimation and debiasing procedures

[
108
,
57
,
23
,
24
]
that retain the optimality of their oracle counterparts established in the present paper is a promising direction for future work.

Figure 8
:
Statistical-computational tradeoffs in phase retrieval under the standard Gaussian design whose spectral distribution is given by the Marchenko–Pastur law.
The left panel shows
4
curves obtained in the same way as in
Figure

5
with an additional one corresponding to the asymptotic overlap achieved by the spectral estimator given by
[
60
, Equation (20)]
.
The right panel plots the function
{\mathcal{F}}
at
\delta=1.01
.

Statistical and computational optimality.

For a generic orthogonally invariant GLM, our spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP stands as the state-of-the-art efficient estimator.
Its performance matches the conjectured Bayes risk in some regimes while leaving gaps in other regimes, notably when the state evolution equations
have multiple fixed points (see the discussion following
Theorem

4.6
).
It has been conjectured
[
65
,
66
]
that Bayes-GVAMP is optimal among all polynomial-time algorithms.
However, we caution that the mere existence of multiple state evolution fixed points and the discrepancy between Bayes risk and the performance of spectrally initialized Bayes-GVAMP (see
Figure

8
) do not necessarily entail a fundamental gap between statistical and computational limits.
For instance, for noiseless phase retrieval
Equation

6.4
with an i.i.d. Gaussian design, there exist efficient algorithms
[
1
,
93
]
achieving overlap
1
(i.e., perfect recovery) as long as
n\geq d+1
(in particular,
\delta\geq 1
).
In contrast, Bayes-GVAMP or GAMP requires
\delta\gtrsim 1.13
to the same end.
Understanding when and why Bayes-GVAMP is computationally optimal is an interesting avenue for future research.

Appendix A
Free cumulants and moments

For a matrix
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
, denote by
m_{2k}
the
k
-th (where
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
) moment of the empirical spectral distribution of
XX^{\top}
.
The
k
-th moment of the empirical spectral distribution of
X^{\top}X
is then given by

\displaystyle m^{\prime}_{2k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1,&k=0\\
\widehat{\delta}m_{2k},&k\geq 1\end{cases},

where
\widehat{\delta}=n/d
.
Denote by
(\kappa_{2k})_{k\geq 1}
the rectangular free cumulants of
XX^{\top}
; see, e.g.,
[
46
, Section 2.4]
for the precise definition.
The first four of them are explicitly given by:

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}

\displaystyle=m_{2}=\frac{m_{2}^{\prime}}{\widehat{\delta}},

(A.1a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}

\displaystyle=m_{4}-(1+\widehat{\delta})m_{2}^{2}=\frac{m_{4}^{\prime}}{\widehat{\delta}}-\frac{1+\widehat{\delta}}{\widehat{\delta}^{2}}m_{2}^{\prime 2},

(A.1b)

\displaystyle\kappa_{6}

\displaystyle=m_{6}-(3+3\widehat{\delta})m_{4}m_{2}+(2+3\widehat{\delta}+2\widehat{\delta}^{2})m_{2}^{3}=\frac{m_{6}^{\prime}}{\widehat{\delta}}-\frac{3+3\widehat{\delta}}{\widehat{\delta}^{2}}m_{4}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}+\frac{2+3\widehat{\delta}+2\widehat{\delta}^{2}}{\widehat{\delta}^{3}}m_{2}^{\prime 3},

(A.1c)

\displaystyle\kappa_{8}

\displaystyle=m_{8}-(4+4\widehat{\delta})m_{6}m_{2}-(2+2\widehat{\delta})m_{4}^{2}+(10+16\widehat{\delta}+10\widehat{\delta}^{2})m_{4}m_{2}^{2}-(5+10\widehat{\delta}+10\widehat{\delta}^{2}+5\widehat{\delta}^{3})m_{2}^{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{8}^{\prime}}{\widehat{\delta}}-\frac{4+4\widehat{\delta}}{\widehat{\delta}^{2}}m_{6}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime}-\frac{2+2\widehat{\delta}}{\widehat{\delta}^{2}}m_{4}^{\prime 2}+\frac{10+16\widehat{\delta}+10\widehat{\delta}^{2}}{\widehat{\delta}^{3}}m_{4}^{\prime}m_{2}^{\prime 2}-\frac{5+10\widehat{\delta}+10\widehat{\delta}^{2}+5\widehat{\delta}^{3}}{\widehat{\delta}^{4}}m_{2}^{\prime 4}.

(A.1d)

The rectangular free cumulants of
X^{\top}X
are denoted by
(\kappa_{2k}^{\prime})_{k\geq 1}
and satisfy
\kappa_{2k}^{\prime}=\widehat{\delta}\kappa_{2k}
.

If the empirical spectral distributions of
XX^{\top}
and
X^{\top}X
converge in the sense of
Item

(A2)
, then
m_{2k},m_{2k}^{\prime}
and
\kappa_{2k},\kappa_{2k}^{\prime}
also converge.
We denote their limits by
\overline{m}_{2k},\overline{m}_{2k}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\kappa}_{2k},\overline{\kappa}_{2k}^{\prime}
.
Due to the one-to-one correspondence
\overline{\kappa}_{2k}=\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2k}^{\prime}
, we will always refer to
\overline{\kappa}_{2k}
.

Appendix B
Proof of
Theorem

4.1

As signposted in
Section

5
, let us start by considering the following linearized GVAMP iteration: for
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle v^{t}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}X^{\top}g^{t},

(B.1a)

\displaystyle u^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}XX^{\top}-I_{n}\right)g^{t},

\displaystyle g^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\overline{G}u^{t},

(B.1b)

where
\overline{G}=\diag(\overline{g}(y))\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
for any function
\overline{g}
subject to
Item

(A5)
.
Suppose that the initializer
g^{1}
is given by

\displaystyle g^{1}

\displaystyle=\overline{G}(\nu_{0}z+\tau_{0}n^{0}),

(B.2)

where
n^{0}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{n},I_{n})
is independent of everything else and
\nu_{0},\tau_{0}
are defined as

\displaystyle\nu_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rho+w_{2}}},

\displaystyle\tau_{0}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{w_{1}}{\rho+w_{2}}}.

(B.3)

Lemma

B.1
below (proved in
Appendix

C
) shows that under the initialization scheme
Equation

B.2
, several state evolution parameters of
Equation

B.1
stay at their fixed points across all iterates.

Lemma B.1

(Stationary state evolution parameters)
.

Consider the linearized GVAMP in
Equation

B.1
initialized with
Equation

B.2
.
Let
Equation

4.13
hold.
Then for every
t\geq 1
, there exist
\chi_{t},\nu_{t}\neq 0
and
\omega_{t},\tau_{t}>0
such that

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}v^{t}&\beta_{*}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\chi_{t}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+\omega_{t}{\mathsf{M}}_{t}&{\mathsf{B}}_{*}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}u^{t}&z&\varepsilon\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\nu_{t}{\mathsf{Z}}+\tau_{t}{\mathsf{N}}_{t}&{\mathsf{Z}}&{\mathsf{E}}\end{bmatrix},

(B.4)

where
({\mathsf{B}}_{*},{\mathsf{M}}_{t})\sim P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
and
({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{M}}_{t})\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2})\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t}^{2}}{\nu_{t}^{2}}

\displaystyle=w_{1},

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{t}^{2}}{\chi_{t}^{2}}

\displaystyle=w_{2},

\displaystyle\frac{\nu_{t}}{\chi_{t}}

\displaystyle=w_{3},

\displaystyle\frac{\chi_{t+1}}{\nu_{t}}

\displaystyle=w_{4},

\displaystyle\frac{\nu_{t}}{\nu_{t-1}}

\displaystyle=\gamma,

(B.5)

where the right-hand sides of all equalities above are positive.

From
Lemma

B.1
, let us highlight a somewhat unusual feature of the linearized GVAMP algorithm in
Equation

B.1
.
Though the state evolution parameters are initialized with positive finite values in
Equation

B.3
, they may not have a positive finite limit as
t\to\infty
.
According to
Equation

B.5
, the state evolution parameters form a geometric series indexed by
t
of rate
\gamma
and may converge to
0
or
\infty
as
t\to\infty
unless
\gamma=1
.
This implies, for instance, that as
t\to\infty
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-1}\left\|u^{t+1}\right\|_{2}^{2}
may not converge to a positive finite value and
\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-1}\left\|u^{t+1}-u^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}
may not converge to
0
.
However,
Lemma

B.1
shows that certain ratios of the state evolution parameters do have nontrivial limits.
This can be used to show that as
t\to\infty
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|u^{t+1}\right\|_{2}/\left\|u^{t}\right\|_{2}
converges to
\gamma
(see
Equation

E.5
) and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\left\|u^{t+1}\right\|_{2}^{-1}u^{t+1}-\left\|u^{t}\right\|_{2}^{-1}u^{t}\right\|_{2}
converges to
0
(see
Equation

E.23
).
Identifying such ratios whose fixed points (in the large
t
limit) exist is a critical step towards proving the alignment between
v^{t+1}
and
v_{1}(D)
in
Lemma

B.3
.

Lemma B.2

(Right edge of the bulk)
.

Consider the spectral estimator defined through
Equation

4.1
using a preprocessing function satisfying
Item

(A5)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{2}(D)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{\circ},

where
\lambda^{\circ}
on the right-hand side is defined in
Equation

4.9
.

Lemma

B.2
is proved in
Appendix

D
.

The next lemma shows that
v^{t}
is aligned with the top eigenvector of
D
for sufficiently large
t
and
d
.
The proof is presented in
Appendix

E
.

Lemma B.3
.

Consider the linearized GVAMP in
Equation

B.1
initialized with
Equation

B.2
.
Let
Equation

4.13
hold.
Then almost surely,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left|\left\langle v^{t+1},v_{1}(D)\right\rangle\right|}{\left\|v^{t+1}\right\|_{2}}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(D)

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2},

where
D
is defined in
Equation

4.1a
.

Now,
Theorem

4.1
follows easily from the preceding
3
lemmas.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

The characterizations in
Equations

4.12
and
4.14
of the first and second largest eigenvalues of
D
have been obtained in
Lemmas

B.2
and
B.3
.
It remains to compute the performance of the spectral estimator.
Due to
Lemma

B.3
, it suffices to compute the normalized squared overlap between
\beta_{*}
and
v^{t}
. Using
Lemma

B.1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left|\left\langle\beta_{*},v_{1}(D)\right\rangle\right|^{2}}{\left\|\beta_{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\left|\left\langle\beta_{*},v^{t}\right\rangle\right|^{2}}{\left\|\beta_{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|v^{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\chi_{t}^{2}\rho}{\chi_{t}^{2}\rho+\omega_{t}^{2}}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\rho}{\rho+\frac{\omega_{t}^{2}}{\chi_{t}^{2}}}=\frac{\rho}{\rho+w_{2}}=\eta,

where the last two equalities follow from the definitions of
\eta,w_{2}
in
Equations

C.8
and
4.11
and elementary calculations.
Note that
\eta\in(0,1)
by
w_{2}\in(0,\infty)
from
Lemma

B.1
.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of
Lemma

B.1

Consider the following iteration that is slightly more general than
Equation

B.1
.
For
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle v^{t}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}X^{\top}g^{t},

(C.1a)

\displaystyle u^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}XX^{\top}-I_{n}\right)g^{t},

\displaystyle g^{t+1}

\displaystyle=g_{t+1}(u^{t};z,\varepsilon),

(C.1b)

initialized with
g^{1}=g_{1}(z,\varepsilon)
.

Equation

C.1
can be cast as an instance of GVAMP in
Equation

4.19
by setting

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}x,

\displaystyle\Psi_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}x-1,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=0,

(C.2a)

and making the change of variables

\displaystyle r^{t+1}

\displaystyle=v^{t},

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=0_{d},

\displaystyle p^{t+1}

\displaystyle=u^{t},

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=g^{t+1}.

(C.2b)

Let us define the state evolution recursion for
Equation

C.1
.

Define random variables: for
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}&{\mathsf{W}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{W}}_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}

\displaystyle\sim P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0_{t},\Omega_{t}),

(C.3a)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{B}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{B}}_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\chi_{1}&\cdots&\chi_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{W}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{W}}_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{\top},

(C.3b)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{E}}&{\mathsf{Z}}&{\mathsf{Z}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{Z}}_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}

\displaystyle\sim P_{{\mathsf{E}}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0_{t},\Sigma_{t+1}).

(C.3c)

The state evolution parameters
\Omega_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t\times t}
,
\chi_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\Sigma_{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(t+1)\times(t+1)}
in the above display are defined as follows.
We first recursively define semi-infinite arrays
(\Omega_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}}
,
(\Sigma_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}}
, and
(\chi_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}}
initialized with

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2},

\displaystyle\Omega_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\delta\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2},

\displaystyle\chi_{1}

\displaystyle=\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right].

(C.3d)

Here and below, we use
\partial_{z}g_{t}(z_{t-1};z,e)
to denote the partial derivative of
g_{t}
with respect to its second argument.
For any
t\geq 1
,
1\leq i\leq t
and
0\leq j\leq t
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t+1,1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\rho\chi_{t}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

(C.3e)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t+1,i+1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\left(\chi_{t}\chi_{i}\rho+\Omega_{t,i}\right)+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\rho\chi_{i}+\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{i}({\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\rho\chi_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})g_{i}({\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{i}({\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

(C.3f)

\displaystyle\Omega_{t+1,j+1}

\displaystyle=\delta\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})g_{j+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{j};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{j+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{j};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\right),

(C.3g)

\displaystyle\chi_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

(C.3h)

and
\Sigma_{1,t+1}=\Sigma_{t+1,1},\Sigma_{i+1,t+1}=\Sigma_{t+1,i+1},\Omega_{j+1,t+1}=\Omega_{t+1,j+1}
.

Finally,
\Omega_{t}=(\Omega_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in[t]^{2}}
and
\Sigma_{t+1}=(\Sigma_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in[t+1]^{2}}
are obtained by taking the leading principal minors of the above semi-infinite arrays.

The validity of the state evolution result,
Lemma

C.1
below, crucially relies on the divergence-free property of the denoisers
(g_{t+1})_{t\geq 1}
.

(A11)

For every
t\geq 0
,
g_{t}
is Lipschitz in all of its arguments.
For every
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t+1}^{\prime}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]

\displaystyle=0,

(C.4)

where the distribution of
({\mathsf{Z}}_{t},{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})
is specified in
Equation

C.3
.
Here and below,
g_{t+1}^{\prime}(z_{t};z,e)
denotes the partial derivative of
g_{t+1}
with respect to its first argument.
Moreover,
g_{t+1}^{\prime}(z_{t};z,\varepsilon)
and
\partial_{z}g_{t+1}(z_{t};z,\varepsilon)
are continuous with probability
1
with respect to the law of
({\mathsf{Z}}_{t},{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})
.

Lemma C.1

(State evolution)
.

Consider the linearized GVAMP algorithm in
Equation

C.1
and let
Item

(A11)
hold.
For any
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{*}&v^{1}&\cdots&v^{t}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}&{\mathsf{B}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{B}}_{t}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\varepsilon&z&u^{1}&\cdots&u^{t}&g^{1}&g^{2}&\cdots&g^{t+1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{E}}&{\mathsf{Z}}&{\mathsf{Z}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{Z}}_{t}&g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})&g_{2}({\mathsf{Z}}_{1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})&\cdots&g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\end{bmatrix},

where the random variables on the right-hand sides are defined in
Equation

C.3
.

Lemma

C.1
is proved in
Section

C.1
.

Next, we present an equivalent representation of the random variables
{\mathsf{B}}_{t},{\mathsf{Z}}_{t}
; see
Section

C.2
for a proof.

Lemma C.2

(Alternative state evolution)
.

The random variables
({\mathsf{B}}_{i})_{i\in[t]},({\mathsf{Z}}_{i})_{i\in[t]}
defined in
Equation

C.3
can be equivalently written as:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{B}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\chi_{i}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+\omega_{i}{\mathsf{M}}_{i},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Z}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\nu_{i}{\mathsf{Z}}+\tau_{i}{\mathsf{N}}_{i},

\displaystyle\forall i\in[t].

In the above display,
(\omega_{i}{\mathsf{M}}_{i})_{i\in[t]}
is a centered Gaussian vector independent of
{\mathsf{B}}_{*}\sim P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}
with
{\mathsf{M}}_{i}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1),\forall i\in[t]
;

(\tau_{i}{\mathsf{N}}_{i})_{i\in[t]}
is a centered Gaussian vector independent of
{\mathsf{Z}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2})
with
{\mathsf{N}}_{i}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1),\forall i\in[t]
.
The coefficients
(\omega_{i})_{i\in[t]},(\tau_{i})_{i\in[t]}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{t}
and the correlations
(\mathbb{E}\left[\omega_{r}{\mathsf{M}}_{r}\cdot\omega_{s}{\mathsf{M}}_{s}\right])_{(r,s)\in[t]^{2}}
,
(\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{r}{\mathsf{N}}_{r}\cdot\nu_{s}{\mathsf{N}}_{s}\right])_{(r,s)\in[t]^{2}}
follow the recursions below.
For
t=1
,

\displaystyle\chi_{1}

\displaystyle=\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

\displaystyle\omega_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\delta}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(C.5)

For
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=\chi_{t}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\omega_{t}^{2}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}\right)\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2},

\displaystyle\chi_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

\displaystyle\omega_{t+1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}.

For
r,s\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{r}{\mathsf{N}}_{r}\cdot\tau_{s}{\mathsf{N}}_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\omega_{r}{\mathsf{M}}_{r}\cdot\omega_{s}{\mathsf{M}}_{s}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}\right)\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\omega_{r}{\mathsf{M}}_{r}\cdot\omega_{s}{\mathsf{M}}_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=\delta\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\right).

As suggested by the heuristics in
Section

5
, we will consider
g_{t+1}
of the following form which satisfies
Item

(A11)
.

(A12)

The function
g_{t+1}
in
Equation

C.1
takes the form

\displaystyle g_{t+1}(u^{t};z,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\overline{g}_{t+1}(q(z,\varepsilon))u^{t}

(C.6a)

for some function
\overline{g}_{t+1}
satisfying:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle>0.

(C.6b)

Specializing
Lemma

C.1
to this case, we have the following
Lemma

C.3
whose proof can be found in
Section

C.3
.

Lemma C.3

(State evolution, signal-to-noise ratios)
.

Consider the linearized GVAMP in
Equation

C.1
.
Let
Item

(A12)
and
Equation

4.13
hold.
If
\chi_{1}\neq 0
, then
\nu_{t},\chi_{t+1}\neq 0
and
\tau_{t},\omega_{t}>0
for every
t\geq 1
.
Furthermore, the following recursions can be derived from the state evolution in
Equation

C.3
.
For any
t\geq 1
,
1\leq i\leq t
and
0\leq j\leq t
,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t}^{2}}{\nu_{t}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]}{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}\frac{\tau_{t-1}^{2}}{\nu_{t-1}^{2}}+\frac{\rho\left\{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}+\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\right\}}{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}.

(C.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{t+1}^{2}}{\chi_{t+1}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]}{\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}\frac{\tau_{t}^{2}}{\nu_{t}^{2}}+\frac{\rho\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\right\}}{\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}},

(C.8)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t+1,i+1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\left(\chi_{t}\chi_{i}\rho+\Omega_{t,i}\right)+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\rho\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t-1}\chi_{i}+\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{i-1}\chi_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{t-1}{\mathsf{N}}_{t-1}\cdot\tau_{i-1}{\mathsf{N}}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\right)\rho\nu_{t-1}\nu_{i-1},

(C.9)

\displaystyle\Omega_{t+1,j+1}

\displaystyle=\delta\left\{\rho\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\right)\nu_{t}\nu_{j}+\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{t}{\mathsf{N}}_{t}\cdot\tau_{j}{\mathsf{N}}_{j}\right]\right\}.

(C.10)

If we further choose
\overline{g}_{t+1}=\overline{g}
to be time-invariant, then the state evolution has the following fixed points.
See
Section

C.4
for a proof.

Lemma C.4

(State evolution fixed points)
.

Take
g_{t+1}
of the form
Equation

C.6a
with
\overline{g}_{t+1}=\overline{g}
satisfying
Equation

C.6b
.
Denote the fixed points of the state evolution parameters in
Equations

C.7
and
C.8
by the same notation with time indices removed.
Assuming that (
4.13
) holds,
the fixed points satisfy:

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\tau}^{2}}{\overline{\nu}^{2}}

\displaystyle=w_{1}>0,

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\omega}^{2}}{\overline{\chi}^{2}}

\displaystyle=w_{2}>0.

Finally, the above ingredients allow us to prove
Lemma

B.1
in
Section

C.5
.

C.1
Proof of
Lemma

C.1

According to the specialization in
Equation

C.2
, the linearized GVAMP in
Equation

C.1
is an instance of the generic GVAMP in
Equation

4.19
.
Therefore its state evolution
Equation

C.3
can be derived by specializing
Equation

4.29
.
However, we will derive its state evolution from the following Rotationally Invariant Generalized Approximate Message Passing (RI-GAMP) algorithm studied in
[
VKM
, Equations (4) and (5)]
.
This is for the technical convenience of making the appearance of free cumulants in
Equation

C.3
more transparent.

The RI-GAMP iteration reads: for
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle b^{t}

\displaystyle=X^{\top}h^{t}-\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}\beta_{t,i}f^{i},

\displaystyle f^{t}

\displaystyle=f_{t}(b^{1},\cdots,b^{t}),

(C.11a)

\displaystyle z^{t}

\displaystyle=Xf^{t}-\sum_{i=1}^{t}\alpha_{t,i}h^{i},

\displaystyle h^{t+1}

\displaystyle=h_{t+1}(z^{1},\cdots,z^{t};z,\varepsilon),

(C.11b)

initialized with
h^{1}=h_{1}(z,\varepsilon)
.

Proposition C.5

(
[
VKM
, Theorem 1]
)
.

For any fixed
t\geq 1
, there exist random variables
{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}},({\mathsf{B}}_{i})_{i\in[t]},({\mathsf{Z}}_{i})_{i\in[t]}
(defined in
[
VKM
, Equations (11) to (22)]
) such that if the following conditions hold

(A13)

for every
1\leq i\leq t
,
f_{i}(b_{1},\cdots,b_{i})
and
h_{i}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{i-1};z,\varepsilon)
are Lipschitz in each of their arguments;

(A14)

for every
1\leq i\leq t
,
1\leq j\leq i
and
1\leq k\leq i-1
, the partial derivatives
\partial_{x_{j}}f_{i}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i})
,
\partial_{z_{k}}h_{i}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{i-1},z,\varepsilon)
and
\partial_{z}h_{i}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{i-1},z,\varepsilon)
are all continuous except on a measure
0
set with respect to the joint law of
{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}},({\mathsf{B}}_{i})_{i\in[t]},({\mathsf{Z}}_{i})_{i\in[t]}
,

then

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{*}&v^{1}&\cdots&v^{t}&f^{1}&\cdots&f^{t}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}&{\mathsf{B}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{B}}_{t}&{\mathsf{F}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{F}}_{t}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\varepsilon&z&u^{1}&\cdots&u^{t}&h^{1}&\cdots&h^{t}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{W_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{E}}&{\mathsf{Z}}&{\mathsf{Z}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{Z}}_{t}&{\mathsf{H}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{H}}_{t}\end{bmatrix},

where
{\mathsf{F}}_{i}\coloneqq f_{i}({\mathsf{B}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathsf{B}}_{i})
and
{\mathsf{H}}_{i}\coloneqq h_{i}({\mathsf{Z}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})
.

Consider a special case of the above iteration where

\displaystyle f_{t}(b^{t})

\displaystyle=b^{t},

\displaystyle h_{t+1}(z^{t};z,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}g_{t+1}(z^{t};z,\varepsilon),

\displaystyle h_{1}(z,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}g_{1}(z,\varepsilon).

(C.12)

Item

(A11)
guarantees that functions in
Equation

C.12
satisfy
Items

(A13)
and
(A14)
.
We can write
Equation

C.11
as

\displaystyle b^{t}

\displaystyle=X^{\top}h^{t}-\varepsilon^{t},

(C.13a)

\displaystyle z^{t}

\displaystyle=Xb^{t}-\overline{\kappa}_{2}h^{t}-\delta^{t},

\displaystyle h^{t+1}

\displaystyle=h_{t+1}(z^{t};z,\varepsilon),

(C.13b)

where

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}\beta_{t,i}b^{i},

\displaystyle\delta^{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}\alpha_{t,i}h^{i}+\left(\alpha_{t,t}-\overline{\kappa}_{2}\right)h^{t}.

Using the choice of
h_{t+1}
in
Equation

C.12
and combining
Equations

C.13a
and
C.13b
, we have

\displaystyle b^{t}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}X^{\top}g^{t}-\varepsilon^{t},

\displaystyle z^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(XX^{\top}-\overline{\kappa}_{2}I_{n}\right)h^{t}-X\varepsilon^{t}-\delta^{t}

\displaystyle g^{t+1}

\displaystyle=g_{t+1}(z^{t};z,\varepsilon).

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}XX^{\top}-I_{n}\right)g^{t}-X\varepsilon^{t}-\delta^{t},

Comparing the above iteration with
Equation

C.1
, we recognize that

\displaystyle v^{t}

\displaystyle=b^{t}+\varepsilon^{t},

\displaystyle u^{t}

\displaystyle=z^{t}+X\varepsilon^{t}+\delta^{t}.

(C.14)

Furthermore, evaluating the debiasing coefficients
(\alpha_{t,i})_{i\in[t]},(\beta_{t,i})_{i\in[t-1]}
according to
[
VKM
, Equations (6) to (10)]
, we have,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{t,t}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2},

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{t,i}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\beta_{t,i}=0,

\displaystyle\forall i\in[t-1].

This step crucially requires the divergence-free property of
g_{t+1}
in
Item

(A11)
which causes the coefficients of all memory terms in
Equation

C.11
to vanish and therefore makes the resulting state evolution recursion more tractable.
Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|X\right\|_{2}<\infty
by
Item

(A2)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-1/2}\left\|X\varepsilon^{t}\right\|_{2}=\lim_{d\to\infty}d^{-1/2}\left\|\varepsilon^{t}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-1/2}\left\|\delta^{t}\right\|_{2}=0,

(C.15)

hence it also holds that
\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-1/2}\left\|X\varepsilon^{t}+\delta^{t}\right\|_{2}=0
.
We have shown that the iteration
Equation

C.1
can be cast as an instance of
Equation

C.11
up to vanishing perturbations in the iterates
b^{t},z^{t}
.
We claim that the same state evolution result in
[
VKM
, Theorem 1]
applies to both iterations
Equations

C.1
and
C.11
.
To see this, take any test function
f\in\mathrm{PL}^{2}_{(t+1)\to 1}
.
Then there exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}f(\beta_{*,i},v^{1}_{i},\cdots,v^{t}_{i})-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}f(\beta_{*,i},b^{1}_{i},\cdots,b^{t}_{i})\right|\leq\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left|f(\beta_{*,i},v^{1}_{i},\cdots,v^{t}_{i})-f(\beta_{*,i},b^{1}_{i},\cdots,b^{t}_{i})\right|

(C.16)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|(v^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}-(b^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}\left(1+\left\|(v^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}+\left\|(b^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}\right)

(C.17)

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|(\varepsilon^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}\left(1+\left\|(v^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}+\left\|(b^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}\right)

(C.18)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{d}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|(\varepsilon_{i}^{j})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(1+\left\|(v^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}+\left\|(b^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}\right)^{2}}

(C.19)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{3}C}{d}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|(\varepsilon_{i}^{j})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(1+\left\|(v^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|(b^{j}_{i})_{j\in[t]}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)}

(C.20)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{3}C\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{t}d^{-1}\left\|\varepsilon^{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\sqrt{1+\sum_{j=1}^{t}d^{-1}\left\|v^{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{t}d^{-1}\left\|b^{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{3}C\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{t}d^{-1}\left\|\varepsilon^{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}\sqrt{1+2\sum_{j=1}^{t}d^{-1}\left\|\varepsilon^{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}+3\sum_{j=1}^{t}d^{-1}\left\|b^{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}}.

(C.21)

In the above chain of (in)equalities,

Equation

C.17
follows from the
\mathrm{PL}^{2}
property of
f
(see
Definition

3.1
);

Equation

C.18
is by
Equation

C.14
;

Equations

C.16
and
C.19
are both by Cauchy–Schwarz;

Equation

C.20
uses the elementary inequality
(a+b+c)^{2}\leq 3(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})
for any
a,b,c\in{\mathbb{R}}
;

Equation

C.21
follows since
\left\|u+v\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2(\left\|u\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|v\right\|_{2}^{2})
.
The state evolution result
[
VKM
, Theorem 1]
applies to
(b^{j})_{j\in[t]}
and implies in particular
\lim_{d\to\infty}d^{-1/2}\left\|b^{j}\right\|_{2}<\infty
for every
j\in[t]
.
Since
t
is fixed, this combined with
Equation

C.15
shows

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\left|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}f(\beta_{*,i},v^{1}_{i},\cdots,v^{t}_{i})-\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}f(\beta_{*,i},b^{1}_{i},\cdots,b^{t}_{i})\right|

\displaystyle=0.

A similar argument shows that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(\varepsilon_{i},z_{i},u^{1}_{i},\cdots,u^{t}_{i},g^{1}_{i},\cdots,g^{t}_{i})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(\varepsilon_{i},z_{i},z^{1}_{i},\cdots,z^{t}_{i},\overline{\kappa}_{2}h^{1}_{i},\cdots,\overline{\kappa}_{2}h^{t}_{i})\right|

\displaystyle=0,

for any
g\in\mathrm{PL}^{2}_{2(t+1)\to 1}
.
Therefore, the Wasserstein-
2
limits of
\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{*}&v^{1}&\cdots&v^{t}\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}\varepsilon&z&u^{1}&\cdots&u^{t}&g^{1}&\cdots g^{t}\end{bmatrix}
are the same as those of
\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{*}&b^{1}&\cdots&b^{t}\end{bmatrix}
and
\begin{bmatrix}\varepsilon&z&z^{1}&\cdots&z^{t}&\overline{\kappa}_{2}h^{1}&\cdots&\overline{\kappa}_{2}h^{t}\end{bmatrix}
, respectively, to the latter of which the state evolution result
[
VKM
, Theorem 1]
applies.

Next, we specialize the state evolution recursion in
[
VKM
, Equations (11) to (22)]
to
Equation

C.13
which is also valid for
Equation

C.1
.
Define random variables:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{E}}&{\mathsf{Z}}&{\mathsf{Z}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\sim P_{{\mathsf{E}}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0_{t},\Sigma_{t}),

(C.22a)

\displaystyle\,\widetilde{{\mathsf{Z}}}_{t}=h_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}}),

(C.22b)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}&{\mathsf{W}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{W}}_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\sim P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0_{t},\Omega_{t}),

(C.22c)

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{B}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{B}}_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}\chi_{1}&\cdots&\chi_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+\begin{bmatrix}{\mathsf{W}}_{1}&\cdots&{\mathsf{W}}_{t}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}.

(C.22d)

The state evolution parameters
\Sigma_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t\times t},\Omega_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t\times t},\chi_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}
are defined recursively as follows.
For
t=1
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2},

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}

\displaystyle=\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[h_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\delta\overline{\kappa}_{4}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2},

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

(C.22e)

where
({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\sim{\mathcal{N}}\left(0,\sigma^{2}\right)\otimes P_{{\mathsf{E}}}
.

For
t\geq 1
, we now describe how
\Sigma_{t+1},\Omega_{t+1},\chi_{t+1}
can be computed from
\Sigma_{t},\Omega_{t},(\chi_{i})_{i\in[t]}
.
Define

\displaystyle\phi_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]&\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{2}({\mathsf{Z}}_{1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]&\cdots&\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t},

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{B}}_{*}{\mathsf{B}}_{1}\right]&\cdots&\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{B}}_{*}{\mathsf{B}}_{t}\right]\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{B}}_{i}{\mathsf{B}}_{j}\right]\end{bmatrix}_{(i,j)\in[t]^{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t\times t},

\displaystyle\Delta_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{Z}}}_{i}\widetilde{{\mathsf{Z}}}_{j}\right]\end{bmatrix}_{(i,j)\in[t]^{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t\times t}.

Then
\Sigma_{t+1}
is updated as:

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}\rho&\gamma_{t}^{\top}\\
\gamma_{t}&\Gamma_{t}\end{bmatrix}+\overline{\kappa}_{4}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&\rho\phi_{t}^{\top}\\
\rho\phi_{t}&\phi_{t}\gamma_{t}^{\top}+\gamma_{t}\phi_{t}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0&0_{t}^{\top}\\
0_{t}&\Delta_{t}\end{bmatrix}\right)+\overline{\kappa}_{6}\begin{bmatrix}0&0_{t}^{\top}\\
0_{t}&\rho\phi_{t}\phi_{t}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}.

The matrix
\Omega_{t+1}
is updated as:

\displaystyle\Omega_{t+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\delta\left(\overline{\kappa}_{2}\Delta_{t+1}+\overline{\kappa}_{4}\rho\phi_{t+1}\phi_{t+1}^{\top}\right).

The scalar
\chi_{t+1}
is updated as:

\displaystyle\chi_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\phi_{t+1,t+1},

where
\phi_{t+1,t+1}
denotes the last element of the vector
\phi_{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t+1}
.

Note that
\Sigma_{t},\Omega_{t}
are the leading principal minors (of order
t
) of
\Sigma_{t+1},\Omega_{t+1}
, respectively.
So we explicitly write the update rules for the last row and column of
\Sigma_{t+1},\Omega_{t+1}
and
\chi_{t+1}
.
For
1\leq i\leq t
and
0\leq j\leq t
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t+1,1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{B}}_{*}{\mathsf{B}}_{t}\right]+\overline{\kappa}_{4}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\rho\chi_{t}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

(C.22f)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t+1,i+1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{B}}_{t}{\mathsf{B}}_{i}\right]+\overline{\kappa}_{4}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{B}}_{*}{\mathsf{B}}_{i}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{i}({\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{B}}_{*}{\mathsf{B}}_{t}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\overline{\kappa}_{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{Z}}}_{t}\widetilde{{\mathsf{Z}}}_{i}\right]+\overline{\kappa}_{6}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{i}({\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\left(\chi_{t}\chi_{i}\rho+\Omega_{t,i}\right)+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\rho\chi_{i}+\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{i}({\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\rho\chi_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})g_{i}({\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{i}({\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

(C.22g)

\displaystyle\Omega_{t+1,j+1}

\displaystyle=\delta\left(\overline{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{{\mathsf{Z}}}_{t+1}\widetilde{{\mathsf{Z}}}_{j+1}\right]+\overline{\kappa}_{4}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{j+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{j};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\delta\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})g_{j+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{j};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{j+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{j};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\right),

(C.22h)

\displaystyle\chi_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}h_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]=\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

(C.22i)

where we recall the choice of
h_{t}
in
Equation

C.12
.

We conclude the proof by noting that the state evolution recursion
Equation

C.22
is the same as
Equation

C.3
.

C.2
Proof of
Lemma

C.2

According to
Equation

C.3
,
({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{Z}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathsf{Z}}_{t})
is a centered Gaussian vector.
So we can equivalently write

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Z}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\nu_{i}{\mathsf{Z}}+\tau_{i}{\mathsf{N}}_{i},\qquad i\in[t]

where
({\mathsf{N}}_{i})_{i\in[t]}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0_{t},I_{t})
is a standard Gaussian vector independent of
{\mathsf{Z}}
.
The coefficients
(\nu_{i})_{i\in[t]},(\tau_{i})_{i\in[t]}
and the correlations
(\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{r}{\mathsf{N}}_{r}\cdot\tau_{s}{\mathsf{N}}_{s}\right])_{(r,s)\in[t]^{2}}
can be derived from the covariance matrix
\Sigma_{t+1}
.

To this end, we first note that the covariance structure of
({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{Z}}_{t})
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}{\mathsf{Z}}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\rho\chi_{t}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}_{t}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\left(\chi_{t}^{2}\rho+\Omega_{t,t}\right)+2\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\rho\chi_{t}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}.

The coefficients
\nu_{t},\tau_{t}
are therefore given by

\displaystyle\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}{\mathsf{Z}}_{t}\right]}{\sigma^{2}}=\chi_{t}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right],

\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}_{t}^{2}\right]-\nu_{t}^{2}\sigma^{2}=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\left(\chi_{t}^{2}\rho+\Omega_{t,t}\right)+2\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\rho\chi_{t}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\chi_{t}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\right)^{2}\sigma^{2},

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\Omega_{t,t}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}\right)\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}.

(C.23)

Similarly, the correlation
\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{r}{\mathsf{N}}_{r}\cdot\tau_{s}{\mathsf{N}}_{s}\right]
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{r}{\mathsf{N}}_{r}\cdot\tau_{s}{\mathsf{N}}_{s}\right]=\Sigma_{r+1,s+1}-\nu_{r}\nu_{s}\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\left(\chi_{r}\chi_{s}\rho+\Omega_{r,s}\right)+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\rho\chi_{s}+\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\rho\chi_{r}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\chi_{r}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\right)\left(\chi_{s}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\right)\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\Omega_{r,s}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}\right)\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{r}({\mathsf{Z}}_{r-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{s}({\mathsf{Z}}_{s-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right].

Note that for
r=s=t
, the above expression coincides with
\tau_{t}^{2}
in
Equation

C.23
.

Turning to
{\mathsf{B}}_{t+1}
, the representation
{\mathsf{B}}_{t+1}=\chi_{t+1}{\mathsf{B}}_{*}+\omega_{t+1}{\mathsf{M}}_{t+1}
(where
({\mathsf{B}}_{*},{\mathsf{M}}_{t+1})\sim P_{{\mathsf{B}}_{*}}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
) can be obtained by simply redefining
{\mathsf{M}}_{t+1}={\mathsf{W}}_{t+1}/\sqrt{\Omega_{t+1,t+1}}
and
\omega_{t+1}=\sqrt{\Omega_{t+1,t+1}}
where the right-hand sides are given in
Equation

C.3
.

C.3
Proof of
Lemma

C.3

First off, let us use Stein’s lemma and chain rule of derivatives to derive an alternative expression for
\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]
(which is used in
Lemma

C.2
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]-\nu_{t-1}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{t}^{\prime}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right].

When
g_{t}
takes the form of
Equation

C.6
, this identity becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}}){\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1}\right]-\nu_{t-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]=\sigma^{-2}\nu_{t-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}}){\mathsf{Z}}^{2}\right]=\nu_{t-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right],

(C.24)

where we recall
\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}=\sigma^{-1}{\mathsf{Z}}
defined in
Section

3.2
.

Now, specializing
Lemma

C.2
to the case of
Equation

C.6
, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\right)\chi_{t},

(C.25)

\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\omega_{t}^{2}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}{\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}\right)\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\omega_{t}^{2}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\left(\nu_{t-1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}{\mathsf{Z}}^{2}\right]+\tau_{t-1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]\right)+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}\right)\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\nu_{t-1}^{2}

(C.26)

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\omega_{t}^{2}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]\tau_{t-1}^{2}+\rho\left\{\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\right\}\nu_{t-1}^{2},

(C.27)

\displaystyle\chi_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t},

(C.28)

\displaystyle\omega_{t+1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}{\mathsf{Z}}_{t}^{2}\right]+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{t+1}({\mathsf{Z}}_{t};{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\left(\nu_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}{\mathsf{Z}}^{2}\right]+\tau_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]\right)+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\nu_{t}^{2}

(C.29)

\displaystyle=\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]\tau_{t}^{2}+\delta\rho\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\right\}\nu_{t}^{2}.

(C.30)

The equalities
Equations

C.26
,
C.28
and
C.29
all follow from
Equation

C.24
.

By the assumption
\chi_{1}\neq 0
, the recursion
Equation

C.25
and the observation
Equation

4.5
, we have
\nu_{1}\neq 0
.
Eliminating
\chi_{t}
from
Equations

C.25
and
C.28
, we obtain the following recursion for
\nu_{t}
:

\displaystyle\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t-1}.

(C.31)

Using
Equation

4.5
along with
Item

(A12)
in
Equation

C.31
and inducting on
t
, we further obtain
\nu_{t}\neq 0
for every
t\geq 1
.
Using
Item

(A12)
again in
Equation

C.28
, this in turn implies
\chi_{t+1}\neq 0
for every
t\geq 1
.

Next, let us recall the expression of
\omega_{1}
from
Equation

C.5
and make two observations.
Stein’s lemma implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]=\sigma^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right].

(C.32)

Cauchy–Schwarz inequality implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}.

(C.33)

By the above two observations and recalling the definition
Equation

3.9
of
\sigma^{2}
,

\displaystyle\omega_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\delta}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\sigma^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}=\delta\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}\left(1+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\right).

(C.34)

By
\chi_{1}\neq 0
and
Item

(A1)
, the first factor of
Equation

C.34
is positive.
We claim that the second factor surrounded by parentheses in
Equation

C.34
is also positive.
By the moment-cumulant relation (see
Appendix

A
),

\displaystyle 1+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{m}_{4}}{\overline{m}_{2}^{2}}-1-\delta.

By definition,
\overline{m}_{2k}=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2k}\right]
where
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}
is given in
Equation

3.8
.
Therefore

\displaystyle 1+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{4}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}\right]^{2}}-\delta=\begin{cases}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}^{4}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}^{2}\right]^{2}}-\delta,&\delta\leq 1\\
\delta\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}^{4}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}^{2}\right]^{2}}-\delta,&\delta>1\end{cases}.

(C.35)

In both cases, the right-hand side is positive, by Cauchy–Schwarz and
Item

(A2)
.
This confirms the positivity of
\omega_{1}
.

We then show
\tau_{1}^{2}>0
.
By
Lemma

C.2
,

\displaystyle\tau_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\omega_{1}^{2}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}\right)\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\delta+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]+\left(\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}\right)\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2},

(C.36)

where the second step uses the expression of
\omega_{1}
.
Using
Equations

C.32
and
C.33
in
Equation

C.36
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tau_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\delta+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\kappa_{2}^{3}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{4}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]^{2}.

By the moment-cumulant relation (see
Appendix

A
),

\displaystyle\delta+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\kappa_{2}^{3}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\overline{m}_{2}\overline{m}_{6}-\overline{m}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{m}_{2}^{4}}.

By the definition of
\overline{m}_{2k}
(see
Appendix

A
