), the numerator of the right-hand side above is

\displaystyle\overline{m}_{2}\overline{m}_{6}-\overline{m}_{4}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{6}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{4}\right]^{2}>0,

(C.37)

by Hölder’s inequality applied to the non-negative random variable
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}\cdot{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{3}
(see
Item

(A2)
).
Therefore
\tau_{1}^{2}>0
.

Eliminating
\omega_{t}
from
Equations

C.27
and
C.30
, we obtain the following recursion for
\tau_{t}
:

\displaystyle\tau_{t}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]\tau_{t-1}^{2}+\rho\left\{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}+\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\right\}\nu_{t-1}^{2}.

(C.38)

Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle>0

(C.39)

since otherwise by
Item

(A12)
, Cauchy–Schwarz and
\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
,

\displaystyle 0<\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]}\cdot\sqrt{3}=0,

which is a contradiction.
We claim that the multiplicative coefficient in front of
\nu_{t-1}^{2}
on the right of
Equation

C.38
is also positive.
Indeed, by Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right],

(C.40)

which can be used to lower bound the coefficient of interest as

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}+\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}+\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}.

(C.41)

The second factor on the right is positive by
Item

(A12)
and the first factor is also positive, as we have already seen in
Equation

C.37
.
Combining the above results with
Equation

4.5
, recalling the positivity of
(\nu_{t}^{2})_{t\geq 1}
shown below
Equation

C.31
and inducting on
t
in
Equation

C.38
, we conclude the positivity of
\tau_{t}
for every
t\geq 1
.

Moving to
\omega_{t}
, we consider the recursion
Equation

C.30
.
The coefficient in front of
\nu_{t}^{2}
on the right of
Equation

C.30
can be similarly seen positive:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\left(1+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\right)>0,

(C.42)

where we have used
Equations

C.40
and
C.35
.
Combining this with
Equation

C.39
and the positivity of
(\tau_{t}^{2})_{t\geq 1},(\nu_{t}^{2})_{t\geq 1}
just shown and inducting on
t
, we conclude the positivity of
\omega_{t}
for every
t\geq 1
.

Consider the recursions
Equations

C.31
and
C.38
for
\nu_{t},\tau_{t}
.
Dividing both sides of
Equation

C.38
by
\nu_{t}^{2}
and using
Equation

C.31
lead us to a recursion for the ratio
\tau_{t}^{2}/\nu_{t}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{t}^{2}}{\nu_{t}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]\frac{\tau_{t-1}^{2}}{\nu_{t-1}^{2}}\frac{\nu_{t-1}^{2}}{\nu_{t}^{2}}+\rho\left\{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}+\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\right\}\frac{\nu_{t-1}^{2}}{\nu_{t}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]}{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}\frac{\tau_{t-1}^{2}}{\nu_{t-1}^{2}}+\frac{\rho\left\{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}+\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}-\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{3}}+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}\right\}}{\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}+\delta\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}},

matching
Equation

C.7
, as desired.
Similarly, dividing both sides of
Equation

C.30
by
\chi_{t+1}^{2}
and using
Equation

C.28
, we get
Equation

C.8
.

Finally, we also specialize
Equations

C.3e
,
C.3f
and
C.3g
to the case subject to
Item

(A12)
.
Again using
Equation

C.24
, we have: for any
1\leq i\leq t,0\leq j\leq t
,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{t+1,i+1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\left(\chi_{t}\chi_{i}\rho+\Omega_{t,i}\right)+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\rho\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t-1}\chi_{i}+\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{i-1}\chi_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}}){\mathsf{Z}}_{t-1}{\mathsf{Z}}_{i-1}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t-1}\nu_{i-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\left(\chi_{t}\chi_{i}\rho+\Omega_{t,i}\right)+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\rho\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t-1}\chi_{i}+\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{i-1}\chi_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\sigma^{2}\nu_{t-1}\nu_{i-1}+\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{t-1}{\mathsf{N}}_{t-1}\cdot\tau_{i-1}{\mathsf{N}}_{i-1}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t-1}\nu_{i-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\kappa}_{2}\left(\chi_{t}\chi_{i}\rho+\Omega_{t,i}\right)+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\rho\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t-1}\chi_{i}+\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{i-1}\chi_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{t-1}{\mathsf{N}}_{t-1}\cdot\tau_{i-1}{\mathsf{N}}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{6}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{i}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\right)\rho\nu_{t-1}\nu_{i-1},

\displaystyle\Omega_{t+1,j+1}

\displaystyle=\delta\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}}){\mathsf{Z}}_{t}{\mathsf{Z}}_{j}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t}\nu_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\sigma^{2}\nu_{t}\nu_{j}+\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{t}{\mathsf{N}}_{t}\cdot\tau_{j}{\mathsf{N}}_{j}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\nu_{t}\nu_{j}

\displaystyle=\delta\left\{\rho\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]\right)\nu_{t}\nu_{j}+\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}_{t+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{g}_{j+1}({\mathsf{Y}})\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{t}{\mathsf{N}}_{t}\cdot\tau_{j}{\mathsf{N}}_{j}\right]\right\},

as claimed in
Equations

C.9
and
C.10
.

C.4
Proof of
Lemma

C.4

The fixed point equation
Equation

4.22
for
\overline{\tau}^{2}/\overline{\nu}^{2}
follows by removing time indices from
Equation

C.7
and solving for
\overline{\tau}^{2}/\overline{\nu}^{2}
.
The fixed point equation
Equation

4.23
for
\overline{\omega}^{2}/\overline{\chi}^{2}
follows by substituting the expression in
Equation

4.22
for
\overline{\tau}^{2}/\overline{\nu}^{2}
into
Equation

C.8
(with time indices removed) and performing elementary algebraic manipulations.

Next, we show positivity of both
\overline{\tau}^{2}/\overline{\nu}^{2}
and
\overline{\omega}^{2}/\overline{\chi}^{2}
.
First consider
\overline{\tau}^{2}/\overline{\nu}^{2}
.
The denominator of
Equation

4.22
is positive by assumption
Equation

4.13
.
The positivity of the numerator has been shown in
Equation

C.41
.
Therefore,
\overline{\tau}^{2}/\overline{\nu}^{2}>0
.
Using this together with
Item

(A12)
,
Equations

C.39
and
C.42
in
Equation

C.8
, we obtain
\overline{\omega}^{2}/\overline{\chi}^{2}>0
.

C.5
Proof of
Lemma

B.1

The convergence result
Equation

B.4
has been guaranteed by
Lemma

C.1
.
It remains to verify the stationarity of the ratios in
Equation

B.5
.
To this end, first note that by the choice of
\nu_{0},\tau_{0}
in
Equation

B.3
,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{0}^{2}}{\nu_{0}^{2}}

\displaystyle=w_{1}=\frac{\overline{\tau}^{2}}{\overline{\nu}^{2}},

(C.43)

where the last equality is by
Lemma

C.4
.

By the choice
Equation

B.2
of
g_{1}
and Stein’s lemma,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{z}g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{Z}}\cdot\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})(\nu_{0}{\mathsf{Z}}+\tau_{0}{\mathsf{N}}_{0})\right]=\nu_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[g_{1}({\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}(\nu_{0}{\mathsf{Z}}+\tau_{0}{\mathsf{N}}_{0})^{2}\right]=\sigma^{2}\nu_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\tau_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right],

where
{\mathsf{N}}_{0}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
is independent of
{\mathsf{Z}},{\mathsf{E}}
.
Using these formulas, we evaluate
\chi_{1},\omega_{1}
according to
Equation

C.3d
:

\displaystyle\chi_{1}

\displaystyle=\delta\nu_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right],

\displaystyle\omega_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\delta\frac{1}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\left(\sigma^{2}\nu_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\tau_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]\right)+\delta\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\rho\nu_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\delta\rho\nu_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\delta\frac{\tau_{0}^{2}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]+\delta\rho\nu_{0}^{2}\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}.

Since
\nu_{0}
is positive, so is
\chi_{1}
.
Then

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{1}^{2}}{\chi_{1}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho}{\delta}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]}{\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}\frac{\tau_{0}^{2}}{\nu_{0}^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{eqn:tau0/nu0}}}{=}\frac{\rho}{\delta}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]+\frac{\overline{\kappa}_{4}}{\overline{\kappa}_{2}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})^{2}\right]}{\delta\overline{\kappa}_{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})\overline{{\mathsf{Z}}}^{2}\right]^{2}}\frac{\overline{\tau}^{2}}{\overline{\nu}^{2}}\overset{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{eqn:omega2/chi2}}}{=}\frac{\overline{\omega}^{2}}{\overline{\chi}^{2}}=w_{2},

where the last equality is by
Lemma

C.4
.
This implies, by induction on
t
, the first two statements in
Equation

B.5
.

The remaining statements in
Equation

B.5
follow immediately from
Equations

C.25
,
C.28
and
C.31
.

Appendix D
Proof of
Lemma

B.2

Recall
z=X\beta_{*}=O\Lambda Q^{\top}\beta_{*}
, where the second equality is by the SVD of
X
(see
Item

(A2)
).
Let
w\coloneqq\Lambda Q^{\top}\beta_{*}
such that
z=Ow
.
Define

\displaystyle\coloneqq Q\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}T\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda Q^{\top},

(D.1)

where
\widetilde{O}\sim\haar({\mathbb{O}}(n-1))
is independent of everything else.

Lemma D.1
.

It holds almost surely that

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}(\widecheck{D})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{2}(D)\leq\lambda_{1}(\widecheck{D}).

(D.2)

Proof.

We write the matrix
D
as

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=X^{\top}TX=Q\Lambda^{\top}O^{\top}TO\Lambda Q^{\top}.

It suffices to study the eigenvalues of
\Lambda^{\top}O^{\top}TO\Lambda
since conjugation by orthogonal matrices preserves the spectrum.
By
Proposition

K.6
, we have

\displaystyle O\,|\,\left\{Ow=z\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\frac{zw^{\top}}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top},

where
\widetilde{O}\sim\haar({\mathbb{O}}(n-1))
is independent of everything else.
Then

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\top}O^{\top}TO\Lambda\,|\,\left\{Ow=z\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\Lambda^{\top}\left(\frac{wz^{\top}}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}\right)T\left(\frac{zw^{\top}}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\right)\Lambda.

By the Courant–Fischer theorem,

\displaystyle\left.\lambda_{2}\left(\Lambda^{\top}O^{\top}TO\Lambda\right)\,|\,\left\{Ow=z\right\}\right.

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{V}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\\
\dim({\mathcal{V}})=d-1\end{subarray}}\max_{v\in{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}}v^{\top}\left(\frac{\Lambda^{\top}wz^{\top}}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}\right)T\left(\frac{zw^{\top}\Lambda}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda\right)v

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}\\
\left\langle v,\frac{\Lambda^{\top}w}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}}\right\rangle=0\end{subarray}}v^{\top}\left(\frac{\Lambda^{\top}wz^{\top}}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}\right)T\left(\frac{zw^{\top}\Lambda}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda\right)v

\displaystyle\leq\max_{v\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}}v^{\top}\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}T\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda v

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\left(\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}T\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda\right).

This proves the left-most inequality in
Equation

D.2
.

Again by the Courant–Fischer theorem,

\displaystyle\left.\lambda_{2}\left(\Lambda^{\top}O^{\top}TO\Lambda\right)\,|\,\left\{Ow=z\right\}\right.

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{V}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\\
\dim({\mathcal{V}})=d-1\end{subarray}}\max_{v\in{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}}v^{\top}\left(\frac{\Lambda^{\top}wz^{\top}}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}\right)T\left(\frac{zw^{\top}\Lambda}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda\right)v

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}\\
\left\langle v,v_{*}\right\rangle=0\end{subarray}}v^{\top}\left(\frac{\Lambda^{\top}wz^{\top}}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}\right)T\left(\frac{zw^{\top}\Lambda}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda\right)v

\displaystyle\geq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}\\
\left\langle v,v_{*}\right\rangle=0\\
\left\langle v,\frac{\Lambda^{\top}w}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}}\right\rangle=0\end{subarray}}v^{\top}\left(\frac{\Lambda^{\top}wz^{\top}}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}\right)T\left(\frac{zw^{\top}\Lambda}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}^{2}}+\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda\right)v

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}\\
\left\langle v,v_{*}\right\rangle=0\\
\left\langle v,\frac{\Lambda^{\top}w}{\left\|z\right\|_{2}}\right\rangle=0\end{subarray}}v^{\top}\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}T\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda v

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathcal{U}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\\
\dim({\mathcal{U}})=d-2\end{subarray}}\max_{v\in{\mathcal{U}}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}}v^{\top}\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}T\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda v

\displaystyle=\lambda_{3}\left(\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}T\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda\right),

where we identify the minimizing subspace
{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
of dimension
d-1
with its normal vector
v_{*}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
, i.e.,
{\mathcal{V}}_{*}=\left\{v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:\left\langle v,v_{*}\right\rangle=0\right\}
.
This proves the right-most inequality in
Equation

D.2
.
∎

It remains to study the extremal eigenvalues of the matrix
\widecheck{D}
in
Equation

D.1
.
Due to potential interest from a random matrix theory viewpoint, we present in
Sections

D.1
,
D.2
,
D.3
and
D.4
self-contained results for a slightly more general version of
\widecheck{D}
given in
Equation

D.3
.

D.1
Setup

Let
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{(d\wedge n)\times(d\wedge n)}
and
T\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be symmetric matrices such that
\Sigma
is strictly positive definite. Consider the symmetric random matrix

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}\coloneqq\Lambda^{\top}O^{\top}TO\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

where
O\sim\haar(\mathbb{O}(n))
and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
is defined by

\displaystyle\Lambda\coloneqq\begin{cases}\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma^{1/2}\\
0_{(n-d)\times d}\end{bmatrix}&\text{if }n\geq d,\\
\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma^{1/2}&0_{n\times(d-n)}\end{bmatrix}&\text{if }n<d.\end{cases}

We assume the following on
\Sigma
and
T
.

(A15)

n/d\to\delta\in(0,\infty)
as
n\to\infty
.

(A16)

\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{2}
,
\left\|\Sigma^{-1}\right\|_{2}
, and
\left\|T\right\|_{2}
are uniformly bounded over
n
.

(A17)

As
n\to\infty
, the empirical spectral distributions
\mu_{T}
and
\mu_{\Sigma}
of
T
and
\Sigma
converge respectively to
\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}
and
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}
, with
\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}},\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}\neq\delta_{0}
.

(A18)

For each fixed
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\spec\Sigma

\displaystyle\subset\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon),

\displaystyle\spec T

\displaystyle\subset\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)

for sufficiently large
n
, where
\spec(\cdot)
denotes the set of eigenvalues.

(A19)

The measures
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}
and
\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}
do not decay too fast around the edges in the following sense:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{z\nearrow\inf\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{x-z}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}(x)&=\infty=\lim_{z\searrow\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{z-x}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}(x),\\
\lim_{z\searrow\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{z-x}\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}(x)&=\infty.\end{split}

(A20)

\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}>0
.

We comment on the above assumptions.

Item

(A15)
is a restatement of
Item

(A4)
.
Note that in the setting of
Section

3.2
,
\Sigma^{1/2}
corresponds to
\diag(\lambda)
where
\lambda
is defined in
Item

(A2)
.
Therefore, it can be checked that all other assumptions have been covered in
Items

(A2)
and
(A5)
.
In particular, the uniform boundedness of
\left\|T\right\|_{2}
in
Item

(A16)
follows since
{\mathcal{T}}(y)=1-\frac{\gamma}{\overline{g}(y)+\gamma}
is bounded from above due to positivity of both
\gamma
and
\overline{g}(y)+\gamma
given by
Item

(A5)
.
Also,
\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})
is prevented from being constantly
0
by
Equation

4.3
, implying
\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}\neq\delta_{0}
in
Item

(A16)
.
Finally, since
\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})
is larger than
-\gamma
, has mean
0
and cannot be constantly
0
, the random variable
{\mathsf{T}}=\frac{\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})}{\overline{g}({\mathsf{Y}})+\gamma}
assigns positive mass to
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, therefore
Item

(A20)
holds.

D.2
Convergence of bulk edge

Consider the following variant of
\widetilde{D}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{D}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\widetilde{T}\widetilde{O}\widetilde{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},

(D.3)

where now
\widetilde{O}
is Haar distributed on
{\mathbb{O}}(n-1)
independent of
\widetilde{T}
and
\widetilde{\Lambda}
,
\widetilde{T}
is a random minor of
T
, and
\widetilde{\Lambda}
satisfies

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}=\begin{cases}\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{1/2}&0_{d-1}\\
0_{(n-d)\times(d-1)}&0\end{bmatrix}&\text{if }n\geq d,\\
\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{1/2}&0_{(n-1)\times(d-n+1)}\end{bmatrix}&\text{if }n<d,\end{cases}

for a random minor
\widetilde{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
with size
n\wedge d-1
.

Before proceeding, we verify that
\widecheck{D}
in
Equation

D.1
is a special case of
\widehat{D}
in
Equation

D.3
.
Consequently, all results concerning
\widehat{D}
apply to
\widecheck{D}
.
Indeed, the spectrum of
\widecheck{D}
is the same as that of

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\top}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}T\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\widetilde{O}\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda.

(D.4)

Identifying

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{z^{\perp}}^{\top}T\Pi_{z^{\perp}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(n-1)\times(n-1)},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{w^{\perp}}^{\top}\Lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(n-1)\times d},

we see that the matrix
Equation

D.4
is a special case of
\widehat{D}
.

For the matrix
\widehat{D}
, our goal is to show the following.

Lemma D.2
.

It holds almost surely as
n\to\infty
that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\widehat{D}),\lambda_{3}(\widehat{D})\to\sup\supp(\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}\boxtimes\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}),

where

\displaystyle\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\delta}\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}+\frac{1-\delta}{\delta}\delta_{0}&\text{if }\delta>1,\\
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(D.5)

Note that
{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}
in
Equation

D.5
coincides with
{\mathsf{\Lambda}}_{n}^{2}
in
Equation

3.8
.
We prove
Lemma

D.2
at the end of this section.

D.2.1
Strong asymptotic freeness of Haar orthogonal and deterministic matrices

The first ingredient of the proof of
Lemma

D.2
is the strong asymptotic freeness of Haar orthogonal and deterministic matrices, that is of independent interest.

Proposition D.3
.

Let
U_{1},\ldots,U_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
be a collection of independent random matrices Haar-distributed on
\mathbb{SO}(N)
and
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
be deterministic matrices. Suppose that
\max_{j=1,\ldots,q}\left\|Y_{j}\right\|_{2}\leq C
for a constant
C>0
. Then, for any self-adjoint
*
-polynomial
Q
in
(p+q)
variables and any constant
\varepsilon>0
, for sufficiently large
N
we have

\displaystyle\spec(Q(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{p}))\subset\spec(Q(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{p}))+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon),

where the binary operation on the right-hand side is Minkowski sum and
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p})
is a collection of
*
-free Haar unitary elements
1
1

1

A Haar unitary element is a unitary operator
u
in a tracial von Neumann algebra
({\mathcal{M}},\langle\cdot\rangle)
whose spectral distribution is uniform on
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
.
that are
*
-free of
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})
.

Note that
Proposition

D.3
applies directly to our model
\widehat{D}
, since we may take
\widetilde{O}
to be Haar-distributed on
\mathbb{SO}(n-1)
, instead of
\mathbb{O}(n-1)
, without changing the law of
\widehat{D}
.

On a high level, our proof follows
[
32
, Section 3]
; we aim at expressing
U_{i}
’s as a functional calculus of some other matrix for which the strong asymptotic freeness is known. We start the proof with the strong asymptotic freeness of GOEs
2
2

2

The Gaussian orthogonal ensemble
\GOE(d)
is the probability measure on
d\times d
real symmetric matrices
G
such that
G_{i,i}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,2/d)
and
G_{i,j}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/d)
for all
1\leq i<j\leq d
.
and deterministic matrices.

Lemma D.4

(
[
45
, Theorem 4.2]
)
.

Proposition

D.3
holds if we replace
(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p})
with independent GOEs
(W_{1},\ldots,W_{p})
and
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p})
with free semi-circular elements
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{p})
.

In the next input, we represent a Haar orthogonal matrix in a canonical form
U\Theta U^{\top}
with a Haar orthogonal
U
, so that one can express it as a functional calculus of an orthogonally invariant matrix. In what follows we write

\displaystyle R(\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2},\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{R}.

Lemma D.5

(
[
68
, Theorem 3.5]
)
.

Let
\Theta\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
be a random block diagonal matrix distributed as

\displaystyle\Theta=\begin{cases}\diag(R(\theta_{1}),\ldots,R(\theta_{m}))&N=2m,\\
\diag(R(\theta_{1}),\ldots,R(\theta_{m}),1)&N=2m+1,\end{cases}

where
0\leq\theta_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\theta_{m}\leq\pi
is sampled from the joint density (with suitable choices of
Z_{N}
and
Z_{N}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{N}}\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq m}(\cos\theta_{j}-\cos\theta_{k})^{2},\qquad

\displaystyle\text{if }N=2m,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{N}^{\prime}}\prod_{1\leq j\leq m}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\theta_{j}}{2}\right)\prod_{1\leq k<\ell\leq m}(\cos\theta_{j}-\cos\theta_{k})^{2},\qquad

\displaystyle\text{if }N=2m+1.

Let
V
be a Haar orthogonal matrix independent of
\Theta
. Then the matrix
V\Theta V^{\top}
is Haar distributed on
\mathbb{SO}(N)
.

As one can see, unlike the usual diagonalization, we find that the matrix
\Theta
is a block diagonal matrix consisting of
(2\times 2)
blocks. Indeed, the true eigenvectors of a Haar orthogonal matrix
U
should be genuinely complex with the complex-conjugate symmetry. This makes it difficult to express
U
as a functional calculus of GOE, whose eigenvectors are real.

Instead, we shall express
U
as a functional calculus of a
skew
-symmetric matrix; skew-symmetric matrices already have the same canonical form consisting of
(2\times 2)
blocks, the only difference being that the blocks are of the form
xJ
for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
instead of
R(\theta)
where

\displaystyle J=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}.

Note that the spectrum of a skew-symmetric matrix is purely imaginary and the eigenvalues of
J
are
-\mathrm{i},\mathrm{i}
.
More fundamentally, it is natural to work with skew-symmetric matrices as the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(2m)
associated with the group
\mathbb{SO}(2m)
is exactly the space of skew-symmetric matrices. In the complex case, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{u}(n)
associated to
\mathbb{U}(n)
is the space of skew-Hermitian matrices, which is nothing but Hermitian matrices rotated by a factor of
\mathrm{i}
.

Thus we work with the simplest orthogonally invariant skew-symmetric matrix, which can be written as
X-X^{\top}
where
X\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
has independent
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/N)
entry, i.e. Ginibre ensemble. To this end, we next show that we can take
W_{i}
’s in
Lemma

D.4
to be real Ginibre ensembles:

Lemma D.6
.

Proposition

D.3
holds if we replace
(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p})
with independent real Ginibre ensembles
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
, and
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p})
with
*
-free circular elements
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})
.

Proof.

In what follows, we use

\displaystyle A\otimes B

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}B_{1,1}A&\cdots&B_{1,q}A\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
B_{p,1}A&\cdots&B_{p,q}A\end{bmatrix}

to denote the Kronecker product of an
m\times n
matrix
A
and a
p\times q
matrix
B
.
Let
W_{1},\ldots,W_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N\times 2N}
be independent GOEs, and take projection matrices

\displaystyle P_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{N}&0_{N\times N}\end{bmatrix},\qquad P_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}0_{N\times N}&I_{N}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\Pi_{k\ell}=P_{k}^{\top}P_{\ell}=I_{N}\otimes E_{k\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N\times 2N},

where
E_{k\ell}=e_{k}e_{\ell}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2}
for
k,\ell\in\{1,2\}
.
Then we find that the collection

\displaystyle X_{i}=\sqrt{2}P_{1}W_{i}P_{2}^{\top}

consists of independent Ginibre ensembles. Now we find that

\displaystyle Q(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})\otimes I_{2}

\displaystyle=Q(X_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,X_{p}\otimes I_{2},Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,Y_{q}\otimes I_{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Q}(W_{1},\ldots,W_{p},\Pi_{11},\Pi_{12},\Pi_{21},\Pi_{22},Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,Y_{q}\otimes I_{2})

for another
*
-polynomial
\widetilde{Q}
that is obtained from
Q
via

\displaystyle X_{i}\otimes I_{2}=\sqrt{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2}P_{k}^{\top}P_{1}W_{i}P_{2}^{\top}P_{k}=\sqrt{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2}\Pi_{k1}W_{i}\Pi_{2k}.

Now an application of
Lemma

D.4
gives, in the limit
N\to\infty
, that

\displaystyle\spec(Q(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}))

\displaystyle=\spec(Q(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})\otimes I_{2})

(D.6)

\displaystyle\subset\spec(\widetilde{Q}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{p},\Pi_{11},\Pi_{12},\Pi_{21},\Pi_{22},Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,Y_{q}\otimes I_{2}))+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\spec(Q(\widehat{x}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x}_{p},Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,Y_{q}\otimes I_{2})+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon),

where
(s_{i})_{1\leq i\leq p}
are free semi-circular elements that are
*
-free of
\{Y_{j}\otimes I_{2},\Pi_{k\ell}\}
, both realized in the free product von Neumann algebra
{\mathcal{M}}*\mathbb{C}^{2N\times 2N}
, and in the last line we abbreviated

\displaystyle\widehat{x}_{i}\coloneqq\sqrt{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2}\Pi_{k1}s_{i}\Pi_{2k}.

We next show the
*
-distributional identity

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{x}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x}_{p},Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,Y_{q}\otimes I_{2}\right)\overset{d}{=}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}),

(D.7)

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{p}
is a collection of circular elements that are
*
-free within themselves and from
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}
. We achieve this via the so-called
meta-argument
. By the usual asymptotic freeness of GOEs, for any
*
-polynomial
P
, we have

\displaystyle\langle P\left(\widehat{x}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x}_{p},Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,Y_{q}\otimes I_{2}\right)\rangle=\lim_{M\to\infty}\left\langle P\left(\widehat{X}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{X}_{p},I_{M}\otimes Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,I_{M}\otimes Y_{q}\otimes I_{2}\right)\right\rangle,

where
\left\{\widehat{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{(2NM\times 2NM)}:1\leq i\leq p\right\}
are given by

\displaystyle\widehat{X}_{i}=\sqrt{2}\sum_{k=1}^{2}(I_{M}\otimes\Pi_{k1})\widetilde{W}_{i}(I_{M}\otimes\Pi_{2k})

with a collection
(\widetilde{W}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq p}
of GOEs of size
(2NM\times 2NM)
. Next notice that each
\widehat{X}_{i}
can be written as

\displaystyle\widehat{X}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{X}_{i}&0\\
0&\widetilde{X}_{i}\end{bmatrix}=\widetilde{X}_{i}\otimes I_{2},

where
(\widetilde{X}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq p}
is a collection of independent real Ginibre ensemble of size
(NM\times NM)
.
Thus

\displaystyle\left\langle P\left(\widehat{X}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{X}_{p},I_{M}\otimes Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,I_{M}\otimes Y_{q}\otimes I_{2}\right)\right\rangle=\left\langle P\left(\widetilde{X}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{X}_{p},I_{M}\otimes Y_{1},\ldots,I_{M}\otimes Y_{q}\right)\right\rangle,

and taking the limit as
M\to\infty
gives

\displaystyle\langle P\left(\widehat{x}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x}_{p},Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,Y_{q}\otimes I_{2}\right)\rangle=\langle P\left(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}\right)\rangle.

Since
P
was generic, this shows
Equation

D.7
so that

\displaystyle\spec(Q(\widehat{x}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x}_{p},Y_{1}\otimes I_{2},\ldots,Y_{q}\otimes I_{2}))=\spec(Q(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})).

Plugging this in
Equation

D.6
proves the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

D.3
.

We aim at approximately coupling a Haar orthogonal matrix of order
N
with

\displaystyle F((X-X^{\top})/\sqrt{2})

for some function
F
applied according to functional calculus, where
X\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is a real Ginibre ensemble. Indeed, since
X-X^{\top}
is invariant under orthogonal conjugation and skew-symmetric, we can write its canonical form as in
Lemma

D.5
as

\displaystyle\frac{X-X^{\top}}{\sqrt{2}}=V\widetilde{\Theta}V^{\top},\qquad\widetilde{\Theta}=\begin{cases}\diag(\lambda_{1}J,\ldots,\lambda_{m}J),&N=2m,\\
\diag(\lambda_{1}J,\ldots,\lambda_{m}J,0),&N=2m+1,\end{cases}\qquad J=\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},

(D.8)

where
V
is a Haar orthogonal matrix independent of
\widetilde{\Theta}
and

-\lambda_{m}\leq\cdots\leq-\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{m}

are the ordered eigenvalues of
(X-X^{\top})/(\sqrt{2}\mathrm{i})
, with an extra nullity when
N
is odd (recall that skew-symmetric matrices of odd order are singular). By the usual functional calculus we get

\displaystyle F((X-X^{\top})/\sqrt{2})=VF(\widetilde{\Theta})V^{\top},

and taking
F(\cdot)=\exp(f(\cdot))
for an odd function
f:\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\to\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
, we get

\displaystyle F(\widetilde{\Theta})=\begin{cases}\diag(F(\lambda_{1}J),\ldots,F(\lambda_{m}J)),&N=2m,\\
\diag(F(\lambda_{1}J),\ldots,F(\lambda_{m}J),\exp(f(0))),&N=2m+1,\end{cases}

and by spectral theorem we can compute each block as

\displaystyle F(\lambda_{i}J)

\displaystyle=F\left(\widetilde{U}\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}&0\\
0&-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}\end{bmatrix}\widetilde{U}^{*}\right)=\exp\left(\widetilde{U}\begin{bmatrix}f(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i})&0\\
0&-f(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i})\end{bmatrix}\widetilde{U}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp(-\mathrm{i}f(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i})J)=\begin{bmatrix}\cos(\mathrm{i}f(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}))&\sin(\mathrm{i}f(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}))\\
-\sin(\mathrm{i}f(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}))&\cos(\mathrm{i}f(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i}))\end{bmatrix}=R(-\mathrm{i}f(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i})),

with a suitable unitary matrix
\widetilde{U}\in\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
diagonalizing
J
.

Next, we choose a suitable odd function
f
so that for independently sampled
\widetilde{\Theta}
and
\Theta
(recall that
(\lambda_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
and
(\theta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
are in increasing order)

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\left\|F(\lambda_{i}J)-R(\theta_{i})\right\|\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq m}|-\mathrm{i}f(\mathrm{i}\lambda_{i})-\theta_{i}|=o(1),

which would then give a coupling between
F((X-X^{\top})/\sqrt{2})
and a
\mathbb{SO}(N)
-Haar matrix
U
as

\displaystyle\left\|F((X-X^{\top})/\sqrt{2})-U\right\|=\left\|VF(\widetilde{\Theta})V^{\top}-V\Theta V^{\top}\right\|=\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\left\|F(\lambda_{i}J)-R(\theta_{i})\right\|=o(1),

where
V
,
\widetilde{\Theta}
, and
\Theta
are all independently sampled.

In order to construct the appropriate function
f
, take
F_{\mathrm{qc}}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
to be the odd extension of the c.d.f. of the quarter-circle distribution, i.e.,

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{qc}}(x)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{x}\mathbbm{1}_{[-2,2]}(t)\frac{\sqrt{4-t^{2}}}{\pi}\mathrm{d}t.

Now since
\mathrm{i}(X-X^{\top})/\sqrt{2}
is a complex Wigner matrix, the empirical distribution of
\lambda_{i}
’s converge to the quarter circle distribution on
[0,2]
, which gives the uniform convergence of c.d.f.s on
\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[0,2]}\left|F_{\mathrm{qc}}(x)-\frac{|\{i\in[m]:\lambda_{i}\leq x\}|}{\lceil N/2\rceil}\right|\to 0,\qquad\text{almost surely}.

In particular, restricting to
x=\lambda_{i}
yields

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq N/2}\left|F_{\mathrm{qc}}(\lambda_{i})-\frac{i+\mathbbm{1}(N\text{ is odd})}{\lceil N/2\rceil}\right|\to 0,\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Likewise, taking
\mu_{\theta}
to be the empirical c.d.f. of
\theta_{i}
’s (on
[0,\pi]
) from
Lemma

D.5
, we have that
\mu_{\theta}
converges to the uniform distribution on
[0,\pi]
(see e.g.
[
68
, Theorem 4.13]
), so

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq N/2}\left|\mu_{\theta}(\theta_{i})-\frac{\theta_{i}}{\pi}\right|=\max_{1\leq i\leq N/2}\left|\frac{i+\mathbbm{1}(N\text{ is odd})}{\lceil N/2\rceil}-\frac{\theta_{i}}{\pi}\right|\to 0.

Therefore we find that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq N/2}\left|\pi F_{\mathrm{qc}}(\lambda_{i})-\theta_{i}\right|\to 0\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Thus we choose the odd function
f
as

\displaystyle f(\mathrm{i}x)=\mathrm{i}\pi F_{\mathrm{qc}}(x),

which then guarantees, for the function
F=\mathrm{e}^{f}
,

\displaystyle\left\|VF(\widetilde{\Theta})V^{\top}-V\Theta V^{\top}\right\|\to 0,\qquad\text{almost surely}.

Using the complex version of the Stone–Weierstrass theorem, we next take a sequence of polynomials
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
so that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in[-4,4]}|P_{n}(x)-\exp(\mathrm{i}\pi F_{\mathrm{qc}}(x))|=0.

Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|Q(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})-Q(F((X_{1}-X_{1}^{\top})/\sqrt{2}),\ldots,F((X_{p}-X_{p}^{\top})/\sqrt{2}),Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|Q(V_{1}\Theta_{1}V_{1}^{\top},\ldots,V_{p}\Theta_{p}V_{p}^{\top},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})-Q(V_{1}F(\widetilde{\Theta}_{1})V_{1}^{\top},\ldots,V_{p}F(\widetilde{\Theta}_{p})V_{p}^{\top},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\left\|\Theta_{i}-F(\widetilde{\Theta}_{i})\right\|\to 0,\qquad\text{almost surely},

(D.9)

where the implicit constant depends only on
Q
and the upper bound
C
for
\left\|Y_{j}\right\|
’s,
(V_{i},\Theta_{i},\widetilde{\Theta}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq p}
are all independent,
V_{i}
’s are Haar distributed on
\mathbb{O}(N)
,
\Theta_{i}
’s are distributed as in
Lemma

D.5
, and
\widetilde{\Theta}_{i}
’s are distributed as
Equation

D.8
.
Now we use the polynomial
P_{n}
to get

\displaystyle\left\|Q(V_{1}F(\widetilde{\Theta}_{1})V_{1}^{\top},\ldots,V_{p}F(\widetilde{\Theta}_{p})V_{p}^{\top},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})-Q(V_{1}P_{n}(-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Theta}_{1})V_{1}^{\top},\ldots,V_{p}P_{n}(-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Theta}_{p})V_{p}^{\top},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})\right\|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\lesssim\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{R}\\
|x|\leq\max\limits_{j=1,\ldots,p}\left\|\widetilde{\Theta}_{j}\right\|\end{subarray}}|P_{n}(x)-F(\mathrm{i}x)|.

(D.10)

The last quantity converges to
0
in the limit
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}
in this order, since
\left\|\widetilde{\Theta}_{j}\right\|\leq 2\left\|X_{j}\right\|
is bounded by
4
almost surely as
N\to\infty
(since
X^{\top}X
is a Wishart ensemble) and

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 4}|P_{n}(x)-F(\mathrm{i}x)|=\sup_{|x|\leq 4}|P_{n}(x)-\exp(\mathrm{i}\pi F_{\mathrm{qc}}(x))|\to 0\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

We thus conclude that, in the limit
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle\spec(Q(U_{1},\ldots,U_{p},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}))

\displaystyle\subset\spec(Q(V_{1}P_{n}(-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Theta}_{1})V_{1}^{\top},\ldots,V_{p}P_{n}(-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\Theta}_{p})V_{p}^{\top},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}))+(-\varepsilon/3,\varepsilon/3)

\displaystyle=\spec(Q(P_{n}(-\mathrm{i}(X_{1}-X_{1}^{\top})/\sqrt{2}),\ldots,P_{n}(-\mathrm{i}(X_{p}-X_{p}^{\top})/\sqrt{2}),Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}))+(-\varepsilon/3,\varepsilon/3)

\displaystyle\subset\spec(Q(P_{n}(-\mathrm{i}(x_{1}-x_{1}^{*})/\sqrt{2}),\ldots,P_{n}(-\mathrm{i}(x_{p}-x_{p}^{*})/\sqrt{2}),Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}))+(-2\varepsilon/3,2\varepsilon/3)

\displaystyle\subset\spec(Q(F((x_{1}-x_{1}^{*})/\sqrt{2}),\ldots,F((x_{p}-x_{p}^{*})/\sqrt{2}),Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q}))+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon),

where the second line follows from
Equations

D.9
and
D.10
, the fourth line from
Lemma

D.6
, and the last line again from
Equation

D.10
.
We conclude using that
(F((x_{i}-x_{i}^{*})/\sqrt{2}))_{1\leq i\leq p}
are
*
-free Haar unitary elements: Their
*
-freeness follows from those of
x_{i}
’s, and each of them is Haar unitary since
\mathrm{i}(x_{i}-x_{i}^{*})/\sqrt{2}=\sqrt{2}\re[\mathrm{i}x_{i}]
is a semi-circular element, whose push forward under
x\mapsto F(-\mathrm{i}x)
has the uniform spectral distribution on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
∎

D.2.2
Insensitivity to
\delta

The following result is our second ingredient, which we use to deal with the discrepancy between
\Lambda
and
\widetilde{\Lambda}
when
\delta=1
.

Lemma D.7
.

Let
\mu
and
\nu
be compactly supported Borel probability measures on
(0,\infty)
and
\mathbb{R}
, respectively. Suppose that
\sup\supp\nu>0
. Then, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\sup\supp\left(((1-\varepsilon)\mu+\varepsilon\delta_{0})\boxtimes\nu\right)\leq\sup\supp\left(\mu\boxtimes\nu\right).

Proof.

Let
{\bm{s}}
and
{\bm{t}}
be realizations of
\mu
and
\nu
in
W^{*}
-probability spaces
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, respectively. Then we easily find that the measure

(1-\varepsilon)\mu+\varepsilon\delta_{0}
is the spectral measure of
{\bm{s}}\otimes(1-{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon})
in the (von Neumann algebra) tensor product
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\otimes L^{\infty}([0,1])
, where
{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon}=\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\varepsilon]}\in L^{\infty}([0,1])
is the projection. Now we have that the free convolution

\displaystyle((1-\varepsilon)\mu+\varepsilon\delta_{0})\boxtimes\nu

is the spectral measure of
(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes(1-{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon})){\bm{t}}(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes(1-{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon}))
in the (von Neumann algebra) free product
({\mathcal{M}}_{1}\otimes L^{\infty}([0,1]))*{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
.

Then we find

\displaystyle\sup\supp\left(((1-\varepsilon)\mu+\varepsilon\delta_{0})\boxtimes\nu\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{v\in{\mathcal{H}},\left\|v\right\|=1}\langle v,(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes(1-{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon})){\bm{t}}(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes(1-{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon}))v\rangle

\displaystyle=\sup_{v\in{\mathcal{H}},\left\|v\right\|=1}\langle(1\otimes(1-{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon}))v,(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes 1){\bm{t}}(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes 1)(1\otimes(1-{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon}))v\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{w\in\mathrm{Range}(1\otimes(1-{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon})),\left\|w\right\|\leq 1}\langle w,(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes 1){\bm{t}}(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes 1)w\rangle

\displaystyle=0\vee\sup_{w\in\mathrm{Range}(1\otimes(1-{\bm{p}}_{\varepsilon})),\left\|w\right\|=1}\langle w,(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes 1){\bm{t}}(\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}\otimes 1)w\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 0\vee\sup\supp(\mu\boxtimes\nu),

where
{\mathcal{H}}
is the Gelfand–Naimark–Segal (GNS) Hilbert space of
({\mathcal{M}}_{1}\otimes L^{\infty}([0,1]))*{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
.
To conclude, we only need to notice that
\sup\supp(\mu\boxtimes\nu)\geq 0
: If not, the realization
\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}{\bm{t}}\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}
would be negative semidefinite, which contradicts
\sup\supp\nu>0
as
{\bm{s}}
is invertible since
\mu
is supported on
(0,\infty)
.
∎

D.2.3
Proof of
Lemma

D.2

Notice that, by Cauchy interlacing, the empirical eigenvalue distributions of
\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{{}^{\top}}
and
\widetilde{T}
converge respectively to
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}
and
\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}
. Hence from the usual asymptotic freeness it immediately follows that the empirical spectral distribution of
\widehat{D}
converges to
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}\boxtimes\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}
, which then implies

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{3}(\widehat{D})\geq\sup\supp(\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}\boxtimes\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}),\qquad\text{almost surely},

since the left-hand side is bounded from below by any
O(1)
-quantile of
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}\boxtimes\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}
.

For the upper bound, we first claim that
\lambda_{1}(\widehat{D})=\lambda_{1}(\sqrt{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\widetilde{T}\widetilde{O}\sqrt{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}})
. To see this, note that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\widehat{D})=\begin{cases}\lambda_{1}(\sqrt{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\widetilde{T}\widetilde{O}\sqrt{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}})&\text{if }n\geq d,\\
0\vee\lambda_{1}(\sqrt{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\widetilde{T}\widetilde{O}\sqrt{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}})=0\vee\lambda_{1}(\widetilde{\Sigma}^{1/2}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\widetilde{T}\widetilde{O}\widetilde{\Sigma}^{1/2})&\text{if }n<d,\end{cases}

and that for
n<d
the matrix
\widetilde{\Sigma}^{1/2}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\widetilde{T}\widetilde{O}^{\top}\widetilde{\Sigma}
cannot be negative semidefinite due to
Item

(A20)
. Then, since we may replace
\widetilde{O}
by a matrix Haar-distributed from
\mathbb{SO}(n-1)
, we may directly apply
Proposition

D.3
to obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(\widehat{D})=\sup\supp(\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}\boxtimes\mu_{\widetilde{T}})

almost surely as
n\to\infty
, where we used

\displaystyle\mu_{\sqrt{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}{\bm{u}}^{\top}\widetilde{T}{\bm{u}}\sqrt{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}}=\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}\boxtimes\mu_{\widetilde{T}},

for a Haar unitary element
{\bm{u}}
.

To finish the proof, one must take the limit
n\to\infty
in the matrices
\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}
and
\widetilde{T}
. Indeed, using
[
67
, Corollary 2.1 and Theorem A.1]
, we have that

\displaystyle\sup\supp\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}\boxtimes\mu_{\widetilde{T}}\to\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}\boxtimes\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}

almost surely as
n\to\infty
, provided the matrices converges ‘strongly’ i.e. for each fixed
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\spec(\widetilde{T})\subset\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon),

(D.11)

\displaystyle\spec(\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top})\subset\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}+(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon).

(D.12)

In fact,
Equation

D.11
is always guaranteed by
Item

(A18)
. On the other hand for
Equation

D.12
, notice that

\displaystyle\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}=\begin{cases}\frac{d-1}{n-1}\mu_{\widetilde{\Sigma}}+\frac{n-d}{n-1}\delta_{0}&\text{if }n\geq d,\\
\mu_{\widetilde{\Sigma}}&\text{if }n<d.\end{cases}

Hence, as long as
\delta\neq 1
, the measures
\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}
and
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}
have point masses at the origin of comparable sizes (or both have none). In this case, again
Item

(A18)
guarantees
Equation

D.12
. Therefore for
\delta\neq 1
we have proved

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\lambda_{1}(\widehat{D})=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup\supp(\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}\boxtimes\mu_{\widetilde{T}})=\sup\supp(\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}\boxtimes\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}).

However
Equation

D.12
may fail when
\delta=1
, since
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}=\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}
does not have a point mass at zero whereas
\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}
(as well as
\Lambda\Lambda^{\top}
) might have
o(1)
nullity. In order to deal with this, we use
Lemma

D.7
to get

\displaystyle\sup\supp(\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\top}}\boxtimes\mu_{\widetilde{T}})\leq\sup\supp(\mu_{\widetilde{\Sigma}}\boxtimes\mu_{\widetilde{T}}).

Now
\mu_{\widetilde{\Sigma}}
converges ‘strongly’ to
\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}=\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}
in the sense of
Equation

D.12
by Cauchy interlacing, so that

\displaystyle\sup\supp(\mu_{\widetilde{\Sigma}}\boxtimes\mu_{\widetilde{T}})\to\sup\supp(\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}_{n}}\boxtimes\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}})

almost surely as
n\to\infty
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

D.2
.

D.3
Spectral edge of free multiplicative convolution

In this section we provide a characterization of the free multiplicative convolution. Since it is of independent interest, we replace
\Sigma
and
T
with general operators
{\bm{s}}
and
{\bm{t}}
that are self-adjoint, free elements in a
W^{*}
-probability space
({\mathcal{M}},\langle\cdot\rangle)
such that
{\bm{s}}\geq 0
.
Assume the following on the spectral measures
\mu_{{\bm{s}}}
and
\mu_{{\bm{t}}}
.

(A21)

\mu_{{\bm{s}}}
and
\mu_{{\bm{t}}}
are non-degenerate, i.e., not point masses.

(A22)

The map

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)\ni z\mapsto\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s}{s-z}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{s}}}(s)=\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{{\bm{s}}-z}\right\rangle\in\mathbb{C}

extends analytically through the origin, i.e. the spectral measure
\mu_{{\bm{s}}}
is supported on a strictly positive interval modulo a possible point mass at the origin. We write
\widetilde{\mu}_{{\bm{s}}}=\mu_{{\bm{s}}}-\mu_{{\bm{s}}}(\{0\})\delta_{0}
.

(A23)

\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}}>0
.

(A24)

We have

\displaystyle\lim_{z\searrow\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{s}}}}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{{\bm{s}}-z}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\infty=\lim_{z\nearrow\inf\supp\widetilde{\mu}_{{\bm{s}}}}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{z-{\bm{s}}}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\lim_{z\searrow\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}}}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-z}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\infty.

Lemma D.8
.

1.

Define
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{{\bm{t}}}\subset\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{w\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}}:\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle=0\right\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{{\bm{t}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{w\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}}:\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle=1-\mu_{{\bm{s}}}(\{0\})\right\}.

Then
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}\cap(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}^{\prime}\cap(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)
are finite sets.

2.

For each
w\in(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)\setminus{\mathcal{S}}
, there exists a unique
\omega(w)\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(\inf\supp\widetilde{\mu}_{{\bm{s}}},\sup\supp\widetilde{\mu}_{{\bm{s}}})
such that

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle=\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{{\bm{s}}-\omega(w)}\right\rangle.

(D.13)

3.

The map
\omega:(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}\to\mathbb{R}
defined in
Item

2
extends meromorphically to an open set in
\mathbb{C}
containing
(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)
. The extension is analytic at each
w\in(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}
, has a pole at each
w\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}
, and has a zero at each
w\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{{\bm{t}}}
.

4.

Define the function
\psi:(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)\setminus{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle\psi(w)=w\omega(w)\left(1-\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{{\bm{s}}-\omega(w)}\right\rangle^{-1}\right)^{-1}\equiv w\omega(w)\left(1-\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-1}\right)^{-1}.

(D.14)

Then
\psi
extends analytically to an open set in
\mathbb{C}
containing
(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)
, and
\lim_{w\to+\infty}\psi^{\prime}(w)=\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle
.

Proof.

For proofs of
Items

1
,
3
and
2
, see
[
105
, Items 1 to 3 of Lemma E.2]
. For
Item

4
, note that we can write
\psi
as

\displaystyle\psi(w)=w\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{{\bm{s}}-\omega(w)}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{s}}-\omega(w)}\right\rangle^{-1}.

Thus
\psi
is a meromorphic function on a neighborhood of
(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)
by
Item

2
, with possible poles at
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}
. Hence we only need to check that each
w\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}
is a removable singularity for
\psi
. Recall that
|\omega(z)|\to\infty
as
z\to w\in{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{t}}}
, so that by dominated convergence

\displaystyle\psi(z)

\displaystyle=z\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}\omega(z)}{{\bm{s}}-\omega(z)}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{\omega(z)}{{\bm{s}}-\omega(z)}\right\rangle^{-1}\to\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle w,\qquad\text{as }z\to w.

(D.15)

This shows that
w
is a removable singularity of
\psi
.
For the last part on
\psi^{\prime}
, straightforward computation yields that whenever
w>\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}}
,

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}w}\left(\omega(w)\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\omega^{\prime}(w)\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle+\omega(w)\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle\right)\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-1}-\omega(w)\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-2}

\displaystyle=\left(\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{({\bm{s}}-\omega(w))^{2}}\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle+\omega(w)\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle\right)\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-1}

(D.16)

\displaystyle\qquad-\omega(w)\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-2}

\displaystyle=\left(\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{({\bm{s}}-\omega(w))^{2}}\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{{\bm{s}}-\omega(w)}\right\rangle+\omega(w)\right)\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad-\omega(w)\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-2}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{({\bm{s}}-\omega(w))^{2}}\right\rangle^{-1}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}^{2}}{({\bm{s}}-\omega(w))^{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad-\left\langle\frac{\omega(w){\bm{s}}}{{\bm{s}}-\omega(w)}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle^{-2}\left\langle\frac{1}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle,

(D.17)

where in
Equation

D.16
we use the following expression of
\omega^{\prime}(w)
derived from the defining equation
Equation

D.13
and the implicit function theorem:

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}(w)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{({\bm{t}}-w)^{2}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{({\bm{s}}-\omega(w))^{2}}\right\rangle^{-1}.

Using that
|\omega(w)|\to\infty
as
w\to+\infty
, we easily check that the first term of
Equation

D.17
vanishes and the second term converges to
\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle
in the limit.
∎

Theorem D.9
.

We have

\displaystyle\sup\supp\mu_{\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}{\bm{t}}\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}}=\psi(a^{\circ}),

where
a^{\circ}>\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}}
is the largest critical point of the analytic function
\psi:(\sup\supp\mu_{{\bm{t}}},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
defined above in
Item

4
of
Lemma

D.8
.

We prove
Theorem

D.9
at the end of this section after collecting the inputs.

D.3.1
Setup

Denote
\mathbb{C}_{+}\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\im z>0\}
and
\mathbb{C}_{-}\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\im z<0\}
.

Definition D.1
.

Let
{\bm{a}}\in{\mathcal{M}}
be a non-zero self-adjoint element. Define two analytic maps on
\mathbb{C}_{+}
as

\displaystyle K_{{\bm{a}}}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1-\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle^{-1},

\displaystyle L_{{\bm{a}}}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{K_{{\bm{a}}}(z)}{z}\equiv\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle^{-1}.

Remark D.1
.

Notice that
K_{{\bm{a}}}
is indeed well-defined, that is, the map
\langle{\bm{a}}/({\bm{a}}-z)\rangle
has no zero in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. To see this, suppose on the contrary that there is a zero
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{x-z}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)=\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle=0.

Then we have

\displaystyle\im\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{x-z}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)=\im z\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{|x-z|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)=0

and

\displaystyle\re\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{x-z}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}-x\re z}{|x-z|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)=0.

Combining the two equations gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{|x-z|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)=0,

which obviously is a contradiction.

Lemma D.10
.

Let
{\bm{a}}\in{\mathcal{M}}
be self-adjoint with a non-degenerate spectral measure.
Let
K_{{\bm{a}}}
and
L_{{\bm{a}}}
be given in
Definition

D.1
.

1.

K_{{\bm{a}}}
and
L_{{\bm{a}}}
meromorphically extend to
\mathbb{C}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\bm{a}}}
with
K_{{\bm{a}}}(\overline{z})=\overline{K_{{\bm{a}}}(z)}
and
L_{{\bm{a}}}(\overline{z})=\overline{L_{{\bm{a}}}(z)}
. The two meromorphic extensions have the same set of poles in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\bm{a}}}
, given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{{\bm{a}}}=\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\bm{a}}}:\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle=0\right\}.

(D.18)

2.

L_{{\bm{a}}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\subset\mathbb{C}_{+}
. If, in addition,
{\bm{a}}\geq 0
, then
K_{{\bm{a}}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\subset\mathbb{C}_{+}
.

Proof.

Item

1
is an immediate consequence of the definitions of
K_{{\bm{a}}}
and
L_{{\bm{a}}}
and the fact that the map

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\bm{a}}}\ni z\mapsto\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle\ni\mathbb{C}

(D.19)

is analytic. The poles of
K_{{\bm{a}}}
and
L_{{\bm{a}}}
are exactly the zeros of this function; notice that the two maps

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle

cannot have a common zero. Finally, the zeros of the function in
Equation

D.19
are all real by
Remark

D.1
, from which
Equation

D.18
follows.

For
Item

2
, to check
L_{{\bm{a}}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\subset\mathbb{C}_{+}
, we take
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
and write

\displaystyle\im L_{{\bm{a}}}(z)

\displaystyle=\im\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle^{-1}=\left|\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle\right|^{-2}\im\left[\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle\left(1+\overline{z}\overline{\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\im z\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{x-z}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)\right|^{-2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{|x-z|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)-\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{x-z}\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)\right|^{2}\right)>0,

(D.20)

where we used Cauchy-Schwarz in the last inequality. For
{\bm{a}}\geq 0
, the fact that
K_{{\bm{a}}}(\mathbb{C}_{+})\subset\mathbb{C}_{+}
follows from
observing that

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{x-z}(x\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x))

is the Stieltjes transform of the positive measure
x\mathrm{d}\mu_{{\bm{a}}}(x)
.
∎

Lemma D.11
.

The map
K_{{\bm{a}}}
is meromorphic around
\infty
with a pole at
\infty
. The pole is simple if
\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle\neq 0
and double if
\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle=0
. More precisely, there exist a constant
c>0
and an analytic function
g
on
\left\{z:|z|<c\right\}
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K_{{\bm{a}}}(1/z)}=\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle z+(\langle{\bm{a}}^{2}\rangle-\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle^{2})z^{2}+z^{3}g(z),\qquad|z|<c.

(D.21)

Consequently, there exists a constant
C>0
such that following holds with the choices

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}\coloneqq\{z:|z|>C\},\qquad{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq\{z:|z|>C,\,\,\re z>C|\im z|\}.

1.

If
\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle>0
, there exists a unique analytic inverse
K_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}
of
K_{{\bm{a}}}
on
{\mathcal{N}}
. The map
K_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}
maps
{\mathcal{N}}\cap\mathbb{C}_{+}
,
{\mathcal{N}}\cap\mathbb{C}_{-}
, and
{\mathcal{N}}\cap\mathbb{R}
into
\mathbb{C}_{+},\mathbb{C}_{-}
, and
\mathbb{R}
, respectively.

2.

If
\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle=0
, there exists a unique analytic map
h_{{\bm{a}}}
on
{\mathcal{N}}
such that

\displaystyle K_{{\bm{a}}}(h_{{\bm{a}}}(\sqrt{z}))=z=K_{{\bm{a}}}(h_{{\bm{a}}}(-\sqrt{z})),

\displaystyle\qquad z\in{\mathcal{N}},

(D.22)

\displaystyle\qquad h_{{\bm{a}}}(\sqrt{K_{{\bm{a}}}(w)})=w,

\displaystyle\qquad w\in{\mathcal{C}},

(D.23)

where we choose the branch of
\sqrt{z}
to be
(-\infty,0)
, i.e.
\sqrt{r\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}}=\sqrt{r}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta/2}
for
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi]
. The map
h_{{\bm{a}}}
maps
{\mathcal{C}}\cap\mathbb{C}_{+}
,
{\mathcal{C}}\cap\mathbb{C}_{-}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}\cap\mathbb{R}
into
\mathbb{C}_{+},\mathbb{C}_{-},
and
\mathbb{R}
, respectively. In this case we denote
K_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(z)\coloneqq h_{{\bm{a}}}(\sqrt{z})
on a suitable neighborhood of
\infty
.

Proof.

Note first that
Equation

D.21
is a direct consequence of the definition:

\displaystyle\begin{split}1/K_{{\bm{a}}}(1/z)=z\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{1-z{\bm{a}}}\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{1-z{\bm{a}}}\right\rangle^{-1}&=z\left(\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle+z\langle{\bm{a}}^{2}\rangle+O(|z|^{2})\right)\left(1+\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle z+O(|z|^{2})\right)^{-1}\\
&=\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle z+(\langle{\bm{a}}^{2}\rangle-\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle^{2})z^{2}+O(|z|^{3}).\end{split}

(D.24)

For
Item

1
, since the linear term in
Equation

D.21
is non-vanishing, we have a unique analytic inverse
f
of
1/K_{{\bm{a}}}(1/z)
around the origin. Then it is easy to check that
1/f(1/z)
serves as the inverse function of
K_{{\bm{a}}}
. The last assertion follows from the fact that
\im K_{{\bm{a}}}(z)
has the same sign as
\im z
for large enough
|z|
, which in turn follows from

\displaystyle\begin{split}\im K_{{\bm{a}}}(z)&=\im z\left|\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-z}\right\rangle\right|^{-2}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{|{\bm{a}}-z|^{2}}\right\rangle=\im z\frac{\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle|z|^{-2}+O(|z|^{-3})}{|\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle z^{-1}+O(|z|^{-2})|^{2}}=\im z\left(\frac{1}{\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle}+O(|z|^{-1})\right).\end{split}

For
Item

2
, we can find a unique analytic function
p_{{\bm{a}}}(z)
in a neighborhood of the origin so that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K_{{\bm{a}}}(1/z)}

\displaystyle=p_{{\bm{a}}}(z)^{2},

\displaystyle p_{{\bm{a}}}(0)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle p_{{\bm{a}}}^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\langle{\bm{a}}^{2}\rangle}>0.

(D.25)

By the same reasoning as above, the map
p_{{\bm{a}}}
has an inverse
p_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}
around the origin. Then we choose
h_{{\bm{a}}}(z)=1/p_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(1/z)
in a neighborhood of
\infty
. Now we have the first equality of
Equation

D.22
as

\displaystyle K_{{\bm{a}}}(h_{{\bm{a}}}(\sqrt{z}))=K_{{\bm{a}}}(1/p_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(1/\sqrt{z}))=\frac{1}{p_{{\bm{a}}}(p_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(1/\sqrt{z}))^{2}}=z.

Likewise we can prove the second equality of
Equation

D.22
.

To prove
Equation

D.23
, notice that
p_{{\bm{a}}}^{\prime}(0)>0
implies that there is a constant
c>0
such that
z\mapsto p_{{\bm{a}}}(z)^{2}
maps the cone
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{|w|<c,|\im w|<c\re w\}
into the slit-plane
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]
. On this cone we have
\sqrt{p_{{\bm{a}}}(w)^{2}}=p_{{\bm{a}}}(w)
, so that for
w^{-1}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle h_{{\bm{a}}}(\sqrt{K_{{\bm{a}}}(w)})=\frac{1}{p_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(1/\sqrt{K_{{\bm{a}}}(w)})}=\frac{1}{p_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(\sqrt{1/K_{{\bm{a}}}(w)})}=\frac{1}{p_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(\sqrt{p_{{\bm{a}}}(1/w)^{2}})}=w.

Noticing that the inverse-cone
\{w^{-1}:w\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\}
contains another a set of the form
\{w:|w|>C,\re w>C|\im w|\}
, we have proved
Equation

D.23
. The uniqueness follows from those of
p_{{\bm{a}}}
and its inverse. Finally, for the last assertion on the parity of
h_{{\bm{a}}}(z)
, we use
\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle=0
to write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\im K_{{\bm{a}}}(w)=\im\left[\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}}{{\bm{a}}-w}+\frac{{\bm{a}}}{w}\right\rangle^{-1}\right]=\im w\left|\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}^{2}}{w({\bm{a}}-w)}\right\rangle\right|^{-2}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{a}}^{2}(2\re w-{\bm{a}})}{|w({\bm{a}}-w)|^{2}}\right\rangle.\end{split}

(D.26)

Plugging in
w=K_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(z)
and taking
z
in a cone

\displaystyle\{z:|z|>C^{\prime},\re z>C^{\prime}|\im z|\},

(D.27)

we have

\displaystyle\re K_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(z)=\re h_{{\bm{a}}}(\sqrt{z})>\re[\sqrt{\langle{\bm{a}}\rangle^{2}}\sqrt{z}(1+O(|z|^{-1/2}))]>\left\|{\bm{a}}\right\|,

where we used that, by
Equation

D.25
,
h_{{\bm{a}}}
admits the expansion

\displaystyle h_{{\bm{a}}}(z)=\frac{1}{p_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(1/z)}=\left(\frac{1}{p_{{\bm{a}}}^{\prime}(0)z}+O(|z|^{-2})\right)^{-1}=\sqrt{\langle{\bm{a}}^{2}\rangle}z(1+O(|z|^{-1})).

This proves the second factor of
Equation

D.26
is positive for
w=K_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(z)
, so that
\im K_{{\bm{a}}}^{-1}(z)=\im h_{{\bm{a}}}(\sqrt{z})
has the same sign as
\im z
, hence as
\im\sqrt{z}
.
∎

D.3.2
Analytic subordination

Proposition D.12

(
[
2
, Theorem 1.3]
)
.

There is a unique pair of analytic functions
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}:\mathbb{C}_{+}\to\mathbb{C}
that satisfies the following for each
z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}(z)/z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\qquad\Omega_{2}(z)\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

\displaystyle K_{{\bm{s}}}(\Omega_{1}(z))=K_{{\bm{t}}}(\Omega_{2}(z))=\frac{\Omega_{1}(z)\Omega_{2}(z)}{z},

(D.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to+\infty}|\Omega_{1}(rz)|=\infty=\lim_{r\to+\infty}|\Omega_{2}(rz)|.

Furthermore, the three quantities in
Equation

D.28
are all equal to

\displaystyle K_{{\bm{s}}^{1/2}{\bm{t}}{\bm{s}}^{1/2}}(z).

D.3.3
Consequences of analytic subordination

In what follows, we write

\lambda^{\circ}\coloneqq\sup\supp\mu_{\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}{\bm{t}}\sqrt{{\bm{s}}}},

and denote by
\overline{\mathbb{R}}
the one-point compactification
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}\cong{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
of
\mathbb{R}
.

Corollary D.13
.

The maps
\Omega_{1}
and (resp.
\Omega_{2}
) extends meromorphically (resp. analytically) through a neighborhood of the half-line
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
. Their images of
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
satisfy
\Omega_{1}((\lambda^{\circ},\infty))\subset\overline{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}
,
\Omega_{2}((\lambda^{\circ},\infty))\subset(\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}},\infty)
. Finally, the map
\Omega_{2}
is strictly increasing in
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
.

Proof.

Applying
Item

3
of
Lemma

D.8
with
{\bm{t}}
replaced by
{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}
, we find that the map
K_{{\bm{s}}}^{-1}\circ K_{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}}:(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}
is well-defined and meromorphically extends to a neighborhood of
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
. On the other hand,
Proposition

D.12
shows that
K_{{\bm{s}}}^{-1}\circ K_{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}}
matches with
\Omega_{1}
in a open set close to
\infty
. Thus
K_{{\bm{s}}}^{-1}\circ K_{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}}
provides a meromorphic extension of
\Omega_{1}
to a neighborhood of
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
. Then we extend
\Omega_{2}
to the same neighborhood via
Equation

D.28
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}(z)=\frac{zK_{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}}(z)}{\Omega_{1}(z)}.

It also immediately follows that
\Omega_{1}((\lambda^{\circ},\infty))\subset\overline{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}
, and that
\Omega_{2}((\lambda^{\circ},\infty))\subset\overline{\mathbb{R}}
.

We next show that
\Omega_{2}
has no poles in
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
. Assume the contrary, so that there is a pole
x>\lambda^{\circ}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to x,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}|L_{{\bm{s}}}(\Omega_{1}(z))|=\lim_{z\to x,z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}\frac{|\Omega_{2}(z)|}{|z|}=\infty,

which is a contradiction since
\Omega_{1}(x)\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}
. Thus
\Omega_{2}
has no poles in
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
hence analytically continues through the line
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
.

Next, we claim that there is a finite Borel measure
\widetilde{\mu}
so that

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}(z)=\frac{z}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle}-\frac{\langle{\bm{s}}^{2}\rangle-\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2}}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2}}\langle{\bm{t}}\rangle+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{x-z}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\mu}(x),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}_{+},

(D.29)

which is in turn implied by, due to Nevalinna-Pick representation,

\displaystyle\limsup_{y\to\infty}y\left|\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y)-\frac{1}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle}\mathrm{i}y+\frac{\langle{\bm{s}}^{2}\rangle-\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2}}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2}}\langle{\bm{t}}\rangle\right|<\infty.

(D.30)

To see
Equation

D.30
, for large enough
y
so that
\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y)=K_{{\bm{s}}}^{-1}(K_{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}}(\mathrm{i}y))
and
\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y)=K_{{\bm{t}}}^{-1}(K_{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}}(\mathrm{i}y))
, we notice that

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y)-\frac{1}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle}\mathrm{i}y=\mathrm{i}y\left(L_{{\bm{s}}}(\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y))-\frac{1}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle}\right).

(D.31)

We then use
Equation

D.21
to get

L_{{\bm{s}}}(\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y))-\frac{1}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle}=\frac{1}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle+(\langle{\bm{s}}^{2}\rangle-\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2})\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y)^{-1}+O(|\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y)|^{-2})}-\frac{1}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle}=-\frac{\langle{\bm{s}}^{2}\rangle-\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2}}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2}\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y)}+O(|\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y)|^{-2}),

so that

\displaystyle y\left|\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y)-\frac{1}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle}\mathrm{i}y+\frac{\langle{\bm{s}}^{2}\rangle-\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2}}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2}}\langle{\bm{t}}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{y(\langle{\bm{s}}^{2}\rangle-\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2})}{\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle^{2}}\left|-\frac{\mathrm{i}y}{\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y)}+\langle{\bm{t}}\rangle\right|+O\left(\frac{y^{2}}{|\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y)|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim y\left|-\frac{1}{L_{{\bm{t}}}(\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y))}+\langle{\bm{t}}\rangle\right|+|L_{{\bm{t}}}(\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y))|^{-2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{y}{|\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y)|}+|L_{{\bm{t}}}(\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y))|^{-2}

\displaystyle=|L_{{\bm{s}}}(\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y)|^{-1}+|L_{{\bm{t}}}(\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y))|^{-2},

where the third line follows from
Equation

D.21
applied to
{\bm{t}}
in place of
{\bm{a}}
.
This proves
Equation

D.30
as

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to\infty}L_{{\bm{s}}}(\Omega_{1}(\mathrm{i}y))^{-1}=\lim_{|\omega|\to\infty}L_{{\bm{s}}}(\omega)^{-1}=\langle{\bm{s}}\rangle,\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{y\to\infty}L_{{\bm{t}}}(\Omega_{2}(\mathrm{i}y))^{-1}=\lim_{|\omega_{2}|\to\infty}L_{{\bm{t}}}(\omega_{2})^{-1}=\langle{\bm{t}}\rangle.

Applying Stieltjes inversion to
Equation

D.29
, since
\Omega_{2}
extends analytically through
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
and is real-valued therein, we find that
\supp\widetilde{\mu}\subset(-\infty,\lambda^{\circ}]
. Then it also follows that
\Omega_{2}
is strictly increasing in
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
. If
\Omega_{2}(x)=\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}
for some
x>\lambda^{\circ}
, we would have

\displaystyle-\infty=\lim_{w\searrow\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-w}\right\rangle=\lim_{z\searrow x}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-\Omega_{2}(z)}\right\rangle=\lim_{z\searrow x}\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}}{{\bm{s}}-\Omega_{1}(z)}\right\rangle,

which in turn implies
\lim_{z\searrow x}\Omega_{1}(z)=\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}
. But then we have a contradiction as

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\searrow x}K_{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}}(z)=\lim_{z\searrow x}\frac{\Omega_{1}(z)\Omega_{2}(z)}{z}=\frac{(\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}})(\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}})}{x}.\qed

D.3.4
Proof of
Theorem

D.9

Theorem

D.9
follows from the following lemma.

Lemma D.14
.

Let
w>\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}
. Recall the map
\psi
defined in
Item

4
of
Lemma

D.8
.

1.

If
\psi(\widetilde{w})>\lambda^{\circ}
for all
\widetilde{w}\geq w
, then
\psi^{\prime}(w)>0
.

2.

If
\psi^{\prime}(\widetilde{w})>0
for all
\widetilde{w}\geq w
, then
\psi(\widetilde{w})\geq\lambda^{\circ}
.

We omit the proof of
Theorem

D.9
given
Lemma

D.14
since it is exactly the same as
[
105
, proof of Lemma 5.5 given Lemma E.1]
.

Proof of
Lemma

D.14
.

Recall from
Lemma

D.8
the triplet
(\psi(\widetilde{w}),\omega(\widetilde{w}),\widetilde{w})
of meromorphic functions in
\widetilde{w}\in(\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}},\infty)
. It also follows from the definitions of
\psi
and
\omega
that the triplet satisfies the same subordination equation as in
Equation

D.28
, i.e.

\displaystyle K_{{\bm{s}}}(\omega(\widetilde{w}))=K_{{\bm{t}}}(\widetilde{w})=\frac{\widetilde{w}\omega(\widetilde{w})}{\psi(\widetilde{w})},\qquad\widetilde{w}>\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}}.

(D.32)

Recall from the proof of
Lemma

D.8
that
\psi^{\prime}(\widetilde{w})
is positive for large enough
\widetilde{w}>0
. We next claim that the same holds for
(\omega/\psi)^{\prime}
. Note first that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\omega(\widetilde{w})}{\psi(\widetilde{w})}=\frac{\omega(\widetilde{w})}{\widetilde{w}}L_{{\bm{s}}}(\omega(\widetilde{w}))=\frac{1}{\widetilde{w}}K_{{\bm{s}}}(\omega(\widetilde{w}))=\frac{1}{\widetilde{w}}K_{{\bm{t}}}(\widetilde{w})=L_{{\bm{t}}}(\widetilde{w}),\end{split}

where we used the definition of
\omega
in the last equality. Then we can compute the derivative easily using
Equation

D.20
via Nevanlinna derivative:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\widetilde{w}}L_{{\bm{t}}}(\widetilde{w})=\lim_{\eta\searrow 0}\frac{\im L_{{\bm{t}}}(\widetilde{w}+\mathrm{i}\eta)}{\eta}=\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-\widetilde{w}}\right\rangle^{-2}\left(\left\langle\frac{1}{({\bm{t}}-\widetilde{w})^{2}}\right\rangle-\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{t}}-\widetilde{w}}\right\rangle^{2}\right)>0.\end{split}

Now by taking large
\widetilde{w}>0
at which derivatives of
\psi
and
\omega/\psi
are both positive, we find that there is a small, open sector in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
based at
\widetilde{w}
on which all of
(\psi(\widetilde{w}),\omega(\widetilde{w})/\psi(\widetilde{w}),\widetilde{w})
are in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
. By analytic continuation the subordination equations
Equation

D.32
hold on the sector, so that by the uniqueness in
Proposition

D.12
we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{2}(\psi(\widetilde{w}))

\displaystyle=\widetilde{w},

(D.33)

\displaystyle\Omega_{1}(\psi(\widetilde{w}))

\displaystyle=\omega(\widetilde{w}),

on a neighborhood of
(\sup\supp\mu_{{\mathsf{T}}},\infty)
.

We start with the proof of
Item

1
. Taking the derivative of
Equation

D.33
and noticing from
Corollary

D.13
that
\Omega_{2}
is strictly increasing in
(\lambda^{\circ},\infty)
proves that
\psi^{\prime}(\widetilde{w})>0
for all
\widetilde{w}\geq w
.

For
Item

2
, we consider the analytic inverse function
\psi^{-1}:[\psi(w),\infty)\to(w,\infty)
. By
Equation

D.33
this inverse function must match with
\Omega_{2}
, so that
\Omega_{2}
extends analytically to a neighborhood of
[\psi(w),\infty)
and is real-valued on the interval. If we assume on the contrary that
\lambda^{\circ}>\psi(w)
, then for
x\in(\psi(w),\lambda^{\circ})\setminus\{0\}
we would have

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\searrow 0}\im\left\langle\frac{1}{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{x}\lim_{\eta\searrow 0}\im\left\langle\frac{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}-x-\mathrm{i}\eta}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{x}\lim_{\eta\searrow 0}\im\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-\Omega_{2}(x+\mathrm{i}\eta)}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{x}\im\left\langle\frac{{\bm{t}}}{{\bm{t}}-\Omega_{2}(x)}\right\rangle=0,

where the last two equalities follow from
Corollary

D.13
. By Stieltjes inversion this would imply that
(\psi(w),\lambda^{\circ})\setminus\{0\}
does not intersect with
\supp\mu_{{\bm{s}}{\bm{t}}}
, contradicting the definition
